The canonical wall structure and intrinsic mirror symmetry Thanks: M.G. was supported by the ERC grant MSAG, EPSRC grant EP/N03189X/1 and a Royal Society Wolfson Research Merit Award. Research by B.S. was partially supported by NSF grant DMS-1903437. Thanks: AMS MSC: 14J33, 14N35, 14A21
Mark Gross, Bernd Siebert

Abstract
. As announced in [ GS6 ] in 2016, we construct and prove consistency of the canonical wall structure . This construction starts with a log Calabi-Yau pair ( X , D ) (X,D) and produces a wall structure, as defined in [ GHS ] . Roughly put, the canonical wall structure is a data structure which encodes an algebro-geometric analogue of counts of Maslov index zero disks. These enumerative invariants are defined in terms of the punctured invariants of [ ACGS2 ] . There are then two main theorems of the paper. First, we prove consistency of the canonical wall structure, so that, using the setup of [ GHS ] , the canonical wall structure gives rise to a mirror family. Second, we prove that this mirror family coincides with the intrinsic mirror constructed in [ GS7 ] . While the setup of this paper is narrower than that of [ GS7 ] , it gives a more detailed description of the mirror.



Introduction

The mathematical investigation of the mirror phenomenon observed in physics

[
GP
,
CLS
,
COGP
]
has long been vexed by the basic question how broadly
mirror pairs exist. Mirror symmetry is certainly too wide a phenomenon to even
imagine an answer that is immediately satisfying from all perspectives. The
authors have nevertheless long been convinced that there is a fundamental
mechanism underlying all mirror phenomena that is both key to general
constructions of mirror pairs and to establish various mirror correspondences
between them.

Intrinsic mirror symmetry

Following a long line of developments and refinements around the SYZ philosophy
of dual torus fibrations
[
SYZ
,
Gr1
,
KS1
,
Fu
,
GS1
,
KS2
,
GS3
,
Au
,
GHK
,
Tu
,
Ab1
,
Ab2
]
, the authors in
[
GS7
]
proposed an
intrinsic mirror construction for logarithmic Calabi-Yau varieties.

The construction takes as input a pair
(X,D)
of a normal crossings divisor
D

on a smooth variety
X
, projective over a point or a curve, such that

X\setminus D
supports a holomorphic volume form with at most logarithmic poles
along
D
. We assume further that
D
has a zero-dimensional stratum. The mirror
is constructed as the affine or projective spectrum of a newly defined degree
zero relative quantum cohomology ring for the pair
(X,D)
that is expected to
only depend on
X\setminus D
. The definition of the relative quantum cohomology
ring has been made possible by the development of punctured Gromov-Witten
invariants, a variant of logarithmic Gromov-Witten invariants
[
GS4
,
Ch
,
AC
]
admitting negative contact orders, in joint work of the authors with
Abramovich and Chen
[
ACGS2
]
. The structure coefficients of relative
quantum cohomology involve invariants with two positive and one negative or zero
contact orders.

In our opinion this is a very clean and satisfying construction from the
algebraic geometric point of view: It generalizes many of the known geometric
mirror constructions to a large,
birationally distinguished
class of
algebraic varieties.
1
1

1

It is a certain joke of history that a slight
twist to one of the first objects arising in the context of mirror symmetry,
namely quantum cohomology, turns out to hold an answer to the puzzling
fundamental question in mirror symmetry in such great generality.
For example,
maximally unipotent degenerations of smooth proper Calabi-Yau varieties, the
original object of study in mirror symmetry, and Fano manifolds with an
anticanonical divisor with zero-dimensional stratum both fall in this class of
varieties.

There is also a clear tentative interpretation of relative quantum cohomology in
terms of symplectic geometry as a degree zero symplectic cohomology ring

[
Pa
,
Po1
,
GaPo
,
Po2
]
. This link both
points to possible generalizations of the construction to a purely symplectic
framework and to higher degree relative quantum cohomology rings
[
GPoS
]
, as
well as to a proof of homological mirror symmetry
[
Ko
]
in this framework

[
PS
]
.

The canonical wall structure and the SYZ interpretation of
intrinsic mirror symmetry

The present paper puts the intrinsic mirror construction of

[
GS7
]
into the context of our long-term program aiming at an
algebraic-geometric implementation of the SYZ picture of mirror symmetry. Our
program is based on a tropicalization of the geometry, leading to a polyhedral
manifold
B
with an integral affine structure with singularities (a

polyhedral affine manifold
) together with a wall structure. The
polyhedral affine manifold provides the central fiber of the mirror family, the
uncorrected mirror, while the wall structure carries the quantum corrections to
build a consistent deformation of this uncorrected mirror. In
[
GS3
]
we
gave an algorithmic inductive construction of the wall structure for cases with
affine singularities that are locally rigid in some sense, purely in terms of
affine geometry. We have long suspected, but could make precise only in very
limited cases
[
GPS
,
GHK
]
, that for the construction of the mirror of a pair

(X,D)
the wall structure should be constructible from the tropicalizations of
rational curves on
X
virtually intersecting
D
at one point; the enumerative
invariants carried by the wall structure should then be the corresponding
punctured Gromov-Witten invariants, see e.g.
[
GS5
, §4]
. This picture is
the algebraic-geometric analogue of the quantum corrections on the symplectic
side from Maslov index zero pseudoholomorphic disks with boundaries on
Lagrangian fibers that give rise to the bounding cochains in
[
FOOO
]
.

With punctured Gromov-Witten theory sufficiently developed
[
ACGS2
]
, we
have now been able to make this picture a reality. Moreover, using the general
framework of constructing families from consistent wall structures worked out in

[
GHS
]
, we give an alternative and technically simpler proof of the
associativity of the degree zero relative quantum cohomology ring from

[
GS7
]
in the case of interest for mirror symmetry, where
(X,D)
has
logarithmic Kodaira dimension zero and
D
has a zero-dimensional stratum. We
emphasize that only the present paper makes the clear connection of the
intrinsic mirror construction to the SYZ picture. The assumption that

D
has a zero-dimensional stratum indeed provides families of Lagrangian tori
in
X\setminus D
degenerating to the zero dimensional stratum. Thus this
assumption can be both viewed as a replacement of the existence of Lagrangian
torus fibrations or, in a degeneration situation, of the maximally unipotent
monodromy assumption
[
Mo
]
.

The present paper also provides the link to the algorithmic construction of
walls
[
GS3
]
, and to previous mirror constructions in two-dimensions

[
GHK
]
and for cluster varieties
[
GHKK
]
, also giving rise to rich
combinatorial structures, see e.g.
[
Che
,
ChMN
,
ChMd
,
Ma
,
Md1
,
RW
,
Pr
]
. All these previous constructions generalize known and
tested mirror constructions such as
[
Ba
,
BB
,
HV
,
Gr1
]
, and
they have also been independently tested, see e.g.
[
GS2
,
Gr2
,
La
,
RS
,
Md2
,
BBMN
,
HK
]
, thus providing further evidence that
[
GS7
]
really does
produce mirror pairs. Wall structures also give powerful methods for explicit
computations
[
GS3
,
GPS
,
AG
]
, and in fact, all examples of mirror pairs
we have computed explicitly were first obtained via their wall structures.

Another motivation is that the wall structures contain a lot of information not
directly accessible from the relative quantum cohomology ring in
[
GS7
]
.
For example, our wall structures suggest to generalize Mikhalkin-style tropical
correspondence theorems for curve counting from toric ambient geometries to
logarithmic Calabi-Yau varieties. In this correspondence, straight lines are to
be replaced by the
broken lines
reviewed in §
4.1
.
Broken lines are certain piecewise straight lines with possible bends when
crossing a wall due to interaction with the tropical disks carried by the wall.
The present paper also gives a systematic treatment of the geometric
information carried by the wall structure, which sometimes contains interesting
enumerative information in its own right
[
GPS
,
Bo4
,
AG
,
Gf
]
.

Statement of main results

We now describe the results of the paper in more detail. In §
1
we
define the relevant polyhedral affine manifold
B
as the dual intersection
complex of
D
, study the relevant affine geometry and discuss the additional
conditions compared to
[
GS7
]
to fulfill the assumptions on
B
in

[
GHS
]
. As in
[
GS7
]
we distinguish the
absolute
and

relative case
. In both cases
X
is a smooth variety over an
algebraically closed field
\Bbbk
of characteristic zero and
D\subset X
is a
normal crossing divisor. In the absolute case
X
is projective over
\Bbbk
while
in the relative case we have a projective morphism
g:X\to S
with
S
an
affine curve or spectrum of a DVR,
g
smooth away from a closed point
0\in S
and
g^{-1}(0)\subseteq D
. In other words,
g
induces a log smooth morphism
(X,\mathcal{M}_{X})\to(S,\mathcal{M}_{S})

when endowing
X,S
with their respective divisorial log structures. Let

\Sigma(X)
denote the tropicalization of
(X,D)
introduced in
§
1.1
.

Assumption T
.

The pair
(X,D)
fulfills Assumptions
1.1
and
Assumptions
1.2
related to its tropicalization
\Sigma(X)
in the
absolute and relative cases, respectively.

We show in Proposition
1.6
that Assumption T holds for
resolutions of log Calabi-Yau minimal models with connected
D
having a
zero-dimensional stratum, using a result of Kollár and Xu
[
KX
]
. While the
existence of log minimal models is not yet generally known in dimensions greater
than three, a recent result of Birkar paraphrased in Theorem
1.7

shows their existence in the relevant situation under a technical assumption

[
Bi2
, Cor. 1.5]
. The upshot is that we expect Assumption T to be
fulfilled for all practical purposes for cases of interest in mirror symmetry.

§
2
reviews some material concerning
punctured maps, their moduli theory and their tropicalizations. A
key result for this paper is Lemma
2.5
classifying
those tropical punctured maps that later appear in walls and broken lines.

After these preparations, §
3

introduces the two main players of this paper, the canonical wall structure

\mathscr{S}_{\mathrm{can}}
on
B
and logarithmic broken lines, both defined in terms of
certain punctured Gromov-Witten invariants. Walls are defined in terms of
punctured Gromov-Witten invariants with one non-zero contact order. There are in
fact two canonical wall structures
\mathscr{S}_{\mathrm{can}}^{\mathrm{undec}}
,

\mathscr{S}_{\mathrm{can}}
(Constructions
3.8
and

3.13
), where
\mathscr{S}_{\mathrm{can}}^{\mathrm{undec}}
only
fixes the total curve class while
\mathscr{S}_{\mathrm{can}}
prescribes a curve class for each
vertex of the tropical disk (“decorated wall type”). The refinement concerning
curve classes is necessary for some proofs. The formulas defining the walls in
terms of punctured Gromov-Witten invariants are stated in (
3.9
)–(
3.11
) and in (
3.12
) for the
undecorated and the decorated cases, respectively. A similar definition, but
with punctured invariants with two rather than one contact orders, one of which is negative, provides the notion of
logarithmic broken line
.

At this point we have in principle the same objects as in
[
GHS
]
, but we
neither know that the wall structure is consistent nor that our logarithmic
broken lines have anything in common with the broken lines from
[
GHS
]
,
which are defined algebraic-combinatorially from the canonical wall structure

\mathscr{S}_{\mathrm{can}}
. Our first main result, covered in §
4
, is
that these two notions of broken lines indeed agree.

Theorem A
.

Let
(X,D)
fulfill Assumption T. Then the broken lines for the canonical wall
structures
\mathscr{S}_{\mathrm{can}}
and
\mathscr{S}_{\mathrm{can}}^{\mathrm{undec}}
are exactly the logarithmic broken lines.

Theorem A follows from the bijection between logarithmic broken line types and
families of broken lines of fixed combinatorial type (Proposition
4.13
), with equality of the corresponding coefficients (Theorem
4.14
).

The proof of the decisive Theorem
4.14
relies
on a gluing formula for punctured Gromov-Witten invariants that gives the
crucial interpretation of bending at a wall as attaching a number
l
of genus
zero punctured maps with one puncture (“bubbles”) to a genus zero punctured
map with two punctures, by adding an irreducible component with trivial
numerical information and
l+2
punctures and identifying
l+1
pairs of
punctures to nodes. This gluing problem is simplified a lot since broken lines
only interact with walls in a cell of codimension zero or one. The codimension
zero case corresponds to gluing in a zero-dimensional stratum of
D
, with the
added component to the stable map necessarily contracted. In codimension one the
gluing happens along a one-dimensional stratum. Luckily, an argument by Kollár
shows that Assumption T implies that a one-dimensional stratum is actually
isomorphic to
\mathbb{P}^{1}
with stratified structure given by two points
(Proposition
1.3
). Both cases can then be treated in a
rather straightforward manner by the numerical gluing formula for punctured
invariants proved by Yixian Wu
[
Wu
]
, which applies when all gluing strata
are toric. For the reader’s convenience and to fix notations we recall this
formula in Appendix
A
.

The essential property of wall structures needed for constructing deformations
is a certain notion of “consistency”. Consistency says that the schemes
obtained by gluing local standard models for the deformation in codimension zero
and one in a way prescribed by the walls containing any given codimension two
subset
\mathfrak{j}\subset B
has enough global regular functions. We prove this
property by restricting to the wall structure on the star of
\mathfrak{j}
given by the
walls containing
\mathfrak{j}
, and then showing that the theta functions obtained from
sums over logarithmic broken lines with fixed endpoint provide such functions.
Thus consistency comes from invariance properties of certain sums of punctured
invariants. This reverses the logic in
[
GHS
]
where we assume consistency
to construct theta functions, due to a lack of an a priori interpretation of
broken lines. We obtain our second main result, Theorem
5.2
in
the body of the text.

Theorem B
.

Let
(X,D)
fulfill Assumption T. Then the canonical wall structures

\mathscr{S}_{\mathrm{can}}
and
\mathscr{S}_{\mathrm{can}}^{\mathrm{undec}}
are consistent in the sense of

[
GHS
]
.

[
GHS
]
now provides a ring of theta functions that serves as the affine or
projective coordinate ring of our mirror family, depending if we are in the
absolute or relative case. The last section, §
6
, is devoted to proving that this ring agrees with the relative quantum
cohomology of
[
GS7
]
.

Theorem C
.

Let
(X,D)
fulfill Assumption T. The ring of theta functions associated to the
canonical wall structure
\mathscr{S}_{\mathrm{can}}
via
[
GHS
]
agrees with the degree zero
relative quantum cohomology ring from
[
GS7
]
. In particular, the canonical
wall structure in connection with
[
GHS
]
produces the intrinsic mirror
family from
[
GS7
]
.

This theorem is stated as an equality of structure coefficients in
Theorem
6.1
. The key step in the proof is to split the
structure coefficients from
[
GS7
]
according to types of tropical
punctured maps with two unbounded and one bounded leg. A dimension count
shows that such tropical punctured maps split into two connected components when
removing the vertex containing the bounded leg, with each connected component
leading to a tropical punctured map as they appear in broken lines. The relation
to the structure coefficients of the rings defined by the theta functions then
boils down to another application of Yixian Wu’s gluing formula
[
Wu
]
, in a
particularly simple situation with a zero-dimensional gluing stratum and
transversality of the tropical gluing situation, see Lemma
6.6
.

Related work

Under the assumption that
X\setminus D
is affine and contains a
full-dimensional algebraic torus Sean Keel and Tony Yu gave an alternative
construction of the degree zero relative quantum cohomology ring with Berkovich
non-archimedean methods
[
KY
]
. The presence of the algebraic torus makes
it possible to avoid negative contact orders by tropically extending negative
contact order legs out to infinity, so that contact orders become positive.

[
KY
]
also construct wall structures with the same assumptions, via a
different, but presumably equivalent, approach to ours, by counting the effect
on analytic cylinders interacting with the wall.

The paper
[
AG
]
of the first author with Hülya Argüz has been written
in parallel to the present work. Generalizing
[
GPS
]
to higher dimensions,
this work treats the case that
(X,D)
is a blowing up of a toric variety in
hypersurfaces contained in its toric boundary with
D
the strict transform of
that toric boundary. The main result says that in this situation our canonical
wall structure agrees with an algorithmically constructed consistent wall
structure, following
[
GS3
]
. Thus
[
AG
]
provides a rich source of
explicit examples. Because of this, we refer the interested reader to that paper
for examples more complicated than the ones given in this paper.

Honglu Fan, Longting Wu and Fenglong You, in the case of a smooth boundary
divisor, and Hsian-Hua Tseng and Fenglong You in the case of a normal crossings
divisor, made an alternative proposal for a
relative quantum cohomology ring based on orbifold Gromov-Witten invariants

[
FWY
,
TY
]
. As pointed out by Dhruv Ranganathan, their invariants do not
have the correct invariance properties under log étale modifications
to immediately agree with our relative quantum cohomology ring.
However, since the first version of the current paper,

[
BNR
]
has achieved a comparison result between orbifold invariants
and log invariants with non-negative contact orders.
It remains to be
seen if a modification of their definitions could also give
negative contact orders. In any
case, there are no wall structures in this picture since walls are genuinely
tropical objects while orbifold Gromov-Witten theory is not known to be linked to
tropical geometry.

In two dimensions, Pierrick Bousseau
[
Bo1
,
Bo2
,
Bo3
]
gave a
higher genus interpretation for quantum versions of wall structures. It remains
an interesting question as to whether there is a higher-dimensional
generalization of this work.

Yoel Groman and Umut Varolgunes kindly informed us of their work in progress
aiming at a construction of mirrors based on symplectic cohomology type
invariants for compact subsets of symplectic manifolds. One of their geometric
frameworks rely on decomposing a symplectic manifold into simpler pieces using a
multiple cut configuration as in
[
TMZ
, Def. 13]
. A multiple cut
configuration gives rise to an SC symplectic degeneration, which is a symplectic
analogue of a semi-stable degeneration. Therefore, this approach is a symplectic
hybrid version of both the patching construction from
[
GS3
]
and the
symplectic cohomology interpretation of
[
GS7
]
. It presently does not
involve a wall structure.

1.
The basic setup

1.1.
The tropicalization of a log Calabi-Yau pair

We use the same setup for the log Calabi-Yau case as in
[
GS7
]
, §1.
Explicitly, we fix a non-singular variety
\underline{X}
of dimension
n
and a simple
normal crossings divisor
D\subseteq\underline{X}
yielding a divisorial log structure
on
\underline{X}
, log smooth over
\operatorname{Spec}\Bbbk
. We write
X
as this log scheme, and

D=D_{1}+\cdots+D_{s}
the decomposition of
D
into its irreducible
components. We consider the
absolute case
, in which
X
is
projective over
S=\operatorname{Spec}\Bbbk
, and the
relative case
, in which one has in
addition a projective log smooth morphism
g:X\rightarrow S
with
S
a
regular one-dimensional scheme over
\operatorname{Spec}\Bbbk
with a divisorial
log structure coming from a single closed point
0\in S
. We include here
the case that
S
is the spectrum of a complete DVR, or is an affine curve.
Necessarily
g^{-1}(0)\subseteq D
, but we do not require equality.

To avoid combinatorial complexities and to fit with the hypotheses of

[
GHS
]
, we will assume that for any index set
I\subseteq\{1,\ldots,s\}
,
the (possibly empty) stratum
X_{I}:=\bigcap_{i\in I}D_{i}
of
D
is
connected. This can always be achieved via a log étale birational modification
of
X
. In this case, the
tropicalization

\Sigma(X)
(see

[
ACGS1
, §2.1.4]
) has a simple description, following
[
GS7
, Ex. 1.4]
, as a polyhedral cone complex in
\operatorname{Div}_{D}(X)^{*}_{\mathbb{R}}
. Here

\operatorname{Div}_{D}(X)
is the group of divisors supported on
D
and
\operatorname{Div}_{D}(X)^{*}_{\mathbb{R}}
is
the dual vector space. If
D_{1}^{*},\ldots,D_{s}^{*}
is the basis dual to

D_{1},\ldots,D_{s}
, then

\Sigma(X):=\left\{\sum_{i\in I}\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}D_{i}^{*}\,|\,I\subseteq\{1,\ldots,s\},X_{I}\not=\emptyset\right\}.

For
\rho=\sum_{i\in I}\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}D_{i}^{*}\in\Sigma(X)
, we will often write
X_{\rho}

instead of
X_{I}
in the sequel, keeping with the convention
of
[
ACGS1
,
ACGS2
]
. We usually view
X_{\rho}
as a log scheme,
with the strict closed embedding
X_{\rho}\hookrightarrow X
.
We write
|\Sigma(X)|
for the support
of this polyhedral cone complex.

For a stratum
X_{I}=X_{\rho}
, we write
\partial X_{I}
or

\partial X_{\rho}
for the reduced
divisor on
X_{I}
given as

\sum_{j\not\in I}D_{j}\cap X_{I}.

The pair
(X,D)
is

log Calabi-Yau
if the logarithmic canonical class

K_{X}+D
is
numerically equivalent to an effective
\mathbb{Q}
-divisor supported on
D
.
We then fix once and for all an explicit representation

(1.1)

K_{X}+D\equiv_{\mathbb{Q}}\sum_{i}a_{i}D_{i}

with the
a_{i}\geq 0
. In general, our mirror construction depends
on this choice in that it determines the set of good divisors below
and hence the Kontsevich-Soibelman skeleton. For the situations
considered in this paper, however, we expect this skeleton to be
well-defined. This is known to be the case if
(X,D)\rightarrow S
is
a degeneration of Calabi-Yau manifolds with
D
the fibre over
0\in S
, see
[
NX
]
. More generally, see

[
GS7
, Ex. 1.23]
for further discussion
on this point. Recall that
K_{X}+D
is the first Chern class
of the sheaf
\Omega^{1}_{X}=\Omega^{1}_{\underline{X}}(\log D)

of logarithmic differential forms.
We call
D_{i}

good
if
a_{i}=0
.
We call a stratum
X_{I}

good
if
a_{i}=0
for all
i\in I
.
We write

\partial^{\mathrm{good}}X_{I}:=\sum_{j\not\in I,a_{j}=0}D_{j}\cap X_{I},\quad\quad\partial^{\mathrm{bad}}X_{I}:=\sum_{j\not\in I,a_{j}>0}D_{j}\cap X_{I},

so that
\partial X_{I}=\partial^{\mathrm{good}}X_{I}+\partial^{\mathrm{bad}}X_{I}
.

The
Kontsevich-Soibelman skeleton
of
X
is the pair
(B,\mathscr{P})

where

\mathscr{P}:=\{\sigma\in\Sigma(X)\,|\,\hbox{$X_{\sigma}$ is good}\}

and

B=\bigcup_{\sigma\in\mathscr{P}}\sigma.

Thus
B
is a topological space with polyhedral cone decomposition
\mathscr{P}

with all cones standard simplicial cones.

We denote by
\mathscr{P}^{[i]}
the set of
i
-dimensional cones of
\mathscr{P}
and

\mathscr{P}_{{\operatorname{max}}}
the set of maximal cones.

In
[
GS7
]
, we were able to construct a ring from the above data
with no further assumptions. However, for the setup of wall crossing
structures in
[
GHS
]
, we need to impose some additional
hypotheses on the pair
(X,D)
and the map to
S
. We list these
assumptions here, which will be in force for the remainder of the
paper, and discuss in §
1.2
the naturality of these assumptions.

Assumptions 1.1

(The absolute case)
.

We assume that

(1)

\mathscr{P}
contains an
n
-dimensional cone, where
n=\dim X
.

(2)

Whenever
\rho\in\mathscr{P}^{[n-1]}
,
\sigma\in\Sigma(X)
with

\dim\sigma=n
and
\rho\subseteq\sigma
, we also have
\sigma\in\mathscr{P}
.
Put another way, a good one-dimensional stratum
X_{\rho}
only
intersects good divisors.

(3)

Whenever
\rho\in\mathscr{P}
with
\dim X_{\rho}>1
,
\partial^{\mathrm{good}}X_{\rho}
is connected.

Assumptions 1.2

(The relative case)
.

We assume that, with
g:X\rightarrow S
in the relative case and
\dim X=n
:

(1)

Conditions (1)–(3) of Assumptions
1.1
hold for
the pair
(X,D)
.

(2)

For any
\rho\in\mathscr{P}
,
g|_{X_{\rho}}
has
geometrically connected fibres.
(Note
g|_{X_{\rho}}
may be constant.)

Proposition 1.3
.

If
(X,D)
satisfies Assumptions
1.1
or
1.2

in the absolute or relative cases respectively, then
(B,\mathscr{P})
is a
pseudomanifold in the sense of satisfying conditions (1)–(5)
of
[
GHS
, Constr. 1.1]
. Further, for each one-dimensional
good stratum
X_{\rho}
, either (a)
X_{\rho}\cong\mathbb{P}^{1}
and

\partial X_{\rho}
consists of two points, or (b) we are in the
relative case and
g|_{X_{\rho}}
is an isomorphism, with
\partial X_{\rho}

a single point.

Proof.

First, conditions (1) and (2) of
[
GHS
, Constr. 1.1]

follow immediately from the construction of
\mathscr{P}
as a fan
in
\operatorname{Div}_{D}(X)^{*}_{\mathbb{R}}
. Note this description is possible precisely
because we have made the assumption that the
X_{I}
are connected.

Condition (3) states that every
\rho\in\mathscr{P}
is contained in an

n
-dimensional cone
\sigma\in\mathscr{P}
. In other words:

(1.2)

Every good stratum of
X
contains a zero-dimensional good stratum.

We will show (
1.2
)
by adapting an argument
of Kollár
[
Kl2
, Thm. 10]
. First note that
by adjunction, for
\rho\in\mathscr{P}
, we may inductively write

K_{X_{\rho}}+\partial X_{\rho}\equiv_{\mathbb{Q}}\sum_{i:D_{i}\not\supseteq X_{\rho}}a_{i}(D_{i}\cap X_{\rho}).

Thus the pair
(X_{\rho},\partial X_{\rho})
may also be viewed as
a log Calabi-Yau variety in our sense, with Assumptions

1.1
, (2),(3) holding automatically for this pair.

We now prove (
1.2
) by induction on
\dim X
,
with base case
\dim X=1
. In this case, (
1.2
)
is obvious, but we observe more.
In the absolute case,
X
is a compact non-singular curve and

D
is non-empty. By Assumptions
1.1
, (2),
all components of
D
must be good, and thus
K_{X}+D\equiv 0
. Thus
the only possibility is that
X\cong\mathbb{P}^{1}
and
D
consists of
two distinct points. In the relative case, by Assumptions
1.2
,
(2),
X\rightarrow S
has connected fibres and hence is an isomorphism,
and thus
D
consists of one point lying over
0\in S
.

Now assume (
1.2
) is true when
\dim X<n
, and consider
the case
\dim X=n
. Suppose
that
\rho
is maximal in
\mathscr{P}
, i.e., is not contained in a larger cone
of
\mathscr{P}
, but with
\dim\rho<n
.
By assumption, there exists an
n
-dimensional cone
\sigma\in\mathscr{P}
.
This gives strata
X_{\rho}
,
X_{\sigma}
with
\dim X_{\rho}>0
,

\dim X_{\sigma}=0
. After reordering the indices
1,\ldots,s
, we
may assume
X_{\sigma}\subseteq D_{1}
and
X_{\rho}\subseteq D_{r}
,
and by connectivity of
\partial^{\mathrm{good}}X
there exists
a sequence of good divisors
D_{2},\ldots,D_{r-1}
such that

D_{i}\cap D_{i+1}\not=\emptyset
for
1\leq i\leq r-1
.
Noting that
(D_{1},\partial D_{1})

satisfies Assumptions
1.1
, we see by the
inductive hypothesis that necessarily the good stratum
D_{1}\cap D_{2}

of
(D_{1},\partial D_{1})

contains a zero-dimensional good stratum. Thus the same is true of

(D_{2},\partial D_{2})
. Continuing in this way, we see the same
is true of
(D_{r},\partial D_{r})
, and hence again by the inductive
hypothesis
X_{\rho}
contains a zero-dimensional good stratum.

Note this argument also shows that all good one-dimensional strata satisfy
conditions (a) or (b) in the statement of the proposition.
Condition (4) of
[
GHS
, Constr. 1.1]
, which states that every

n-1
-dimensional cone
\rho
of
\mathscr{P}
is contained in one or two

n
-dimensional cones, thus follows immediately. In case (a),
\rho
is
contained in two maximal cones and in case (b),
\rho
is contained
in one maximal cone.

Finally, Condition (5) of
[
GHS
, Constr. 1.1]
follows
immediately from the assumed connectedness of

\partial^{\mathrm{good}}X_{\tau}
for
\tau\in\mathscr{P}
with

\dim\tau\leq n-2
.
∎

Example 1.4
.

We will illustrate some features of our construction via a simple example.
This is a special case of the blow-ups of toric varieties considered
more generally in
[
AG
]
, and we refer the reader to that paper for
more details and other more interesting examples.

Write
(\overline{X},\overline{D})
for the toric pair given by
\overline{X}=\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
and
\overline{D}
its toric boundary.
Label the six toric boundary divisors as
\overline{D}_{i,0}
,
\overline{D}_{i,\infty}
, where
\overline{D}_{i,0}
indicates the product obtained by
replacing the
i^{th}
factor in
\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
with

\{0\}
, and
\overline{D}_{i,\infty}
instead replaces the
i^{th}
factor with

\{\infty\}
.

Let
Z_{1},Z_{2}\subseteq\overline{X}
be the curves

Z_{1}:=\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\{0\}\times\{1\}\subseteq\overline{D}_{2,0},\quad Z_{2}:=\overline{D}_{1,\infty}\cap\overline{D}_{2,\infty}=\{\infty\}\times\{\infty\}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}.

Let
\pi:X\rightarrow\overline{X}
be the blow-up with center

Z_{1}\cup Z_{2}
, with exceptional divisors
E_{1},E_{2}
sitting over

Z_{1}
and
Z_{2}
respectively, see Figure
1.1
. Let
D_{i,0}
,
D_{i,\infty}
be
the strict transforms of
\overline{D}_{i,0},\overline{D}_{i,\infty}
.

Figure 1.1.
Sketch of
(X,D)
in Example
1.4
.

Then we take

D=D_{1,0}+D_{2,0}+D_{3,0}+D_{1,\infty}+D_{2,\infty}+D_{3,\infty}+E_{2}.

Note that the blow-up along
Z_{2}
is a toric blow-up, so if

\pi
is factored as
X\rightarrow X^{\prime}\rightarrow\overline{X}

with
X^{\prime}
this toric blow-up,
D
is the strict transform of
the toric boundary of
X^{\prime}
, and
K_{X}+D=0
.
Thus all divisors are good, and
(X,D)
satisfies
Assumptions
1.1
.

In this case
(B,\mathscr{P})
is piecewise linear isomorphic to the fan
for
X^{\prime}
.

1.2.
Minimal log Calabi-Yau pairs

We now briefly discuss how restrictive Assumptions
1.1

and
1.2
might be. Certainly, they impose much stronger
conditions than those imposed in
[
GS7
]
. Indeed, for a simple
example, consider
X
a rational elliptic surface with
f:X\rightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}
the elliptic fibration, and assume the fibre of
f
over

p_{1}\in\mathbb{P}^{1}
is a Kodaira type
I_{n}
fibre for some
n\geq 3
.
Take
D=f^{*}(p_{1}+p_{2}+p_{3})
where
p_{2},p_{3}\in\mathbb{P}^{1}
are points over
which the fibre of
f
is non-singular.
Since
K_{X}=f^{*}(-p_{1})
, we may in fact write

K_{X}+D\sim 2f^{*}(p_{3}).

Thus with this choice of representation (
1.1
),
each irreducible component of
f^{-1}(p_{1})
and the
fibre
f^{-1}(p_{2})
are good divisors. So

\partial^{\mathrm{good}}X
is not connected. Note also
that
f^{-1}(p_{2})
does not contain a zero-dimensional stratum
even though
\partial^{\mathrm{good}}X
does.

An obvious problem with this example is that the pair
(X,D)

is not log Kodaira dimension zero. In particular, if we assume

(X,D)
is log Kodaira dimension zero, we conjecturally obtain
the desired hypotheses. The standard conjectures of the minimal model
program in particular would imply the existence of a log Calabi-Yau
minimal model:

Definition 1.5
.

A
log Calabi-Yau minimal model
is a
\mathbb{Q}
-factorial
divisorial log terminal (dlt) pair
(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})
such that
K_{X^{\prime}}+D^{\prime}\equiv 0
.

Let
(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})
be a log Calabi-Yau minimal model. As necessarily each
irreducible component of
D^{\prime}
is
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier, the argument of

[
NXY
, Thm. 4.5]
shows that
if
C\subseteq D^{\prime}
is a one-dimensional
stratum of
D^{\prime}
, then
X^{\prime}
is non-singular and
D^{\prime}
is simple normal crossings
in a neighbourhood of
C
.
2
2

2

The context of
[
NXY
]
is in the case
of a degeneration of Calabi-Yau manifolds, but the argument still applies
in our case.
Hence, we may choose a resolution of
singularities
\phi:X\rightarrow X^{\prime}
with exceptional divisor
E
,
such that
D=\phi^{-1}_{*}(D^{\prime})+E
is
simple normal crossings, and such that
\phi
is an isomorphism
in a neighbourhood of each one-dimensional stratum of
D^{\prime}
.
(Here
\phi^{-1}_{*}
is the notation of
[
Kl1
]
for the
strict transform.) We may then write

(1.3)

K_{X}+\phi_{*}^{-1}D^{\prime}=\phi^{*}(K_{X^{\prime}}+D^{\prime})+\sum_{i}a(E_{i},X^{\prime},D^{\prime})E_{i},

where the
E_{i}
are the irreducible components of
E
, and

a(E_{i},X^{\prime},D^{\prime})
is, by definition, the log discrepancy. By
the definition of dlt (see e.g.,
[
Kl1
, Def. 2.8]
), we have

a(E_{i},X^{\prime},D^{\prime})>-1
for all
i
. Since
K_{X^{\prime}}+D^{\prime}\equiv 0
, we may
add
E
to both sides to obtain

(1.4)

K_{X}+D\equiv\sum_{i}a_{i}D_{i}

with
a_{i}\geq 0
and
a_{i}=0
if and only if
D_{i}=\phi_{*}^{-1}(D_{j}^{\prime})

for some
j
. Thus we see that good components of
D

are precisely the strict transforms of components of
D^{\prime}
.

Proposition 1.6
.

Let
(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})
be a log Calabi-Yau minimal model with
X^{\prime}

projective, and let

\phi:(X,D)\rightarrow(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})
be a resolution of singularities
which is an isomorphism in neighbourhoods of one-dimensional strata of
D^{\prime}
.
Suppose that any intersection of components
of
D^{\prime}
is connected
3
3

3

This requirement is only necessary to
ensure the assumptions on connectivity of strata made throughout this
paper.
and that
D^{\prime}
has a zero-dimensional
stratum. Then
(X,D)
satisfies Assumptions

1.1
and
(B,\mathscr{P})

is the dual intersection cone complex of
(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})
.

Proof.

Since
D^{\prime}
has a zero-dimensional stratum, it follows that every stratum of
D^{\prime}

has a zero-dimensional stratum by
[
KX
, Thm. 2,(1)]
. The assumption that

\phi
is an isomorphism on neighbourhoods of one-dimensional strata of
D^{\prime}

then implies that for any stratum
Z^{\prime}
of
D^{\prime}
, there is a dense open subset

U^{\prime}\subseteq Z^{\prime}
such that
\phi^{-1}(U^{\prime})\rightarrow U^{\prime}
is an isomorphism. Let

Z
be the closure of
\phi^{-1}(U^{\prime})
: this allows us to pass from a stratum of

D^{\prime}
to a stratum of
D
. In particular, writing
D^{\prime}=D_{1}^{\prime}+\cdots+D^{\prime}_{s^{\prime}}
,

D=D_{1}+\cdots+D_{s}
with
s\geq s^{\prime}
, we may order the latter divisors so that
D_{i}=\phi_{*}^{-1}D_{i}^{\prime}
for
i\leq s^{\prime}
. Thus if
Z^{\prime}=\bigcap_{i\in I}D_{i}^{\prime}
, then the
corresponding stratum
Z
coincides with
D_{I}
. Since the good divisors amongst
the
D_{i}
are precisely
D_{1},\ldots,D_{s^{\prime}}
, the claim concerning
(B,\mathscr{P})

follows.

Now conditions (1) and (2) of Assumptions
1.1
hold by assumption,
the first since
D^{\prime}
is assumed to have a zero dimensional stratum and the
second because
\phi
is assumed to be an isomorphism in a neighbourhood of each
one-dimensional stratum of
D^{\prime}
. Finally (3) holds from results of Kollár and
Xu
[
KX
]
. Indeed, if
Z^{\prime}
is a stratum of
D^{\prime}
of dimension at least two,
and
Z^{\prime\prime}\subseteq Z^{\prime}
is the union of strata properly contained in
Z^{\prime}
, then

Z^{\prime\prime}
is connected, see §2, paragraph 16 of
[
KX
]
, as well as the
discussion immediately preceding
[
KX
, §2]
, which shows that
(Z^{\prime},Z^{\prime\prime})
is
also a dlt log Calabi-Yau pair.
∎

In dimension larger than three,
the existence of log Calabi-Yau minimal models of pairs
(X,D)

of log Kodaira dimension zero is expected but currently unknown.
Caucher Birkar, however, has given a criterion for the existence of
minimal models and the necessary connectedness statement of
Assumption
1.1
. We paraphrase
[
Bi2
, Thm. 1.4, Cor. 1.5]
:

Theorem 1.7
.

Let
(X,D)
be a pair as in §
1.1
with

K_{X}+D\equiv_{\mathbb{R}}\sum_{i}a_{i}D_{i}
with
a_{i}\geq 0
. Suppose

\mathscr{P}
has an
n=\dim X
-dimensional cone corresponding to a zero
dimensional stratum consisting of
x\in X
. Let

\phi:Y\rightarrow X
be the blow-up of
X
at
x
with exceptional
divisor
E
, and suppose that
\phi^{*}(K_{X}+D)-tE
is not
pseudo-effective
4
4

4

i.e., is not a limit of effective
\mathbb{R}
-divisors.

for every real number
t>0
. Then
(X,D)
has a log Calabi-Yau minimal
model, and the connectedness hypothesis Assumptions
1.1
,(3)
holds.

In the relative case, there is a different kind of result to which one may
appeal. With
S
as usual, write
S^{\circ}=S\setminus\{0\}
,
and suppose given a
\mathbb{Q}
-factorial dlt pair
(X^{\circ},D^{\circ})
equipped
with a flat morphism
g^{\circ}:X^{\circ}\rightarrow S^{\circ}

which is a relatively minimal log Calabi-Yau, i.e., the intersection
number of
K_{X^{\circ}}+D^{\circ}
with any curve contracted by

g^{\circ}
is zero. Then
[
KNX
, Thm. 2]
, generalizing
the statements of
[
Bi1
, Thm. 1.4]
and
[
HX
, Thm. 1.1]
,
states that there is a finite map
\tau:S^{\prime}\rightarrow S
totally ramified
over
0\in S
and a morphism

g^{\prime}:(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})\rightarrow S^{\prime}
such that:

(1)

The restriction of
g^{\prime}
to
\tau^{-1}(S^{\circ})
is isomorphic
to the pull-back morphism

(X^{\circ},D^{\circ})\times_{S^{\circ}}\tau^{-1}(S^{\circ})\rightarrow\tau^{-1}(S^{\circ}).

(2)

(X^{\prime},D^{\prime}+F^{\prime})
is dlt and relatively log Calabi-Yau,
where
F^{\prime}
is the fibre of

g^{\prime}
over
0\in S^{\prime}
.

Given this, if the general fibre of
g^{\circ}
has a zero-dimensional stratum
then
(X^{\prime},D^{\prime}+F^{\prime})
has a zero-dimensional stratum over
0\in S^{\prime}
. In any event,
as long as
(X^{\prime},D^{\prime}+F^{\prime})
has a zero-dimensional stratum, we may then resolve
singularities to obtain
(X,D)\rightarrow(X^{\prime},D^{\prime}+F^{\prime})
and apply
Proposition
1.6
to obtain the needed connectedness
statement of Assumption
1.1
, (3). Some additional care may be
necessary, however, to guarantee that
(X,D)\rightarrow S^{\prime}
is log smooth.

In the classical case of a degeneration of genuine Calabi-Yau manifolds,
the issue of log smoothness is not a concern, and the following proposition
summarizes the above discussion in this case.

Proposition 1.8
.

In the situation above, suppose
X^{\circ}\rightarrow S^{\circ}
is a
relatively minimal family of non-singular Calabi-Yau manifolds, i.e.,

K_{X^{\circ}/S^{\circ}}\equiv 0
. Then:

(1)

After a possible base-change
S^{\prime}\rightarrow S
branched at
0\in S
,
there is a dlt relatively minimal model
(X^{\prime},F^{\prime})\rightarrow S^{\prime}

where
F^{\prime}
is the fibre over
0\in S^{\prime}
and
X^{\prime}\setminus F^{\prime}\rightarrow S^{\prime}\setminus\{0\}
is the
base-change of
X^{\circ}\rightarrow S^{\circ}
.

(2)

If the monodromy of the family
X^{\circ}\rightarrow S^{\circ}

is maximally unipotent,
then there is a resolution of singularities

\phi:(X,D)\rightarrow(X^{\prime},F^{\prime})
and an expression

K_{X}+D\equiv\sum a_{i}D_{i}

with
a_{i}\not=0
if and only if
D_{i}
is exceptional for
\phi
.
Further, possibly after first performing a further base-change,
the data
(X,D)\rightarrow S^{\prime}
together with this description
of
K_{X}+D
satisfies Assumptions
1.2
.

Proof.

(1) follows from the discussion preceding the proposition.

For (2), first note that by
[
NXY
, Thm. 4.5]
,
X^{\prime}
is non-singular
in some neighbourhood of each one-dimensional stratum of
F^{\prime}
and

D^{\prime}
is simple normal crossings in that neighbourhood. Thus we may
find a resolution
\phi:(X,D)\rightarrow(X^{\prime},F^{\prime})
with (1)
D
a
normal crossings divisor which is the reduction
of
\phi^{-1}(F^{\prime})
; (2)
\phi
is an
isomorphism in neighbourhoods of one-dimensional strata of
F^{\prime}
; and
(3)
\phi

induces an isomorphism
X\setminus D\cong X^{\prime}\setminus F^{\prime}
.
After taking further base-change and resolving the resulting
singularities in a standard crepant
toric way appropriately (see
[
KKMS
, II,§4, III, Thm. 4.1]
),
we may further assume that any intersection of
irreducible components of
D
is connected. We may then use
(
1.3
) and (
1.4
) to obtain the
desired expression for
K_{X}+D
.

The condition of Assumption
1.1
, (1) is then implied
by
[
NX
, Thm. 4.1.10]

if the family
X\rightarrow S^{\prime}
is maximally unipotent,
The remaining conditions of Assumptions
1.1
and

1.2
are then immediate by construction and
Proposition
1.6
.
∎

Of course the existence of a maximally unipotent degeneration for a
type of Calabi-Yau manifold has been classically viewed as a
prerequisite for the existence of a mirror.

The conclusion is that the hypotheses of Assumptions
1.1

and
1.2
should be expected to hold in the cases of
interest for mirror symmetry.

1.3.
The affine structure

We continue to assume that
(X,D)
satisfies Assumptions
1.1

or
1.2
in the absolute or relative cases, with
\dim X=n
.

We set

\Delta:=\bigcup_{\sigma\in\mathscr{P}\atop\dim\sigma=n-2}\sigma

and

B_{0}:=B\setminus\Delta.

We also denote by
\partial B
the union of those
\rho\in\mathscr{P}^{[n-1]}

contained in only one top-dimensional cone, and write
\mathscr{P}_{\partial}\subseteq\mathscr{P}
for the set of cones contained in
\partial B
.
Set
\mathscr{P}_{\mathrm{int}}=\mathscr{P}\setminus\mathscr{P}_{\partial}
.
By Proposition
1.3
, in the
absolute case
\partial B=\emptyset
.

We define an integral affine structure
on
B_{0}
as follows. For
\tau\in\mathscr{P}
, denote
the
open star
of
\tau
to be

\mathrm{Star}(\tau):=\bigcup_{\tau\subseteq\sigma\in\mathscr{P}}\operatorname{Int}(\sigma).

If
\sigma\in\mathscr{P}_{{\operatorname{max}}}
, then
\sigma
, hence
\operatorname{Int}(\sigma)
,
is already endowed naturally with an affine coordinate chart arising
from its linear embedding in
\operatorname{Div}_{D}(X)^{*}_{\mathbb{R}}
.
If
\rho\in\mathscr{P}^{[n-1]}_{\partial}
, then
\mathrm{Star}(\rho)
inherits
an affine structure with boundary from the unique maximal
cone
\sigma
containing
\rho
. On the other hand, if

\rho\in\mathscr{P}^{[n-1]}_{\mathrm{int}}
, then
\rho=\sigma\cap\sigma^{\prime}
with

\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}\in\mathscr{P}^{[n]}
. We then define an embedding

(1.5)

\psi_{\rho}:\sigma\cup\sigma^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n},

well-defined up to an element of
\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{Z})
,
as follows. Let
\rho
be generated by
D_{i_{1}}^{*},\ldots,D_{i_{n-1}}^{*}
,
and assume that
\sigma
is generated by
\rho
and
D_{i_{n}}^{*}
,
while
\sigma^{\prime}
is generated by
\rho
and
D_{i_{n}^{\prime}}^{*}
.
Choose integral bases
e_{1},\ldots,e_{n}
and
e_{1},\ldots,e_{n-1},e_{n}^{\prime}

of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
subject to the constraint that

(1.6)

e_{n}+e_{n}^{\prime}=-\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}(D_{i_{j}}\cdot X_{\rho})e_{j}.

We note the intersection numbers are defined: the fact that

\rho\in\mathscr{P}_{\mathrm{int}}
implies
X_{\rho}
is proper.
We then define
\psi_{\rho}
to be piecewise linear, linear on
each cone, via

\psi_{\rho}(D_{i_{j}}^{*})=e_{j}
,
1\leq j\leq n
, and

\psi_{\rho}(D_{i_{n}^{\prime}}^{*})=e_{n}^{\prime}
.

This gives rise to affine charts
\psi_{\rho}:\mathrm{Star}(\rho)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and hence an affine structure on
B_{0}
. In the language
of
[
GHS
, Const. 1.1]
, this gives
(B,\mathscr{P})
the structure
of a
polyhedral affine pseudomanifold
.

Remark 1.9
.

This affine structure was first given in
[
GS6
]
in the case
that
K_{X}+D=0
. More generally, in the relative case
g:X\rightarrow S
with
D=g^{-1}(0)
, so that
g
is a degeneration of Calabi-Yau
manifolds,
[
NXY
]
showed that the above affine structure
was the correct affine structure on the base of the non-Archimedean
SYZ fibration, at least in the case that
(X,D)
is
a resolution
of a dlt relatively minimal model
(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})
as in §
1.2
.
In particular, they showed
that if
Z\subseteq D
was a one-dimensional stratum and
D_{i}\cdot Z<0

for all
D_{i}

containing
Z
, then the formal completion of
X
along
Z
is
isomorphic to the formal completion of a one-dimensional
stratum in a toric variety. The fan determining the toric variety
then determines the affine structure above.

In our situation, the affine structure can be
viewed as natural from the logarithmic point of view.

Lemma 1.10
.

Let
\rho\in\mathscr{P}^{[n-1]}_{\mathrm{int}}
,

\rho\subseteq\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}\in\mathscr{P}^{{\operatorname{max}}}
,
with chart
\psi_{\rho}:\sigma\cup\sigma^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
constructed
above. Let
\Sigma_{\rho}
be the fan consisting of the cones

\psi_{\rho}(\sigma)
,
\psi_{\rho}(\sigma^{\prime})

and their faces, and let
X_{\Sigma_{\rho}}
be the corresponding
toric variety. For
\tau\in\Sigma_{\rho}
, write
X_{\Sigma_{\rho},\tau}
for
the corresponding stratum of
X_{\Sigma_{\rho}}
. Let

X_{\rho}\subseteq X
,
X_{\Sigma_{\rho},\psi_{\rho}(\rho)}\subseteq X_{\Sigma_{\rho}}

be given the log structures making these inclusions strict, with

X_{\Sigma_{\rho}}
carrying the standard toric log structure.
Then there is an isomorphism

X_{\rho}\cong X_{\Sigma_{\rho},\psi_{\rho}(\rho)}

as log schemes over
\operatorname{Spec}\Bbbk
.

Proof.

By Proposition
1.3
,
X_{\rho}\cong\mathbb{P}^{1}
, and certainly

X_{\Sigma_{\rho},\psi_{\rho}(\rho)}\cong\mathbb{P}^{1}
, so the underlying schemes
are isomorphic. Further,
X_{\rho}
contains precisely two
zero-dimensional strata,
X_{\sigma}
and
X_{\sigma^{\prime}}
.
Let
X_{\rho}^{\prime}
denote the log structure on
\underline{X}_{\rho}

induced by the divisor
X_{\sigma}\cup X_{\sigma^{\prime}}
. If

X_{\rho}
is contained in divisors
D_{i_{1}},\ldots,D_{i_{n-1}}

corresponding to the edges of
\rho
,
let
\mathcal{M}_{j}
denote the restriction to
\underline{X}_{\rho}
of the divisorial log
structure on
X
induced by the divisor
D_{i_{j}}
. This log
structure is determined by the line bundle
\mathcal{O}_{X}(D_{i_{j}})|_{X_{\rho}}
.
Finally,

\mathcal{M}_{X_{\rho}}=\mathcal{M}_{X^{\prime}_{\rho}}\oplus_{\mathcal{O}_{X_{\rho}}^{\times}}\bigoplus_{j}\mathcal{M}_{j},

where all pushouts are over
\mathcal{O}_{X_{\rho}}^{\times}
.

On the other hand,
\mathcal{M}_{X_{\Sigma_{\rho},\psi_{\rho}(\rho)}}
has
a similar description, and if
D^{\prime}_{i_{1}},\ldots,D^{\prime}_{i_{n-1}}

are the toric divisors of
X_{\Sigma_{\rho}}
corresponding to the
edges of
\psi_{\rho}(\rho)
, we have

(1.7)

\deg\mathcal{O}_{X}(D_{i_{j}})|_{X_{\rho}}=\deg\mathcal{O}_{X_{\Sigma_{\rho}}}(D^{\prime}_{i_{j}})|_{X_{\Sigma_{\rho},\psi_{\rho}(\rho)}}

by
[
Od
]
, pg. 52 and the definition of
\psi_{\rho}
.
Thus
X_{\rho}
and
X_{\Sigma_{\rho},\psi_{\rho}(\rho)}

are in fact isomorphic as
log schemes.
∎

Example 1.11
.

We return to Example
1.4
. We first
remark that in general for a minimal log Calabi-Yau pair
satisfying Assumptions
1.1
,
the affine structure described on
B_{0}
,
the complement of the union of codimension two cones of
\mathscr{P}
, in fact extends
across the interior of all cones
\rho\in\mathscr{P}
such that

(X_{\rho},\partial X_{\rho})
is a toric pair.
See
[
AG
, Prop. 2.3]
for details. In the case of
Example
1.4
,
only the strict transforms of boundary divisors of
\overline{X}

which meet the center
Z_{1}
transversally fail to be toric: these are

D_{1,0}
and
D_{1,\infty}
.
Hence, after extending the affine structure where possible, the
smaller discriminant locus
\Delta
is homeomorphic to the line

\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}D_{1,0}^{*}\cup\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}D_{1,\infty}^{*}
.
The monodromy about this singularity may be calculated, and takes the form

\begin{pmatrix}1&1&0\\
0&1&0\\
0&0&1\end{pmatrix}
. See

[
AG
, §3.2]
for related calculations carried out in a more
general setting. This is the simplest
kind of singularity appearing in affine manifolds in our program.

We recall some standard notation, e.g., from
[
GHS
]
.

Definition 1.12
.

We write

B(\mathbb{Z}):=B\cap\operatorname{Div}_{D}(X)^{*},

the set of integral points on
B
.

Definition 1.13
.

We take
\Lambda
to be the local system on
B_{0}=B\setminus\Delta
consisting of
integral tangent vectors, and write

\check{\Lambda}:=\operatorname{Hom}(\Lambda,\mathbb{Z})
.

Construction 1.14
.

We now fix a finitely generated abelian group
H_{2}(X)
of possible
degree data for curves on
X
, see, e.g.,

[
GS7
]
, Basic Setup 1.6 for a discussion on this.
We may take, for example,
H_{2}(X)=H_{2}(X,\mathbb{Z})
if working over

\Bbbk=\mathbb{C}
, or may take
H_{2}(X)=N_{1}(X)
, curve classes modulo numerical
equivalence. We require that every proper curve
C\subseteq X

gives a curve class
[C]\in H_{2}(X)
, and that for any
stable map
f:C\rightarrow X
, there is a well-defined
curve class
f_{*}[C]\in H_{2}(X)
. We also require use of
intersection numbers with divisors, i.e., a pairing

H_{2}(X)\times\operatorname{Div}(X)\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
.
As in
[
GS7
]
, Basic Setup 1.6, we assume that
f_{*}[C]

is torsion if and only if
f
is a constant map, in which case

f_{*}[C]=0
.

In the relative case, the inclusion
X_{s}\hookrightarrow X
for

s\in S
should induce a natural map

(1.8)

\iota:H_{2}(X_{s})\rightarrow H_{2}(X).

We further choose a finitely generated monoid

Q\subseteq H_{2}(X)
such that (1)
Q
contains the classes of all
stable maps to
\underline{X}
; (2)
Q
is saturated; (3) the group of
invertible elements of
Q
coincides with the torsion part of

H_{2}(X)
.

We may then define a multi-valued piecewise linear (MPL) function

\varphi
with values in
Q^{{\operatorname{gp}}}_{\mathbb{R}}
, see
[
GHS
]
, Definition 1.8.
Such a function consists of a choice,
for each
\rho\in\mathscr{P}^{[n-1]}_{\mathrm{int}}
, of a single-valued PL function

\varphi_{\rho}:\mathrm{Star}(\rho)\rightarrow Q^{{\operatorname{gp}}}_{\mathbb{R}}
, well-defined
up to linear functions. The kink of
\varphi_{\rho}
is defined as follows. Let

x\in\operatorname{Int}(\rho)
, and let
\rho\subseteq\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}\in\mathscr{P}_{{\operatorname{max}}}
.
Let
n,n^{\prime}\in\check{\Lambda}_{x}
be the slopes of

\varphi_{\rho}|_{\sigma}
and
\varphi_{\rho}|_{\sigma^{\prime}}
. Then
we may write

(1.9)

n^{\prime}-n=\delta\cdot\kappa_{\rho}

where
\kappa_{\rho}\in Q^{{\operatorname{gp}}}
and

\delta:\Lambda_{x}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
is the surjective map
which vanishes on tangent vectors to
\rho
and is positive on
tangent vectors pointing into
\sigma^{\prime}
.
Then
\kappa_{\rho}
is the kink of
\varphi_{\rho}
.

An MPL function is completely determined by giving its kinks,
5
5

5

In

[
GHS
]
, we always worked with a monoid
Q
which was
torsion free. Of course, if
Q
has torsion, then the kink as defined
above only lies in
Q^{{\operatorname{gp}}}/Q^{{\operatorname{gp}}}_{\mathrm{tors}}
. However, if we wish
to work with a curve class group
H_{2}(X)
which has torsion, then
it is more natural to view the MPL function simply as a collection of
kinks in
Q^{{\operatorname{gp}}}=H_{2}(X)
. This will only affect the definition of
the sheaf
\mathcal{P}
, discussed in §
3.1
.

see
[
GHS
, Prop. 1.9]
. Throughout
this paper, we will work with
\varphi
such that
for
\rho\in\mathscr{P}^{[n-1]}_{\mathrm{int}}
,

\kappa_{\rho}=[X_{\rho}]\in H_{2}(X).

Note that this curve class makes sense as
\rho\not\subseteq\partial B
implies that
X_{\rho}
is proper. Further, the class

[X_{\rho}]
is then not invertible in
Q
.

The data of the polyhedral affine manifold
(B,\mathscr{P})
and the MPL
function
\varphi
are the necessary ingredients for the setup
of wall structures of
[
GHS
]
, §2.

We end this subsection by examining,
in the relative case, some additional structure. As
tropicalization is functorial, we obtain a map

\Sigma(g):\Sigma(X)\rightarrow\Sigma(S)=\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0},

which restricts to a map

g_{\mathrm{trop}}:B\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}.

Explicitly, viewing
\Sigma(X)
or
B
as a subset of
\operatorname{Div}_{D}(X)^{*}_{\mathbb{R}}
,
the (not necessarily reduced) divisor
g^{*}(0)
induces
a linear function on the latter space,
which restricts to
\Sigma(g)
or
g_{\mathrm{trop}}
in the two cases.

Proposition 1.15
.

g_{\mathrm{trop}}
is an affine submersion, and
\partial B
is the
union of all
(n-1)
-dimensional cones contained in
g_{\mathrm{trop}}^{-1}(0)
.

Proof.

For the first statement,
it is sufficient to show that
(g_{\mathrm{trop}}|_{\mathrm{Star}(\rho)})\circ\psi_{\rho}^{-1}
is a surjective linear function
on the fan
\Sigma_{\rho}
for each

\rho\in\mathscr{P}^{[n-1]}_{\mathrm{int}}
. Here
\psi_{\rho}
is the
local chart (
1.5
) and
\Sigma_{\rho}
is as defined
in Lemma
1.10
.

If we write the divisor
g^{*}(0)
as
\sum_{i}b_{i}D_{i}
, then

g_{\mathrm{trop}}
takes the value
b_{i}
on
D_{i}^{*}
. In particular,

(g_{\mathrm{trop}}|_{\mathrm{Star}(\rho)})\circ\psi_{\rho}^{-1}
is the piecewise
linear function on
\Sigma_{\rho}

corresponding to a toric divisor
D^{\prime}
on
X_{\Sigma_{\rho}}
which
may be written explicitly as follows. Denote by
D^{\prime}_{j}
the toric
divisor of
X_{\Sigma_{\rho}}
corresponding to the ray
\psi_{\rho}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}D^{*}_{j})
, for
j\in\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{n},i_{n}^{\prime}\}
. Then

D^{\prime}=b_{i_{n}^{\prime}}D^{\prime}_{i_{n}^{\prime}}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}b_{i_{j}}D^{\prime}_{i_{j}}.

However, by (
1.7
),
D^{\prime}\cdot X_{\Sigma_{\rho},\psi_{\rho}(\rho)}=g^{*}(0)\cdot X_{\rho}=0
as
g^{*}(0)
is algebraically equivalent to
the trivial divisor.
Thus
D^{\prime}
is linearly equivalent to zero, so

(g_{\mathrm{trop}}|_{\mathrm{Star}(\rho)})\circ\psi_{\rho}^{-1}
is linear, as claimed.
This latter map is also non-zero, as
X_{\rho}
is contained in
g^{-1}(0)
,
and hence it is surjective.

For the second statement, by definition
\partial B

is the union of those codimension one cones
\rho
only contained in
one maximal cone. But these correspond to the non-compact one-dimensional
good strata
X_{\rho}
, which are precisely the ones for which
g|_{X_{\rho}}

is surjective. However by the definition of
g_{\mathrm{trop}}
, these are
in one-to-one correspondence with the set of those
\rho\in\mathscr{P}^{[n-1]}

with
g_{\mathrm{trop}}(\rho)=0
.
∎

Frequently, in the relative case
g:X\rightarrow S
,
it is more intuitive to work not
with
(B,\mathscr{P})
but with the fibre of
g_{\mathrm{trop}}:B\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
over
1
.
This fits more
closely with the earlier point of view of affine manifolds associated
with degenerations and wall structures in
[
GS1
,
GS3
]
. Set

B^{\prime}:=g_{\mathrm{trop}}^{-1}(1)
, equipped with the polyhedral decomposition

\mathscr{P}^{\prime}:=\{\sigma\cap B^{\prime}\,|\,\sigma\in\mathscr{P}\}.

Note that the elements of
\mathscr{P}^{\prime}
are no longer, in general, cones.
We then observe:

Proposition 1.16
.

Suppose that all good divisors contained in
g^{-1}(0)
have multiplicity
one in
g^{-1}(0)
. Then
(B^{\prime},\mathscr{P}^{\prime})
is a polyhedral affine
pseudomanifold.

Proof.

As
g_{\mathrm{trop}}
is an affine submersion on
B_{0}
by
Proposition
1.15
,
B^{\prime}\cap B_{0}
acquires
an affine structure. Thus we just need to check that

(B^{\prime},\mathscr{P}^{\prime})
satisfies the conditions of
[
GHS
, Constr. 1.1]
.
Note that if
\rho\in\mathscr{P}
is a ray with
g_{\mathrm{trop}}|_{\rho}:\rho\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
surjective, the corresponding
divisor
X_{\rho}
is contained in
g^{-1}(0)
and is good, hence
appears with multiplicity one in
g^{-1}(0)
by assumption. Thus

g_{\mathrm{trop}}^{-1}(1)\cap\rho
is an integral point of
\rho
, so all elements
of
\mathscr{P}^{\prime}
are in fact lattice polytopes. Conditions (1)–(5)
of
[
GHS
, Constr. 1.1]
now follow immediately from the same
conditions for
\mathscr{P}
.
∎

Remark 1.17
.

In the above situation, given an MPL function
\varphi
on
(B,\mathscr{P})
,
we obtain by restricting representatives of an MPL function

\varphi|_{B^{\prime}}
. Note that if
\rho\in\mathscr{P}_{\mathrm{int}}^{[n-1]}
, then
the kink
\kappa_{\rho}
of
\varphi
at
\rho
then agrees with
the kink
\kappa_{\rho\cap B^{\prime}}
of
\varphi|_{B^{\prime}}
along
\rho\cap B^{\prime}
.
This follows immediately from the definition (
1.9
).

Remark 1.18
.

In
[
GHS
, §4.2]
, given a polyhedral affine pseudomanifold with
singularities
B
, we defined a polyhedral affine pseudomanifold

\mathbf{C}B
, the
cone
over
B
. In our current situation,

B
is a conical polyhedral affine pseudomanifold, and
B^{\prime}
as defined
above satisfies
\mathbf{C}B^{\prime}=B
. Here we find it more natural
to work with the cone.

We next consider a special case of the relative situation, satisfying
Assumption
1.2
, when
S
is an affine curve and

(X,D)\rightarrow S
is a degeneration of log Calabi-Yau varieties
which themselves satisfy Asssumption
1.1
.
Recall from Assumption
1.2
that
in the relative case, for
s\in S
a closed point,
s\not=0
,
we have
D_{j}\cap X_{s}
is an irreducible
divisor on
X_{s}
. We may then write

K_{X_{s}}+D_{s}\equiv_{\mathbb{Q}}\sum_{j}a_{j}(D_{j}\cap X_{s}).

Using this representative for the numerical equivalence class
of
K_{X_{s}}+D_{s}
, it then makes sense to ask that the pair

(X_{s},D_{s})
satisfy Assumption
1.1
. We
write
B_{(X_{s},D_{s})}
for the polyhedral affine pseudomanifold
associated to the pair
(X_{s},D_{s})
: this will be pure
(n-1)
-dimensional,
still with
n=\dim X
. We wish to understand the relationship between

B_{(X_{s},D_{s})}
and
B
, the polyhedral affine pseudomanifold
associated to the pair
(X,D)
. This is described by the following
proposition, which will be of use in
[
AG
]
.

Proposition 1.19
.

Assume we are in the relative case, with Assumption
1.2
holding.
Suppose that as described above, for

s\in S
a closed point,
s\not=0
,
(X_{s},D_{s})
satisfies
Assumption
1.1
.
Then:

(1)

\partial B=g_{\mathrm{trop}}^{-1}(0)
is naturally identified with the
polyhedral cone complex
B_{(X_{s},D_{s})}
.

(2)

The structure of integral affine manifold with boundary on

B_{0}
can be extended across the interiors of cells

\omega\in\mathscr{P}^{[n-2]}_{\partial}
. Further, the induced polyhedral affine
pseudomanifold structure on
\partial B
then agrees
with that determined by the pair
(X_{s},D_{s})
.

(3)

Let
\iota:H_{2}(X_{s})\rightarrow H_{2}(X)
be the map of groups
of curve classes induced by the inclusion of the fibre
X_{s}\hookrightarrow X
.
Then for each
\omega\in\mathscr{P}^{[n-2]}_{\partial}
, the
MPL function
\varphi
of Construction
1.14
has a
single-valued representative in
\mathrm{Star}(\omega)
. Further, the restriction
of this single-valued representative to
\mathrm{Star}(\omega)\cap\partial B

has kink
\iota([X_{\omega}\cap X_{s}])
. We write the MPL function
on
\partial B
with such kinks as
\varphi|_{\partial B}
.

Proof.

Step 1.

Proof of (1).

We call a stratum
Z
of
X

vertical
if
g|_{Z}
is constant
and otherwise say
Z
is
horizontal
.
Note that all vertical strata lie over
0\in S
. The rays of
\mathscr{P}

contained in
g_{\mathrm{trop}}^{-1}(0)
correspond precisely to the good
horizontal components of
D
. Thus there is a one-to-one
correspondence between cones
\rho\in\mathscr{P}
with
\rho\subseteq g_{\mathrm{trop}}^{-1}(0)
and good horizontal strata of
D
.
There is also a one-to-one correspondence
between horizontal strata of
X
and strata of
X_{s}
, taking a
horizontal stratum
Z
to the stratum
Z\cap X_{s}
.
Note that by Assumption
1.2
,(2), this is indeed an
irreducible stratum. Conversely, every stratum of
X_{s}
is necessarily
of this form by definition of
D_{s}
.
This correspondence takes good strata to good strata.
Thus
\mathscr{P}_{(X_{s},D_{s})}

may be identified with the set of cones of
\mathscr{P}
contained in

g_{\mathrm{trop}}^{-1}(0)
. Further, since
(X_{s},D_{s})
satisfies Assumptions

1.1
,
B_{(X_{s},D_{s})}
is pure
(n-1)
-dimensional, and hence
coincides with
\partial B
. This gives (1).

Step 2.

Analysis of horizontal good two-dimensional strata.

For (2), we need to give, for
\omega\in\mathscr{P}^{[n-2]}_{\partial}
,
a chart
\psi_{\omega}:\mathrm{Star}(\omega)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
which agrees, up to
elements of
\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{Z})
, with
\psi_{\rho}
on

\mathrm{Star}(\rho)\subseteq\mathrm{Star}(\omega)
whenever
\omega\subset\rho\in\mathscr{P}^{[n-1]}
.

To do so, we first analyze the geometry of the surface
X_{\omega}
.
As
X_{\omega}
is
horizontal, we obtain a non-constant map
g_{\omega}:X_{\omega}\rightarrow S
.
We first analyze
\partial X_{\omega}
. Since
X_{\omega}\cap X_{s}

is one-dimensional,
X_{\omega}\cap X_{s}
is disjoint from any bad
divisor on
X_{s}
by Assumptions
1.1
, (2). Hence

X_{\omega}
is disjoint from any horizontal bad divisor. On the
other hand,
X_{\omega}\cap X_{s}
necessarily contains precisely
two zero-dimensional strata, and hence
X_{\omega}
contains two
horizontal one-dimensional strata
X_{\rho},X_{\rho^{\prime}}
with

\omega\subseteq\rho,\rho^{\prime}
. These are the only horizontal one-dimensional
strata of
X_{\omega}
. Note they are necessarily sections
of
g_{\omega}
by Assumptions
1.2
,(2).
However, since
\partial^{\mathrm{good}}X_{\omega}
is
connected, there must be a chain of vertical good
one-dimensional strata connecting

X_{\rho}
and
X_{\rho^{\prime}}
. Again, by Assumptions
1.1
,
(2), these are disjoint from any bad divisor.
Now the union of vertical strata coincides with

g_{\omega}^{-1}(0)
and

g^{-1}_{\omega}(0)

is connected (again by Assumption
1.2
,(2)),
so it follows that all one-dimensional strata of

X_{\omega}
are disjoint from bad divisors. Thus
X_{\omega}

is disjoint from all bad divisors.

By Proposition
1.3
,
each vertical one-dimensional
stratum in
X_{\omega}
, being good, contains precisely two
zero-dimensional strata. On the other hand,
g_{\omega}^{-1}(0)

contains two zero-dimensional strata
given by
X_{\rho}\cap g_{\omega}^{-1}(0)
and
X_{\rho^{\prime}}\cap g_{\omega}^{-1}(0)
, and all other zero-dimensional strata
of
X_{\omega}
are then intersections of two good vertical one-dimensional
strata. Thus the only possibility for the set of one-dimensional strata
of
X_{\omega}
is as follows: this set of strata
may be written as
\{C_{0},\ldots,C_{r}\}
, with
C_{0}=X_{\rho}
,

C_{r}=X_{\rho^{\prime}}
,
C_{1},\ldots,C_{r-1}
vertical strata, and with

C_{i}\cap C_{j}\not=\emptyset
if and only if
|i-j|\leq 1
. Further,

C_{i}\cap C_{i+1}
consists of one point. See Figure
1.2
.

Figure 1.2.
The two-dimensional stratum
X_{\omega}
.

Now let
(B_{\omega},\mathscr{P}_{\omega})
be the two-dimensional
polyhedral affine pseudomanifold
associated to the log Calabi-Yau variety
(X_{\omega},\partial X_{\omega})

defined over
S
.
We will describe
B_{\omega}
and
\mathscr{P}_{\omega}
explicitly.
Let
\tau_{0},\ldots,\tau_{r}
be the rays of

\mathscr{P}_{\omega}
corresponding to
C_{0},\ldots,C_{r}
. Now define

n_{\tau_{0}}=(0,1)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
,
n_{\tau_{1}}=(1,0)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, and inductively
obtain
n_{\tau_{2}},\ldots,n_{\tau_{r}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
by requiring that

(1.10)

n_{\tau_{\ell-1}}+n_{\tau_{\ell+1}}=-C_{\ell}^{2}n_{\tau_{\ell}},\quad 1\leq\ell\leq r-1.

We may define a coordinate chart

\psi_{\tau_{\ell}}:\mathrm{Star}(\tau_{\ell})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{2}
by mapping primitive
generators of
\tau_{\ell-1},\tau_{\ell}
and
\tau_{\ell+1}
to

n_{\tau_{\ell-1}},n_{\tau_{\ell}}
and
n_{\tau_{\ell+1}}
respectively.
By (
1.6
), this coordinate chart is
compatible with the affine structure on
B_{\omega}\setminus\{0\}
.
Indeed, in (
1.6
), we have

D_{i_{j}}=X_{\tau_{\ell}}=C_{\ell}
.

Now let
g_{\omega,\mathrm{trop}}:B_{\omega}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}

be the tropicalization of
X_{\omega}\rightarrow S
.
Note that
g_{\omega,\mathrm{trop}}|_{\mathrm{Star}(\tau_{1})}\circ\psi^{-1}_{\tau_{1}}

is given by
(1,0)\in(\mathbb{Z}^{2})^{*}
. Indeed, as
C_{0}
is not contained
in
g^{-1}(0)
,
g_{\omega,\mathrm{trop}}
vanishes on
C_{0}
. On the other hand,
the multiplicity of
C_{1}
in
g_{\omega}^{-1}(0)
must be
1
, as
C_{0}\cdot g_{\omega}^{-1}(0)=1
,
C_{0}
being a section. Thus
g_{\omega,\mathrm{trop}}

takes the value
1
on the primitive generator of
\tau_{1}
.

Since
\psi_{\tau_{\ell}}
and
\psi_{\tau_{\ell+1}}
agree on

\mathrm{Star}(\tau_{\ell})\cap\mathrm{Star}(\tau_{\ell+1})
, in fact
g_{\omega,\mathrm{trop}}
is
given by
(0,1)
on all charts. Since
g_{\omega,\mathrm{trop}}
is positive
on generators of
\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{r-1}
and takes the value
0

on
\tau_{r}
, we see that all
n_{\tau_{\ell}}
lie in
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times\mathbb{R}
and
n_{\tau_{r}}=(0,-1)
.
Taking
\Sigma_{\omega}
to be the fan in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
with one-dimensional
cones generated by
n_{\tau_{\ell}}
,
0\leq\ell\leq r
and two-dimensional cones
spanned by
n_{\tau_{\ell}},n_{\tau_{\ell+1}}
,
0\leq\ell\leq r-1
, we see
that the charts
\psi_{\rho_{\ell}}
glue to give an identification of

B_{\omega}
with
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times\mathbb{R}
and
\mathscr{P}_{\omega}
with

\Sigma_{\omega}
, so that
B_{\omega}\setminus\{0\}
is
affine isomorphic to
(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times\mathbb{R})\setminus\{(0,0)\}
.
Further, under this identification,
g_{\omega,\mathrm{trop}}
is given by
projection onto
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
.

Step 3.

A chart on
\mathrm{Star}(\omega)
.

We may now build a chart for
\mathrm{Star}(\omega)
as follows. Let

\omega
be generated by
D_{i_{1}}^{*},\ldots,D_{i_{n-2}}^{*}
, and let

\psi_{j}
,
1\leq j\leq n-2
, be the
\mathbb{R}
-valued piecewise linear function on

B_{\omega}
with kink along
\tau_{\ell}
being
D_{i_{j}}\cdot C_{\ell}
.
Explicitly, such
\psi_{j}
may be constructed by having it take the
value
0
on some choice of two-dimensional cone in
\mathscr{P}_{\omega}
,
and then using the relation

(1.11)

\psi_{j}(n_{\tau_{\ell-1}})+\psi_{j}(n_{\tau_{\ell+1}})=D_{i_{j}}\cdot C_{\ell}-C_{\ell}^{2}\psi_{j}(n_{\tau_{\ell}})

to determine the value of
\psi_{j}
on
n_{\tau_{\ell+1}}
inductively.
Indeed, the reader may easily check that the function defined on

\mathrm{Star}(\tau_{\ell})
satisfying (
1.11
) has kink
D_{i_{j}}\cdot C_{\ell}
using (
1.9
) and (
1.10
).

Let
\rho_{0},\ldots,\rho_{r}
be the codimension one cones containing

\omega
, with
X_{\rho_{\ell}}=C_{\ell}
, and let
D_{k_{\ell}}^{*}
be such that

\omega
and
D_{k_{\ell}}^{*}
generate
\rho_{\ell}
,
0\leq\ell\leq r
.
We may then define

\psi_{\omega}:\mathrm{Star}(\omega)\rightarrow B_{\omega}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-2}
via, with
e_{1},\ldots,e_{n-2}

the standard basis for
\mathbb{R}^{n-2}
,

\psi_{\omega}(D_{i_{j}}^{*})=(0,e_{j}),\quad\quad 1\leq j\leq n-2

and

(1.12)

\psi_{\omega}(D_{k_{\ell}}^{*})=\left(n_{\tau_{\ell}},-\sum_{j=1}^{n-2}\psi_{j}(n_{\tau_{\ell}})e_{j}\right),\quad\quad 0\leq\ell\leq r.

We then extend
\psi_{\omega}
linearly on each cone containing
\omega
.
It is easy to see that
\psi_{\omega}
induces an embedding of

\mathrm{Star}(\omega)
into
B_{\omega}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-2}
. It is thus sufficient
to check that
\psi_{\omega}
is compatible with each
\psi_{\rho_{\ell}}
,

1\leq\ell\leq r-1
, as defined in (
1.5
) and
(
1.6
).
For this, it is enough to verify the equality
(
1.6
). We have for
1\leq\ell\leq r-1
,

\displaystyle\psi_{\omega}(D_{k_{\ell-1}}^{*})+\psi_{\omega}(D_{k_{\ell+1}}^{*})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:psi omega def}}}{{=}}{}

\displaystyle\left(n_{\tau_{\ell-1}}+n_{\tau_{\ell+1}},-\sum_{j=1}^{n-2}(\psi_{j}(n_{\tau_{\ell-1}})+\psi_{j}(n_{\tau_{\ell+1}}))e_{j}\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:ntau relation},\eqref{eq:psij}}}{{=}}{}

\displaystyle\left(-C_{\ell}^{2}n_{\tau_{\ell}},-\sum_{j=1}^{n-2}(D_{i_{j}}\cdot C_{\ell}-C_{\ell}^{2}\psi_{j}(n_{\tau_{\ell}}))e_{j}\right)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle-(D_{k_{\ell}}\cdot X_{\rho_{\ell}})\psi_{\omega}(D_{k_{\ell}}^{*})-\sum_{j=1}^{n-2}(D_{i_{j}}\cdot X_{\rho_{\ell}})\psi_{\omega}(D_{i_{j}}^{*}),

the last equality by
C_{\ell}^{2}=D_{k_{\ell}}\cdot X_{\rho_{\ell}}
.
This verifies (
1.6
),
as desired. Thus we obtain an affine chart on
\mathrm{Star}(\omega)

compatible with the affine structure on
B_{0}
.

Step 4.

Restricting the affine structure on
\mathrm{Star}(\omega)

to
\partial B
.

To complete the proof of (2), we compare the restriction of this chart
to
\mathrm{Star}(\omega)\cap\partial B
with the corresponding chart
for
\partial B
arising from the pair
(X_{s},D_{s})
. It is enough
to verify that

(1.13)

\psi_{\omega}(D_{k_{0}}^{*})+\psi_{\omega}(D_{k_{r}}^{*})=-\sum_{j=1}^{n-2}\big((D_{i_{j}}\cap X_{s})\cdot(X_{\omega}\cap X_{s})\big)\psi_{\omega}(D_{i_{j}}^{*}),

where the intersection number is calculated on
X_{s}
.
We first note that

(1.14)

\psi_{j}(n_{\tau_{0}})+\psi_{j}(n_{\tau_{r}})=D_{i_{j}}\cdot(X_{\omega}\cap g^{-1}(0))=D_{i_{j}}\cdot(X_{\omega}\cap X_{s}).

Indeed, let
\sigma_{\ell,\ell+1}\in\mathscr{P}_{\omega}
be the two-dimensional
cone with boundary rays
\tau_{\ell}
and
\tau_{\ell+1}
, and let

n_{\tau_{\ell}}=(\alpha_{\ell},\beta_{\ell})
. Then by the definition of kink
(
1.9
),
for
1\leq\ell\leq r-1
,

(1.15)

(d\psi_{j}|_{\sigma_{\ell,\ell+1}})(0,-1)+(d\psi_{j}|_{\sigma_{\ell-1,\ell}})(0,1)=\alpha_{\ell}(D_{i_{j}}\cdot X_{\rho_{\ell}}).

Since
\alpha_{\ell}
is the multiplicity of
D_{k_{\ell}}
in

g^{-1}(0)
by definition of
g_{\mathrm{trop}}
, summing (
1.15
)
for
1\leq\ell\leq r-1
gives (
1.14
).
Thus we obtain

\displaystyle\psi_{\omega}(D_{k_{0}}^{*})+\psi_{\omega}(D_{k_{r}}^{*})={}

\displaystyle\left(n_{\tau_{0}}+n_{\tau_{r}},-\sum_{j=1}^{n-2}\big(\psi_{j}(n_{\tau_{0}})+\psi_{j}(n_{\tau_{r}})\big)e_{j}\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:big kink}}}{{=}}{}

\displaystyle\left(0,-\sum_{j=1}^{n-2}\big((D_{i_{j}}\cap X_{s})\cdot(X_{\omega}\cap D_{s})\big)e_{j}\right).

Noting that
\psi_{\omega}(D_{i_{j}}^{*})=(0,e_{j})
, this
gives (
1.13
).

Step 5.

Proof of (3).

For (3), note that we may obtain such a single-valued representative
by constructing a single-valued piecewise linear function

\bar{\varphi}_{\omega}:B_{\omega}\rightarrow Q^{{\operatorname{gp}}}_{\mathbb{R}}
with kink

[X_{\rho_{\ell}}]
along
\tau_{\ell}
, just as we constructed

\psi_{j}
as above. Let
\operatorname{pr}_{1}:B_{\omega}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-2}\rightarrow B_{\omega}

be the first projection. We then obtain a single-valued function

\varphi_{\omega}=\bar{\varphi}_{\omega}\circ\operatorname{pr}_{1}\circ\psi_{\omega}
on

\mathrm{Star}(\omega)
with the correct kinks along the
\rho_{\ell}
.
The kink of the restriction of
\varphi_{\omega}
to

\mathrm{Star}(\omega)\cap\partial B
is then easily calculated
as
\bar{\varphi}_{\omega}(n_{\tau_{0}})+\bar{\varphi}_{\omega}(n_{\tau_{r}})=[X_{\omega}\cap g^{-1}(0)]
as before, and the latter coincides with

\iota([X_{\omega}\cap X_{s}])
, as desired.
∎

2.
Punctured log maps and tropical geometry

Throughout this section we will assume given
g:X\rightarrow S

satisfying Assumptions
1.1
and
1.2

in the absolute and relative cases without further comment.

2.1.
Review of notation

We briefly review notation from
[
ACGS1
,
ACGS2
]
for tropical maps
to
\Sigma(X)
and punctured maps to
X
. We first summarize the tropical
language as developed in
[
ACGS1
, §2.5]
and
[
ACGS2
, §2.2]
.

In what follows,
\mathbf{Cones}
denotes the category of
rational polyhedral cones with integral structure, i.e., objects
are rational polyhedral cones
\omega\subseteq\Lambda_{\omega}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{R}
for

\Lambda_{\omega}

the lattice of integral tangent vectors
6
6

6

In

[
ACGS1
,
ACGS2
]
, we write this lattice
as
N_{\omega}
; here we use the notation
\Lambda_{\omega}
to fit the
notation of
[
GHS
]
.

to
\omega
.
Morphisms are maps of cones induced by maps of the corresponding lattices.
We write
\omega_{\mathbb{Z}}=\omega\cap\Lambda_{\omega}
for the set of integral
points of
\omega
.

We consider graphs
G
, with sets of vertices
V(G)
, edges
E(G)
and legs

L(G)
. In what follows, we will frequently confuse
G
with its topological
realisation
|G|
. Legs will correspond to marked or punctured points of
punctured curves, and are rays in the marked case and compact line segments in
the punctured case. We view a compact leg as having only one vertex. An abstract
tropical curve over
\omega\in\mathbf{Cones}
is data
(G,{\mathbf{g}},\ell)

where
{\mathbf{g}}:V(G)\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
is a genus function and

\ell:E(G)\rightarrow\operatorname{Hom}(\omega_{\mathbb{Z}},\mathbb{N})\setminus\{0\}
determines edge
lengths. Since this paper will only deal with genus
0
curves, we omit

\mathbf{g}
entirely from the sequel.

Associated to the data

(G,\ell)
is a generalized cone complex (a diagram in the
category of
\mathbf{Cones}
with all morphisms being inclusions
of faces induced by a morphism of corresponding lattices)

\Gamma(G,\ell)
along with a morphism of cone complexes

\Gamma(G,\ell)\rightarrow\omega
with fibre over
s\in\operatorname{Int}(\omega)

being a tropical curve, i.e., a metric graph, with underlying graph

G
and affine edge length of
E\in E(G)
being
\ell(E)(s)\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
.
Associated to each vertex
v\in V(G)

of
G
is a copy
\omega_{v}
of
\omega

in
\Gamma(G,\ell)
. Associated to each edge or leg
E\in E(G)\cup L(G)

is a cone
\omega_{E}\in\Gamma(G,\ell)
with

\omega_{E}\subseteq\omega\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
and the map to
\omega

given by projection onto the first coordinate. This projection
fibres
\omega_{E}
in compact intervals or rays over
\omega

(rays for legs representing a marked point).

A
family of tropical maps

to
\Sigma(X)
over
\omega\in\mathbf{Cones}
is a morphism of cone complexes

h:\Gamma(G,\ell)\rightarrow\Sigma(X).

If
s\in\operatorname{Int}(\omega)
, we may view
G
as the fibre of

\Gamma(G,\ell)\rightarrow\omega
over
s
as a metric graph, and
write

h_{s}:G\rightarrow\Sigma(X)

for the corresponding tropical map with domain
G
.
The
type
of such a family consists of the data

\tau:=(G,{\boldsymbol{\sigma}},\mathbf{u})
where

{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}:V(G)\cup E(G)\cup L(G)\rightarrow\Sigma(X)

associates to
x\in V(G)\cup E(G)\cup L(G)
the minimal
cone of
\Sigma(X)
containing
h(\omega_{x})
. Further,

\mathbf{u}
associates to each (oriented) edge or leg
E\in E(G)\cup L(G)

the corresponding
contact order

\mathbf{u}(E)\in\Lambda_{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(E)}
,
the image of the tangent vector
(0,1)\in\Lambda_{\omega_{E}}=\Lambda_{\omega}\oplus\mathbb{Z}
under the map
h
.

As we shall only consider tropicalizations of
pre-stable punctured curves (see
[
ACGS2
, Def. 2.5]
,
following
[
ACGS2
, Prop. 2.21]
we may assume that for
L\in L(G)

with adjacent vertex
v\in V(G)

giving
\omega_{L},\omega_{v}\in\Gamma(G,\ell)
, we have

(2.1)

h(\omega_{L})=(h(\omega_{v})+\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}{\bf u}(L))\cap{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(L)\subseteq\Lambda_{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(L),\mathbb{R}}.

A
decorated type
is data
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}=(G,{\boldsymbol{\sigma}},\mathbf{u},\mathbf{A})

where
\mathbf{A}:V(G)\rightarrow H_{2}(X)
associates a curve class to
each vertex of
G
. The
total curve class
of
{\bf A}

is
A=\sum_{v\in V(G)}{\bf A}(v)
.

We also have a notion of a contraction morphism of types

\phi:\tau\rightarrow\tau^{\prime}
, see
[
ACGS1
, Def. 2.24]
. This
is a contraction of edges on the underlying graphs, and the
additional data satisfies some relations as follows.
If
x\in V(G)\cup E(G)\cup L(G)
, then
{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{\prime}(\phi(x))\subseteq{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(x)
(if
x
is an edge, it may be contracted
to a vertex by
\phi
). Further, if
E\in E(G)\cup L(G)

then
\mathbf{u}(E)=\mathbf{u}^{\prime}(\phi(E))
under the inclusion

\Lambda_{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{\prime}(\phi(E))}\subseteq\Lambda_{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(E)}
, provided that
E
is
not an edge contracted by
\phi
.

We also recall the notion of
global contact order
,

[
ACGS2
, Def. 2.29]
. If
\sigma\in\Sigma(X)
,
we set

\mathfrak{C}_{\sigma}(X):=\operatorname{colim}_{\sigma\subseteq\sigma^{\prime}}\Lambda_{\sigma^{\prime}},

where the colimit is taken in the category of sets,
and define

\mathfrak{C}(X):=\coprod_{\sigma\in\Sigma(X)}\mathfrak{C}_{\sigma}(X).

A
global type of tropical or punctured map
is then

\bar{\tau}=(G,{\boldsymbol{\sigma}},\overline{\mathbf{u}})
, where for each

E\in E(G)\cup L(G)
,
\overline{\mathbf{u}}(E)\in\mathfrak{C}_{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(E)}(X)
.
Note that a type
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}=(G,{\boldsymbol{\sigma}},\mathbf{u})

determines a global type by replacing
\mathbf{u}(E)
with
its image under the natural map
\Lambda_{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(E)}\rightarrow\mathfrak{C}_{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(E)}(X)
.

We say a type (or global type)
\tau
is
realizable
if there exists a
family of tropical maps to
\Sigma(X)
of type (or global type)
\tau
.
We also say
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}=(\tau,{\bf A})
is realizable if
\tau
is
realizable. We note that if a global type
\bar{\tau}
is realizable, there is
only one type
\tau
of tropical map realizing it (see

[
ACGS2
, Lem. 3.5]
). Most of the time in this paper, we will only
deal with realizable types, and hence we will then not distinguish
between a type and a global type in this case.

If a type
\tau
is realizable, then there is a universal family of
tropical maps of type
\tau
, parameterized by an object of

\mathbf{Cones}
. Hopefully without confusion, we will generally write
this cone as
\tau
. Hence we have a cone complex
\Gamma(G,\ell)
equipped
with a map to
\tau
and a map of cone complexes
h=h_{\tau}:\Gamma(G,\ell)\rightarrow\Sigma(X)
. Generally we write
h
rather than
h_{\tau}

when unambiguous. Note that for each
x\in E(G)\cup L(G)\cup V(G)
,
we thus obtain
\tau_{x}\in\Gamma(G,\ell)
the corresponding cone.

We write
\mathcal{X}
for the Artin fan of
X
, see

[
ACMW
]
, as well as
[
ACGS1
, §2.2]
for a summary.
We emphasize here that we always mean the absolute Artin fan,
denoted as
\mathcal{A}_{X}
in
[
ACGS2
]
, even when we are working in the
relative situation.

We refer to
[
ACGS2
, Defs. 2.10, 2.13, 2.14]
for the notion of a family

\pi:C^{\circ}\rightarrow W
of punctured curves and pre-stable or
stable punctured maps
f:C^{\circ}/W\rightarrow X
or

f:C^{\circ}/W\rightarrow\mathcal{X}
. For the most part in this paper, we
work with punctured maps with target
X
defined over
\operatorname{Spec}\Bbbk
,
and in the relative case, only briefly work with punctured maps
defined over
S
.

Given a punctured map with domain
C^{\circ}\rightarrow W
and

W=\operatorname{Spec}(Q\rightarrow\kappa)
for
\kappa
an algebraically closed
field and target
X
or
\mathcal{X}
, we obtain by
functoriality of tropicalizations a family of tropical maps

(2.2)

\textstyle{\Sigma(C)=\Gamma(G,\ell)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Sigma(X)}
\textstyle{\Sigma(W)=\omega=Q^{\vee}_{\mathbb{R}}}

parameterized by
W
.
The
type
of the punctured map is then the type

\tau=(G,{\boldsymbol{\sigma}},\mathbf{u})

of this family of tropical maps. We recall that the punctured
map
f:C^{\circ}/W\rightarrow X
is
basic
if (
2.2
)
is the universal family of tropical maps of type
\tau
.

Given a decorated global type
\bar{\boldsymbol{\tau}}
,
[
ACGS2
, Def. 3.7]

defines the notion of a marking of a punctured map by
\bar{\boldsymbol{\tau}}
.
In particular, this gives rise to moduli spaces

\mathscr{M}(X,\bar{\boldsymbol{\tau}})
(resp.
\mathfrak{M}(\mathcal{X},\bar{\boldsymbol{\tau}})
)
of stable (resp. pre-stable)
\bar{\boldsymbol{\tau}}
-marked punctured maps
to
X
(resp.
\mathcal{X}
). When we are in the relative situation and
wish to work with punctured maps to
X
defined over
S
, then
we write
\mathscr{M}(X/S,\bar{\boldsymbol{\tau}})
for the corresponding moduli space.
We note that while curve classes in

\mathcal{X}
are meaningless, the decoration on
\bar{\boldsymbol{\tau}}
affects the
notion of isomorphism in the category
\mathfrak{M}(\mathcal{X},\bar{\boldsymbol{\tau}})
.
By
[
ACGS2
, Thm. 3.10]
,
these are algebraic stacks,
\mathscr{M}(X,\bar{\boldsymbol{\tau}})
is Deligne-Mumford
and both stacks carry natural log structures, namely the basic
log structure.
Further, there is a natural morphism

(2.3)

\varepsilon:\mathscr{M}(X,\bar{\boldsymbol{\tau}})\rightarrow\mathfrak{M}(\mathcal{X},\bar{\boldsymbol{\tau}})

given by composing a punctured map
C^{\circ}\rightarrow X
with the
canonical map
X\rightarrow\mathcal{X}
.
[
ACGS2
, §4]
then gives a perfect
relative obstruction theory for
\varepsilon
. Most importantly, while the
stacks
