\mathfrak{M}(\mathcal{X},\bar{\boldsymbol{\tau}})
may be quite poorly behaved globally, in fact they
have a simple local structure coming from the fact that they are idealized log
smooth over
S
, see
[
ACGS2
, Thm. 3.24, Rem. 3.25]
.

We sometimes work with moduli spaces
\mathfrak{M}(\mathcal{X},\bar{\tau})
of
pre-stable
\bar{\tau}
-marked punctured maps to
\mathcal{X}
, forgetting the
decorations. The forgetful morphism
\mathfrak{M}(\mathcal{X},\bar{\boldsymbol{\tau}})\rightarrow\mathfrak{M}(\mathcal{X},\bar{\tau})
is strict étale, and we may also write

\varepsilon:\mathscr{M}(X,\bar{\boldsymbol{\tau}})\rightarrow\mathfrak{M}(\mathcal{X},\bar{\tau})

for the composition of (
2.3
) with this forgetful
morphism.

If there is a contraction morphism between decorated global types

\phi:\bar{\boldsymbol{\tau}}\rightarrow\bar{\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{\prime}
, we obtain a forgetful
map
\mathscr{M}(X,\bar{\boldsymbol{\tau}})\rightarrow\mathscr{M}(X,\bar{\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{\prime})
.
This gives rise to a stratified description of these moduli spaces,
see
[
ACGS2
, Rem. 3.29]
.

There is one special choice of decorated type which imposes the minimal
number of conditions on the curve. We always write
\beta

for a
class of punctured map
, which is a type where the
underlying graph
G
has only one vertex and no edges, and

{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}
takes the vertex and every leg to
\{0\}\in\Sigma(X)
.
Put another way, a class of punctured map is the data

\beta=(\overline{\mathbf{u}},A)

where
\overline{\mathbf{u}}
is a collection of
global contact orders
\{\bar{u}_{i}\}\subseteq\mathfrak{C}_{0}(X)
and
A\in H_{2}(X)

is a curve class. Given a decorated global type
\bar{\boldsymbol{\tau}}
,
there is always a unique choice of punctured curve class
\beta

with a contraction morphism
\bar{\boldsymbol{\tau}}\rightarrow\beta
.
Here
A
is the total curve class of
{\bf A}
if

\bar{\boldsymbol{\tau}}=(G,{\boldsymbol{\sigma}},\bar{\mathbf{u}},\mathbf{A})
. In this case
we say the decorated type
\bar{\boldsymbol{\tau}}
is
of class

\beta
, and there
is a canonical morphism
\mathscr{M}(X,\bar{\boldsymbol{\tau}})\rightarrow\mathscr{M}(X,\beta)
,
and an analogous morphism for maps to Artin fans. The moduli
space
\mathscr{M}(X,\beta)
is the moduli space of punctured maps where
only the curve class and the contact orders of the marked and punctured
points are fixed.

Suppose now given
\tau
a realizable type equipped with a contraction morphism

\tau\rightarrow\tau^{\prime}
to a (not necessarily realizable) global type
\tau^{\prime}
,
and let
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{\prime}
be a decoration of
\tau^{\prime}
. As mentioned earlier, we do not
distinguish between the type
\tau
and its corresponding global type. We denote
by
\mathfrak{M}_{\tau}(\mathcal{X},\tau^{\prime})
the reduced closed substack of
\mathfrak{M}(\mathcal{X},\tau^{\prime})

whose underlying closed subspace is the closure of the locus of geometric points
whose corresponding punctured map is of type
\tau
. We may then define

\mathscr{M}_{\tau}(X,{\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{\prime}):=\mathscr{M}(X,{\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{\prime})\times_{\mathfrak{M}(\mathcal{X},\tau^{\prime})}\mathfrak{M}_{\tau}(\mathcal{X},\tau^{\prime}).

While
\mathfrak{M}_{\tau}(\mathcal{X},\tau^{\prime})
, assuming non-empty, always carries points
corresponding to curves of type
\tau
, this may not be the case for

\mathscr{M}_{\tau}(X,{\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{\prime})
. Nevertheless, the type
\tau^{\prime\prime}
of any curve over a
geometric point in
\mathfrak{M}_{\tau}(\mathcal{X},\tau^{\prime})
always has a (not necessarily
unique) contraction map
\tau^{\prime\prime}\rightarrow\tau
. We note that provided

\mathfrak{M}_{\tau}(\mathcal{X},\tau^{\prime})
is non-empty, there is then a canonical morphism

\mathfrak{M}(\mathcal{X},\tau)\rightarrow\mathfrak{M}_{\tau}(\mathcal{X},\tau^{\prime})

of degree
|\operatorname{Aut}(\tau/\tau^{\prime})|
. Here
\operatorname{Aut}(\tau/\tau^{\prime})
denotes the
group of automorphisms of the global type
\tau
compatible
with the contraction map
\tau\rightarrow\tau^{\prime}
, i.e.,
automorphisms of the underlying graph
G
preserving

{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}
and
\bar{\bf u}
and the contraction map.

If
\bar{\boldsymbol{\tau}}=(G,{\boldsymbol{\sigma}},\bar{\bf u},\mathbf{A})
denotes a
choice of global type, and

I\subseteq E(G)\cup L(G)
is a collection of edges and legs, then we write

\mathfrak{M}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}(\mathcal{X},\bar{\boldsymbol{\tau}})=\mathfrak{M}^{{\operatorname{ev}}(I)}(\mathcal{X},\bar{\boldsymbol{\tau}}):=\mathfrak{M}(\mathcal{X},\bar{\boldsymbol{\tau}})\times_{\underline{\mathcal{X}}^{I}}\underline{X}^{I}.

Here
\underline{\mathcal{X}}^{I}
denotes the product of
\#I
copies of
\underline{\mathcal{X}}

over
\underline{S}
, and similarly
\underline{X}^{I}
; the morphism

\mathfrak{M}(\mathcal{X},\bar{\boldsymbol{\tau}})\rightarrow\underline{\mathcal{X}}^{I}
is given by evaluation
at the nodes and punctured points indexed by elements of
I
,
and
\underline{X}^{I}\rightarrow\underline{\mathcal{X}}^{I}
is induced by the canonical smooth
map
\underline{X}\rightarrow\underline{\mathcal{X}}
. The map
\varepsilon
then factors
as

\textstyle{\mathscr{M}(X,\bar{\boldsymbol{\tau}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon^{{\operatorname{ev}}}}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{M}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}(\mathcal{X},\bar{\boldsymbol{\tau}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{M}(\mathcal{X},\bar{\boldsymbol{\tau}}).}

The second morphism is smooth, while
\varepsilon^{{\operatorname{ev}}}
also possesses
a relative obstruction theory compatible with the morphism

\varepsilon
of (
2.3
), see

[
ACGS2
, §4.2]
.

2.2.
Some tropical lemmas

We first observe the following result, which explains why the choice
of affine structure on
B
given in §
1.3

is the correct one.

Lemma 2.1
.

Let
f:C^{\circ}/W\rightarrow X
be a stable punctured map to
X
,
with
W=\operatorname{Spec}(Q\rightarrow\kappa)
a geometric log point. For

s\in\operatorname{Int}(Q^{\vee}_{\mathbb{R}})
, let
h_{s}:G\rightarrow\Sigma(X)
be
the corresponding tropical map. If
v\in V(G)
satisfies

h_{s}(v)\in B_{0}
, then
h_{s}
satisfies the balancing condition
at
v
. More precisely, if
E_{1},\ldots,E_{n}
are the legs
and edges adjacent to
v
, oriented away from
v
,
then the contact orders
\mathbf{u}(E_{i})
may be interpreted
as elements of
\Lambda_{h_{s}(v)}
, the stalk of the
local system
\Lambda
of Definition
1.13
at

h_{s}(v)
. In this group, the balancing condition

(2.4)

\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathbf{u}(E_{i})=0

is satisfied.
Further, in the relative case, the composition
\Sigma(g)\circ h_{s}:G\rightarrow\Sigma(S)=\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
is a balanced tropical map.

Proof.

By definition of
\Delta
,
h_{s}(v)\in B_{0}
implies
that
h_{s}(v)\in\operatorname{Int}(\sigma)\in\mathscr{P}
for
\sigma
of codimension
0

or codimension
1
. If
\sigma\in\mathscr{P}_{{\operatorname{max}}}
, then
necessarily
{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(E_{i})=\sigma
and
\mathbf{u}(E_{i})\in\Lambda_{\sigma}
is
a tangent vector to
\sigma
, and hence can be viewed
as an element of
\Lambda_{h_{s}(v)}
. Necessarily

f
contracts the corresponding component
C_{v}
of
C

to the zero-dimensional stratum
X_{\sigma}
, and hence
the balancing condition holds from
[
ACGS2
, Prop. 2.25]
,
keeping in mind that
\tau_{x}
given in the statement of that proposition
vanishes because
C_{v}
is contracted.

If instead
\sigma\in\mathscr{P}^{[n-1]}
, note that by Assumptions
1.1
,
(2),
\sigma
is not contained in any cone
of
\Sigma(X)
which is not also in
\mathscr{P}
.
Noting also that as
{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(E_{i})
necessarily contains
\sigma
,
we must have
{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(E_{i})\in\mathscr{P}
. In particular,

\mathbf{u}(E_{i})\in\Lambda_{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(E_{i})}

may then be viewed, via parallel transport in
\Lambda
, as a tangent
vector in
\Lambda_{h_{s}(v)}
.

We may now split the punctured map
f
at all nodes of

C
contained in
C_{v}
, and obtain by restriction a punctured
log map
f_{v}:C^{\circ}_{v}\rightarrow X
by
[
ACGS2
, Prop. 5.2]
.
Note that the dual graph of
C^{\circ}_{v}
consists of a single vertex
with legs
E_{1},\ldots,E_{m}
. Further, the contact order of
f_{v}

at the puncture corresponding to
E_{i}
agrees with the
contact order
\mathbf{u}(E_{i})
for the map
f
of the corresponding edge or
leg of
G
, oriented away from
v
.
It is thus sufficient to show balancing for the
tropical map induced by
f_{v}
.

Note
f_{v}
factors through the strict
morphism
X_{\sigma}\hookrightarrow X
. Moreover, by Lemma

1.10
,
X_{\sigma}
is isomorphic to a stratum
of the toric variety
X_{\Sigma_{\sigma}}
, and hence we obtain
a punctured map
f_{v}:C_{v}^{\circ}\rightarrow X_{\Sigma_{\sigma}}
.
However, tropicalizations of punctured maps to toric varieties
are always balanced when viewed as maps to the corresponding
fans, see
[
ACGS2
, Rem. 2.26]
.
The claimed balancing then follows from the construction of the
affine chart
\psi_{\sigma}
.

Finally, in the relative case, necessarily the underlying map

\underline{g}\circ\underline{f}
is constant on irreducible components of
C

since
S
is assumed to be affine. Thus balancing of

\Sigma(g)\circ h_{s}
holds again from
[
ACGS2
, Prop. 2.25]
.
∎

Note that if
\tau=(G,{\boldsymbol{\sigma}},\mathbf{u})
is a type of tropical map
to
\Sigma(X)
, the balancing condition of Lemma
2.1

still makes sense at vertices mapping to
B_{0}
. Indeed, for
v\in V(G)
with
{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(v)
a codimension
zero or one cone of
\mathscr{P}
, we may choose any point
x\in\operatorname{Int}({\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(v))

and (
2.4
) makes sense inside
\Lambda_{x}
. Further, in the
relative case, by composing with
\Sigma(g)
,
\tau
yields a type of
tropical map to
\Sigma(S)=\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, and again it makes sense
to ask that such a type be balanced.

Thus we define:

Definition 2.2
.

A type
\tau
of tropical map to
\Sigma(X)
is
balanced
if:

(1)

For each
v\in V(G)
with
{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(v)\in\mathscr{P}
a codimension zero or one
cone, the balancing condition (
2.4
) holds at
v
.

(2)

In the relative case,
\tau
induces a type of a balanced tropical
map to
\Sigma(S)
.

The following observation shows that certain degree data of maps are
determined by tropical data:

Lemma 2.3
.

Let
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}=(\tau,\mathbf{A})
be a decorated type,
and suppose
\mathscr{M}(X,{\boldsymbol{\tau}})
is non-empty. Then:

(1)

For each
v\in V(G)

with
{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(v)\in\mathscr{P}_{{\operatorname{max}}}
, we have

\mathbf{A}(v)=0
.

(2)

Assume
v\in V(G)
with

\rho={\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(v)\in\mathscr{P}^{[n-1]}_{\mathrm{int}}

contained in
\sigma\in\mathscr{P}_{{\operatorname{max}}}
. Let
E_{1},\ldots,E_{r}
be the edges
adjacent to
v
with
{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(E_{i})=\sigma
for
1\leq i\leq r
. Let

\delta:\Lambda_{\sigma}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
be the quotient map of

\Lambda_{\sigma}
by
\Lambda_{\rho}
positive on tangent vectors pointing from

\rho
into
\sigma
. Then
\mathbf{A}(v)=d[X_{\rho}]
, where

d=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\delta(\mathbf{u}(E_{i})).

We note that this number is independent of the choice of
\sigma
containing

\rho
by the balancing condition.

Proof.

By assumption there exists a punctured map

f:C^{\circ}/W\rightarrow X
over
a geometric log point
W
marked by
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}
. Thus for
a vertex
v\in V(G)
, we obtain via splitting as in the proof of
Lemma
2.1
a subcurve
C^{\circ}_{v}
and a punctured
map
f_{v}:C^{\circ}_{v}\rightarrow X
.

In case (1),
\mathbf{A}(v)=0
is obvious because
f_{v}
maps

C_{v}^{\circ}
to a zero-dimensional stratum.

In case (2),
{\bf A}(v)
must be a multiple of the class
[X_{\rho}]
.
If
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}D_{i}^{*}
is the ray of
\sigma
not contained in

\rho
, then this multiple may be
determined by intersecting the curve class
of
f_{v}
with the divisor
D_{i}
. The claim now
follows immediately from

[
GS7
, Cor. 1.14]
.
∎

Definition 2.4
.

Let
G
be a graph of genus
0
.
The
spine
of
G
is the smallest connected subgraph
G^{\prime}\subseteq G

containing all legs of
G
.

The following is the key tropical argument of the paper. In item (1)
we consider the type of tropical map which will contribute to wall
structures. These are tropical maps where the domain has only one
(non-contracted) leg. Item (1) shows that this leg can
sweep out at most a codimension one polyhedral cone: in the
case of codimension
one these will play the role of a wall in the canonical wall structure.
Item (2), on the other hand, considers types which will correspond
to broken lines with respect to the canonical wall structure, and
the main point of (2) is to show that the spine of
G
, in this case,
is mapped into
B_{0}\subseteq|\Sigma(X)|
. This will be key for the
logarithmic/broken line correspondence theorem,
Theorem
4.14
.

Lemma 2.5
.

Fix a balanced realizable type

\tau=(G,{\boldsymbol{\sigma}},\mathbf{u})
of a genus zero tropical map to

\Sigma(X)
with a distinguished leg
L_{\mathrm{out}}\in L(G)
and

\mathbf{u}(L_{\mathrm{out}})\not=0
. Let
h:\Gamma(G,\ell)\rightarrow\Sigma(X)

be the corresponding universal
family of tropical maps, defined over the cone
\tau
.
For
s\in\tau
,
we write
h_{s}:G\rightarrow\Sigma(X)
for the induced map.

(1)

Suppose
G
has only one leg,
L_{\mathrm{out}}
, and let
\tau_{\mathrm{out}}\in\Gamma(G,\ell)
be the corresponding cone. Suppose further that

{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(L_{\mathrm{out}})\in\mathscr{P}
. Then
\dim h(\tau_{\mathrm{out}})\leq n-1=\dim X-1
.

(2)

Suppose
G
has precisely two legs,
L_{\mathrm{in}}
and
L_{\mathrm{out}}
, with

{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(L_{\mathrm{in}}),{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(L_{\mathrm{out}})\in\mathscr{P}
. Suppose further that

\dim\tau=n-1
and
\dim h(\tau_{\mathrm{out}})=n
.
Then with
G^{\prime}
the spine of
G
and

s\in\operatorname{Int}(\tau)
,
h_{s}(G^{\prime})\subseteq B\subseteq|\Sigma(X)|
, and

h_{s}(G^{\prime})
only intersects codimension zero and one cones of
\mathscr{P}
, except
possibly for the non-vertex endpoints of
L_{\mathrm{in}}
and
L_{\mathrm{out}}
.
Further,
\dim h(\tau_{v})=n-1
for every vertex
v
of the spine
G^{\prime}
.

Proof.

We prove both items simultaneously. We may assume in the first
case that to the contrary
\dim h(\tau_{\mathrm{out}})=n
, and thus
in both cases we have
\dim h(\tau_{\mathrm{out}})=n
.
Further, in the first case we may also assume that
\dim\tau=n-1
.
Indeed, if
\dim\tau>n-1
, there must be an
(n-1)
-dimensional
face
\tau^{\prime}
of
\tau
, necessarily corresponding to a type

\tau^{\prime}
which is a contraction of
\tau
, such that
\dim h(\tau^{\prime}_{\mathrm{out}})=n
. Thus we may replace
\tau
by
\tau^{\prime}
in this case to
ultimately achieve a contradiction.

Write
\sigma_{v}:=h(\tau_{v})
.
As
\dim\tau=n-1
,
\dim\sigma_{v}\leq n-1
.

We shall inductively find a sequence of distinct edges and legs

L_{\mathrm{out}}=E_{1},\ldots,E_{p}
and vertices
v_{1},\ldots,v_{p-1}
of

G
such that
E_{1}=L_{\mathrm{out}}
and
v_{i}
is a vertex of
E_{i}
and

E_{i+1}
. Further, this sequence will satisfy the following
inductive properties, for
s\in\operatorname{Int}(\tau)
:

(1)

The images of the edges
h_{s}(E_{i})
are all contained in
B

and only intersect codimension
0
and
1
cones of
\Sigma(X)
,
except possibly for the non-vertex endpoint of
L_{\mathrm{out}}
and,
if
E_{p}=L_{\mathrm{in}}
, also the non-vertex endpoint of
L_{\mathrm{in}}
.

(2)

\mathbf{u}(E_{i})
is not tangent to
\sigma_{v_{i}}
.

(3)

\dim\sigma_{v_{i}}=n-1
.

We will be able to continue the induction provided
E_{p}
is an edge.
Thus in the first case of the lemma, the induction process would continue
forever, a contradiction. In the second case, eventually
E_{p}=L_{\mathrm{in}}
,
and
G^{\prime}
is the union of
E_{1},\ldots,E_{p}
, giving the desired result.

For the base case, we take
E_{1}=L_{\mathrm{out}}
,
v_{1}
the unique vertex of

E_{1}
. Note from (
2.1
) that

(2.5)

h(\tau_{\mathrm{out}})=(\sigma_{v_{1}}+\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\mathbf{u}(L_{\mathrm{out}}))\cap{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(L_{\mathrm{out}}).

As
\dim h(\tau_{\mathrm{out}})=n
by assumption, necessarily
{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(L_{\mathrm{out}})\in\mathscr{P}_{{\operatorname{max}}}
and
\dim\sigma_{v_{1}}=n-1
.
Thus
{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(v_{1})\in\mathscr{P}
is either codimension
0
or
1
, and in any
case
\sigma_{v_{1}}
intersects the interior of
{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(v_{1})
.
Further,
{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(v_{1})
is a face of
{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(L_{\mathrm{out}})
.
It is then clear that for
s\in\operatorname{Int}(\tau)
,

h_{s}(v_{1})\in\operatorname{Int}(\sigma_{v_{1}})
and

h_{s}(L_{\mathrm{out}})
is contained in
B
and
only intersects codimension zero and one cones of
\Sigma(X)
(except
possibly for the non-vertex endpoint of
L_{\mathrm{out}}
).
Further, if
\mathbf{u}(L_{\mathrm{out}})
were tangent to
\sigma_{v_{1}}
,
then (
2.5
) implies
\dim h(\tau_{\mathrm{out}})=n-1
, a contradiction.
Thus inductive conditions (1)–(3) are satisfied.

We next observe that given a sequence of edges

L_{\mathrm{out}}=E_{1},\ldots,E_{p}
and vertices
v_{i}
of
E_{i}
and
E_{i+1}
,
item (2) for
2\leq i<p
holds regardless of the details of
the construction of this sequence of edges. Indeed, suppose that

\mathbf{u}(E_{i})
is tangent to
\sigma_{v_{i}}
. Split the graph

G
by detaching
E_{i}
from
v_{i}
to obtain two connected components

G_{1},G_{2}
such that
E_{i}
is a leg of
G_{1}
. Hence
E_{1}
is also
contained in
G_{1}
. Now, with
s\in\operatorname{Int}(\tau)
, necessarily

h_{s}(v_{i})+\epsilon\mathbf{u}(E_{i})\in\sigma_{v_{i}}
for

\epsilon
such that
|\epsilon|
is sufficiently small. Thus
there is an
s(\epsilon)\in\operatorname{Int}(\tau)
, depending on
\epsilon
, with
the property that
h_{s(\epsilon)}(v_{i})=h_{s}(v_{i})+\epsilon\mathbf{u}(E_{i})
. In particular, by changing the length of the
edge
E_{i}
, we may glue
h_{s}|_{G_{1}}
to
h_{s(\epsilon)}|_{G_{2}}

to obtain a tropical map
h_{s^{\prime}}:G\rightarrow\Sigma(X)
of type
\tau

which does not coincide
with
h_{s}
, but for which
h_{s^{\prime}}(E_{1})=h_{s}(E_{1})
.
Since
h_{s}(E_{1})
already varies in an
(n-1)
-dimensional family,
necessarily
\dim\tau
must be at least

n
, contradicting the assumption on the dimension.

Now assume further that
E_{1},\ldots,E_{p}
satisfy the inductive
conditions (1)–(3). We wish to construct
E_{p+1}
. By assumption
(1),

\sigma_{v_{p}}
is contained in a codimension one or codimension zero
cone
\sigma={\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(v_{p})
of
\mathscr{P}
, and thus it follows that for
s\in\operatorname{Int}(\tau)
,

h_{s}(v_{p})\in B_{0}
. As
\tau
is a balanced type, the balancing
condition thus holds at
v_{p}
. Since
\mathbf{u}(E_{i})
is
not tangent to
\sigma_{v_{p}}
, there must be at least one other
edge
E
adjacent to
v_{p}
with
\mathbf{u}(E)
not tangent to

\sigma_{v_{p}}
in order for balancing to hold. Choose one such edge
to be
E_{p+1}
, and let
v_{p+1}
be the other vertex of
E_{p+1}

if
E_{p+1}
is an edge.

We check the inductive conditions (1) and (3). For (1), note
that as
s\in\tau
varies,
h_{s}(E_{p+1})
varies in an
(n-1)
-dimensional
family, and as
h_{s}(E_{p+1})
is not tangent to
\sigma_{v_{p}}

by choice of
E_{p+1}
,
h_{s}(E_{p+1})
fills out an
n
-dimensional
subcone
\tau^{\prime}
of some

\sigma^{\prime}\in\Sigma(X)
containing
\sigma
. By Assumptions
1.1
,(2),

\sigma^{\prime}\in\mathscr{P}
. Further, for any
s\in\operatorname{Int}(\tau)
,

h_{s}(E_{p+1})
may only intersect faces of
\sigma^{\prime}
of codimension at most
one, except possibly the non-vertex endpoint of

E_{p+1}
if
E_{p+1}=L_{\mathrm{in}}
. Indeed, this is clear from
(
2.1
) in the latter case.
Otherwise,
E_{p+1}
has another vertex
v_{p+1}
, and if

h_{s}(E_{p+1})
meets a face
\sigma^{\prime\prime}\subseteq\sigma^{\prime}
of codimension
at least
2
, then
h_{s}(v_{p+1})\in\sigma^{\prime\prime}
and
{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(v_{p+1})\subseteq\sigma^{\prime\prime}
. However, then
h_{s}(E_{p+1})
can’t vary in an

(n-1)
-dimensional family. Thus inductive condition (1) follows,
and (3) is also clear for
\sigma_{v_{p+1}}
.
∎

Remark 2.6
.

In fact, the proof of the lemma tells us a bit more. In case (2),
with notation as in the proof, all edges of
G
adjacent to
v_{i}

except for
E_{i}
and
E_{i+1}
are tangent to
\sigma_{v_{i}}
.
Indeed, if not, there would be a choice for the edge
E_{i+1}

and the sequence of edges would not be unique. However, the edges

E_{1},\ldots,E_{p}
must be the unique sequence of edges from

L_{\mathrm{out}}
to
L_{\mathrm{in}}
in the spine
G^{\prime}
.

3.
The canonical wall structure and logarithmic broken lines

We continue with a pair
(X,D)
satisfying Assumptions
1.1

or
1.2
in the absolute and relative cases. We recall
also we have fixed data:

(1)

A group of degree data
H_{2}(X)
.

(2)

A saturated finitely generated monoid
Q\subseteq H_{2}(X)
such that

Q\cap(-Q)=H_{2}(X)_{\mathrm{tors}}
which contains the classes of all
effective curves on
X
. We write the monomial maximal ideal of
Q

\mathfrak{m}:=Q\setminus Q^{\times}.

Throughout this section, we also fix a monoid ideal

I\subseteq Q
such that
\sqrt{I}=\mathfrak{m}
. Equivalently, we require

Q\setminus I
to be finite.

3.1.
Recall of monomials on
B

We have constructed
(B,\mathscr{P},\varphi)

with
\varphi
a
Q^{{\operatorname{gp}}}_{\mathbb{R}}
-valued
MPL function as given in Construction
1.14
.
This choice of function then yields a local system
\mathcal{P}

[
GHS
, Def. 1.15]
on
B_{0}=B\setminus\Delta

fitting into an exact sequence

0\longrightarrow\underline{Q}^{{\operatorname{gp}}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}\longrightarrow\Lambda\longrightarrow 0,

where
\underline{Q}^{{\operatorname{gp}}}
denotes the constant sheaf with stalk
Q^{{\operatorname{gp}}}

on
B_{0}
.
7
7

7

In the case that
Q^{{\operatorname{gp}}}
has torsion,

[
GHS
, Def. 1.15]
is not suitable. Rather, one may define
\mathcal{P}

using the explicit description of parallel transport given below
using the given kinks
\kappa_{\rho}\in Q^{{\operatorname{gp}}}
. In the formalism
of
[
GHS
]
, such torsion may be accommodated by incorporating it
into the parameter ring
A
, see
[
GHS
, Rem. 5.17]
. However,
we do not need such involved notions here.

We write the map
\mathcal{P}_{x}\rightarrow\Lambda_{x}
as

p\mapsto\bar{p}
.
Further, for each
x\in B_{0}
,
[
GHS
, Def. 1.16]
gives a
submonoid
\mathcal{P}^{+}_{x}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{x}
of exponents of monomials
defined at
x
.

For our purposes, rather than reviewing the definition of

\mathcal{P}
, it is easier to give explicit descriptions of the monoids

\mathcal{P}^{+}_{x}
and the effects of parallel transport under these
descriptions.

For
\sigma\in\mathscr{P}_{{\operatorname{max}}}
,
x\in\operatorname{Int}(\sigma)
, we have

(3.1)

\mathcal{P}^{+}_{x}=\Lambda_{x}\times Q.

For
\rho\in\mathscr{P}^{[n-1]}_{\partial}
,
x\in\operatorname{Int}(\rho)
, we have

(3.2)

\mathcal{P}^{+}_{x}=\Lambda_{\rho\sigma}\times Q

where
\Lambda_{\rho\sigma}
is the monoid of integral tangent
vectors contained in the tangent wedge
T_{\rho}\sigma
of
\sigma

along the face
\rho
.
If
\sigma\in\mathscr{P}_{{\operatorname{max}}}
contains
\rho
, parallel transport in the
local system
\mathcal{P}
from
x
to
y\in\operatorname{Int}(\sigma)
induces the inclusion

\mathcal{P}^{+}_{x}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{P}^{+}_{y}
given by
(\lambda,q)\mapsto(\lambda,q)
.

For
\rho\in\mathscr{P}^{[n-1]}_{\mathrm{int}}
,
x\in\operatorname{Int}(\rho)
, we have

(3.3)

\mathcal{P}^{+}_{x}=(\Lambda_{\rho}\oplus\mathbb{N}Z_{+}\oplus\mathbb{N}Z_{-}\oplus Q)/\langle Z_{+}+Z_{-}=\kappa_{\rho}\rangle.

This abstract description requires an ordering

\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}\in\mathscr{P}_{{\operatorname{max}}}
of the maximal cells containing

\rho
and a choice of vector
\xi\in\Lambda_{x}
pointing into

\sigma
and representing a generator of
\Lambda_{\sigma}/\Lambda_{\rho}
.
Then for
y\in\operatorname{Int}(\sigma)
,
y^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Int}(\sigma^{\prime})
, parallel transport
in the local system
\mathcal{P}
yields inclusions

(3.4)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathfrak{t}_{\rho\sigma}:\mathcal{P}_{x}^{+}\hookrightarrow{}&\mathcal{P}_{y}^{+}\\
(\lambda_{\rho},aZ_{+},bZ_{-},q)\mapsto{}&\big(\lambda_{\rho}+(a-b)\xi,q+b\kappa_{\rho})\end{split}

and

(3.5)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathfrak{t}_{\rho\sigma^{\prime}}:\mathcal{P}_{x}^{+}\hookrightarrow{}&\mathcal{P}_{y^{\prime}}^{+}\\
(\lambda_{\rho},aZ_{+},bZ_{-},q)\mapsto{}&\big(\lambda_{\rho}+(a-b)\xi,q+a\kappa_{\rho})\end{split}

respectively. See the discussion of
[
GHS
, §2.2]
.

Given a choice of monoid ideal
I\subseteq Q
, we also introduce
the monoid ideal
I_{x}\subseteq\mathcal{P}^{+}_{x}
when
x\in\operatorname{Int}(\sigma)
,
\sigma\in\mathscr{P}_{{\operatorname{max}}}
defined in the description (
3.1
) as

(3.6)

I_{x}:=\Lambda_{x}\times I.

Notation 3.1
.

For
x\in\operatorname{Int}(\sigma)
,
\sigma\in\mathscr{P}_{{\operatorname{max}}}
, we will often write a monomial
in
\Bbbk[\mathcal{P}^{+}_{x}]=\Bbbk[Q][\Lambda_{x}]
either as
z^{m}
for
m\in\mathcal{P}^{+}_{x}

or as
t^{q}z^{\bar{m}}

for
(\bar{m},q)\in\Lambda_{x}\oplus Q
via (
3.1
).

Similarly, if
x\in\operatorname{Int}(\rho)
with
\rho\in\mathscr{P}^{[n-1]}
,
we have a canonically defined submonoid

\Lambda_{\rho}\oplus Q\subseteq\mathcal{P}^{+}_{x}
via
(
3.2
) or
(
3.3
), hence defining a subring

\Bbbk[Q][\Lambda_{\rho}]\subseteq\Bbbk[\mathcal{P}^{+}_{x}]
. We again write
monomials in this ring as
t^{q}z^{\bar{m}}
for
q\in Q,\bar{m}\in\Lambda_{\rho}
.

We will frequently need to use parallel transport in

\mathcal{P}^{+}
from a cell
\sigma\in\mathscr{P}_{{\operatorname{max}}}
to a cell

\sigma^{\prime}\in\mathscr{P}_{{\operatorname{max}}}
, either with
\sigma=\sigma^{\prime}
or

\sigma\cap\sigma^{\prime}=\rho\in\mathscr{P}^{[n-1]}
. Take any
x\in\operatorname{Int}(\sigma)
,

x^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Int}(\sigma^{\prime})
. If
\sigma=\sigma^{\prime}
, we may define

\mathfrak{t}_{\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}}:\mathcal{P}^{+}_{x}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}^{+}_{x^{\prime}}

to be given by parallel transport along a path contained in
\operatorname{Int}(\sigma)
;
under the representation (
3.1
), this map is the identity.
On the other hand, if
\sigma\cap\sigma^{\prime}\in\mathscr{P}^{[n-1]}
,
parallel transport along a path contained in
\mathrm{Star}(\rho)
gives a map

\mathfrak{t}_{\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}}:\mathcal{P}^{+}_{x}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}_{x^{\prime}}.

Noting that at the level of groups,
\mathfrak{t}_{\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}}=\mathfrak{t}_{\rho\sigma^{\prime}}\circ\mathfrak{t}_{\rho\sigma}^{-1}
, it follows that if

m\in\mathcal{P}^{+}_{x}
and
\bar{m}\in T_{\rho}\sigma
, then

\mathfrak{t}_{\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}}(m)\in\mathcal{P}^{+}_{x^{\prime}}
.
Thus, either in this case or the case
\sigma=\sigma^{\prime}
,
we may write
\mathfrak{t}_{\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}}(m)\in\mathcal{P}^{+}_{x^{\prime}}
whenever

m\in\mathcal{P}^{+}_{x},\quad\bar{m}\in T_{\sigma\cap\sigma^{\prime}}\sigma.

If we let
R^{\prime}_{I}
denote the subring of

(\Bbbk[Q]/I)[\Lambda_{\sigma}]
generated by monomials
of the form
t^{q}z^{\bar{m}}
for
\bar{m}\in T_{\sigma\cap\sigma^{\prime}}\sigma
, we
obtain a ring homomorphism

(3.7)

\mathfrak{t}_{\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}}:R^{\prime}_{I}\rightarrow(\Bbbk[Q]/I)[\Lambda_{\sigma^{\prime}}]

3.2.
The canonical wall structure

3.2.1.
Recall of wall structures

We recall the notion of
walls
and
wall structures

from
[
GHS
, Def. 2.11]
:

Definition 3.2
.

A
wall
on
(B,\mathscr{P})
is a codimension one rational polyhedral subset

\mathfrak{p}\not\subseteq\partial B
of some
\sigma\in\mathscr{P}_{{\operatorname{max}}}
,
along with an element

f_{\mathfrak{p}}=\sum_{m\in\mathcal{P}^{+}_{x},\bar{m}\in\Lambda_{\mathfrak{p}}}c_{m}z^{m}\in\Bbbk[\mathcal{P}^{+}_{x}],

for
x\in\operatorname{Int}(\mathfrak{p})
. Identifying
\mathcal{P}_{y}
with
\mathcal{P}_{x}
by
parallel transport inside
\sigma\setminus\Delta
,
we require that
m\in\mathcal{P}^{+}_{y}

for all
y\in\mathfrak{p}\setminus\Delta
when
c_{m}\not=0
.
We further require that
f_{\mathfrak{p}}\equiv 1\mod\mathfrak{m}
.

Definition 3.3
.

A
wall structure

\mathscr{S}
on
(B,\mathscr{P})
is a finite set of walls.

Remark 3.4
.

The above definitions differ in a couple of ways from that of

[
GHS
, Def. 2.11]
. First, we are less permissive with wall
functions, insisting that
f_{\mathfrak{p}}\equiv 1\mod\mathfrak{m}
. Second,
we are more permissive with the notion of wall structure. In

[
GHS
]
, we insist walls form the codimension one cells of
a rational polyhedral decomposition of
B
refining
\mathscr{P}
. This
is imposed there to make it easier to describe gluing. However,
a wall structure in the above more liberal sense is equivalent
(in the sense of Definition
3.5
below)
to one in the sense of
[
GHS
, Def. 2.11]
,
and we ignore this issue in this section and the next,
only returning to the convention of
[
GHS
]
in §
5
.

Definition 3.5
.

For a wall structure
\mathscr{S}
, we define

\displaystyle|\mathscr{S}|:={}

\displaystyle\bigcup_{\mathfrak{p}\in\mathscr{S}}\mathfrak{p}\cup\bigcup_{\rho\in\mathscr{P}^{[n-1]}}\rho,

\displaystyle\operatorname{Sing}(\mathscr{S}):={}

\displaystyle\Delta\cup\bigcup_{\mathfrak{p}\in\mathscr{S}}\partial\mathfrak{p}\cup\bigcup_{\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}\in\mathscr{S}}(\mathfrak{p}\cap\mathfrak{p}^{\prime})

where the last union is over all pairs of walls
\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}
with

\mathfrak{p}\cap\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}
codimension at least two.

If
x\in B\setminus\operatorname{Sing}(\mathscr{S})
, we define

(3.8)

f_{x}:=\prod_{x\in\mathfrak{p}\in\mathscr{S}}f_{\mathfrak{p}}.

We say two wall structures are
equivalent
(modulo
I
)
if

f_{x}=f^{\prime}_{x}\mod I
for all
x\in B\setminus(\operatorname{Sing}(\mathscr{S})\cup\operatorname{Sing}(\mathscr{S}^{\prime}))
.
Generally we omit
mention of
I
if clear from context.

3.2.2.
The construction

Definition 3.6
.

A
wall type
is a type
\tau=(G,{\boldsymbol{\sigma}},{\mathbf{u}})

of tropical map to
\Sigma(X)
such that:

(1)

G
is a genus zero graph with
L(G)=\{L_{\mathrm{out}}\}
with

{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(L_{\mathrm{out}})\in\mathscr{P}
and
u_{\tau}:={\bf u}(L_{\mathrm{out}})\not=0
.

(2)

\tau
is realizable and balanced.

(3)

Let
h:\Gamma(G,\ell)\rightarrow\Sigma(X)
be the corresponding
universal family of tropical maps, and
\tau_{\mathrm{out}}\in\Gamma(G,\ell)

the cone corresponding to
L_{\mathrm{out}}
. Then
\dim\tau=n-2
and

\dim h(\tau_{\mathrm{out}})=n-1
. Further,
h(\tau_{\mathrm{out}})\not\subseteq\partial B
.

A
decorated wall type
is a decorated type
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}=(\tau,{\bf A})

with
\tau
a wall type.

Before using wall types to define the invariants we use
in the canonical wall structure, we first make an observation
in the relative case needed to show properness of the relevant
moduli spaces. For most of the paper, we only work with the absolute
moduli space
\mathscr{M}(X,\tau)
, but it turns out that in the relative
case, we may also work with the relative moduli space
\mathscr{M}(X/S,\tau)

when
\tau
is a wall type. To make this precise, we note that a realizable
type
\tau
for a punctured map to
X
is a type for
X/S
if
the universal tropical map over
\tau
to
\Sigma(X)
fits into a commutative
diagram

\textstyle{\Gamma(G,\ell)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h_{\tau}}
\textstyle{\Sigma(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma(g)}
\textstyle{\tau\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Sigma(S)}

Proposition 3.7
.

In the relative case, let
\tau
be a wall type for
X
, and
let
\beta
be the class of punctured map determined by the data

u_{\tau}
and
A\in Q\setminus I
a non-zero curve class.
Then
\tau
is a type of punctured map to

X/S
, and
\mathscr{M}(X,\beta)=\mathscr{M}(X/S,\beta)
.

Proof.

We first show that the type
\tau=(G,{\boldsymbol{\sigma}},\mathbf{u})
is in fact a type for

X/S
. Since
G
is connected, it is sufficient to show
that for each
E\in E(G)\cup L(G)
,
\Sigma(g)_{*}({\bf u}(E))=0
.
Recall that as a wall type,
\tau
is realizable and balanced.
Since
\tau
is realizable, we obtain a family of tropical maps

h_{s}:G\rightarrow\Sigma(X)
,
s\in\operatorname{Int}(\tau)
. Composing with

\Sigma(g)
gives a tropical map
\Sigma(g)\circ h_{s}:G\rightarrow\Sigma(S)=\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, which is balanced by Definition
2.2
.
But it is then immediate that this map must be constant,
as any tropical map to
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
satisfying the balancing condition
and with only one leg must be constant. Thus
\tau
is a type
defined over
S
.

The equalities of moduli spaces now follow from

[
ACGS2
, Prop. 5.11]
.
∎

Construction 3.8
.

Fix a wall type
\tau
and a non-zero curve class
A\in Q\setminus I
.
Let
\beta
be the class of punctured map determined by the data

u_{\tau}
and
A
. Then we obtain a reduced closed stratum

\mathfrak{M}_{\tau}(\mathcal{X},\beta)\subseteq\mathfrak{M}(\mathcal{X},\beta)
,
and a moduli space
\mathscr{M}_{\tau}(X,\beta)
along with a morphism

\varepsilon:\mathscr{M}_{\tau}(X,\beta)\rightarrow\mathfrak{M}_{\tau}(\mathcal{X},\beta).

Lemma 3.9
.

\mathscr{M}_{\tau}(X,\beta)
is proper over
\operatorname{Spec}\Bbbk
and carries
a virtual fundamental class of dimension
0
.

Proof.

In the absolute case,
\mathscr{M}_{\tau}(X,\beta)
is closed substack
of
\mathscr{M}(X,\beta)
, which is proper over
\operatorname{Spec}\Bbbk

by
[
ACGS2
, Cor. 3.17]
.

We next consider the relative case.
There is a morphism
\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}=[\mathbb{A}^{1}/\mathbb{G}_{m}]
, induced
by
g
, where
\mathcal{S}
is the Artin fan of
S
. Write

\mathcal{X}_{0}:=\mathcal{X}\times_{\mathcal{S}}[0/\mathbb{G}_{m}]
; this is a closed substack of
\mathcal{X}
.
As
\dim h(\tau_{\mathrm{out}})=n-1
and

h(\tau_{\mathrm{out}})\not\subseteq\partial B
, it follows that

\Sigma(g):h(\tau_{\mathrm{out}})\rightarrow\Sigma(S)=\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
is
surjective by Proposition
1.15
.
By Proposition
3.7
,

\Sigma(g)\circ h_{s}
is constant for each
s\in\tau
, and hence
for any vertex
v\in V(G)
,
\Sigma(g):h(\tau_{v})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}

is also surjective. From this it follows that any
punctured map
C^{\circ}\rightarrow\mathcal{X}
in
\mathfrak{M}_{\tau}(\mathcal{X},\beta)

has image lying set-theoretically in
|\mathcal{X}_{0}|
. Since
\mathfrak{M}_{\tau}(\mathcal{X},\beta)
is reduced by construction, any map must thus factor through

\mathcal{X}_{0}
log-scheme-theoretically, and hence
any punctured map in
\mathscr{M}_{\tau}(X,\beta)
must factor through

X_{0}
log-scheme-theoretically.
Thus
\mathscr{M}_{\tau}(X,\beta)

is a closed substack of
\mathscr{M}(X\times_{S}0,\beta)
,
which is again proper over
\operatorname{Spec}\Bbbk
.

We now calculate the virtual dimension. By
[
ACGS2
, Prop. 3.28]
,
if
\bar{\tau}
is the global type induced by
\tau
, then
\mathfrak{M}(\mathcal{X},\bar{\tau})
is pure-dimensional and reduced of dimension

-3+|L(G)|-\dim\tau=-3+1-(n-2)=-n.

The same is then true for
\mathfrak{M}_{\tau}(\mathcal{X},\beta)
, as
the forgetful map
\mathfrak{M}(\mathcal{X},\bar{\tau})\rightarrow\mathfrak{M}_{\tau}(\mathcal{X},\beta)

is finite of generic degree
|\operatorname{Aut}(\bar{\tau})|
.
The virtual relative dimension of

\mathscr{M}_{\tau}(X,\beta)
over
\mathfrak{M}_{\tau}(\mathcal{X},\beta)
at
a punctured map
f:C^{\circ}\rightarrow X
over a geometric point
is
\chi(f^{*}\Theta_{X})=A\cdot c_{1}(\Theta_{X})+n

by Riemann-Roch.
Recall that
c_{1}(\Theta_{X})=-(K_{X}+D)\equiv_{\mathbb{Q}}-\sum_{i}a_{i}D_{i}

by assumption.
Further, for any generator
D_{i}^{*}
of
{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(L_{\mathrm{out}})
,
it follows that
a_{i}=0
as
{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(L_{\mathrm{out}})\in\mathscr{P}
. Thus by

[
GS7
, Cor. 1.14]
,
A\cdot D_{i}=0
whenever
a_{i}\not=0
.
Thus the total virtual dimension is
0
as claimed.
∎

We now define

(3.9)

W_{\tau,A}:=\deg[\mathscr{M}_{\tau}(X,\beta)]^{\mathrm{virt}}.

In addition,
h|_{\tau_{\mathrm{out}}}:\tau_{\mathrm{out}}\rightarrow\sigma

induces a morphism

h_{*}:\Lambda_{\tau_{\mathrm{out}}}\rightarrow\Lambda_{\sigma},

and we define

(3.10)

k_{\tau}:=|\operatorname{coker}(h_{*})_{\mathrm{tors}}|=|\Lambda_{h(\tau_{\mathrm{out}})}/h_{*}(\Lambda_{\tau_{\mathrm{out}}})|.

Finally, set

(3.11)

\boxed{\mathfrak{p}_{\tau,A}:=\big(h(\tau_{\mathrm{out}}),\exp(k_{\tau}W_{\tau,A}t^{A}z^{-u_{\tau}})\big).}

Here we view
t^{A}z^{-u_{\tau}}
as a monomial in
\Bbbk[\mathcal{P}^{+}_{x}]

for
x\in\operatorname{Int}(h(\tau_{\mathrm{out}}))

as in Notation
3.1
. To view the
exponential as a finite sum, note that

\Bbbk[Q][\Lambda_{h(\tau_{\mathrm{out}})}]\subseteq\Bbbk[\mathcal{P}^{+}_{x}]
, and
we may truncate the infinite sum by removing all monomials
which are zero in
(\Bbbk[Q]/I)[\Lambda_{h(\tau_{\mathrm{out}})}]
.

We then define:

Definition 3.10
.

\mathscr{S}_{{\mathrm{can}}}^{\mathrm{undec}}:=\{\mathfrak{p}_{\tau,A}\,|\,\hbox{$\tau$ an
isomorphism class of wall type, $A\in Q\setminus I$, $W_{\tau,A}\not=0$}\}.

We note the superscript “
\mathrm{undec}
” refers to the
undecorated

wall structure, in distinction with the
decorated
wall structure
we will define in Construction
3.13
,
which will be equivalent to the above wall structure but which will
be more useful in the proof of consistency.

Proposition 3.11
.

\mathscr{S}^{\mathrm{undec}}_{{\mathrm{can}}}
is a wall structure.

Proof.

We need to verify that (1)
\mathfrak{p}_{\tau,A}
is always a wall and (2)

\mathscr{S}^{\mathrm{undec}}_{{\mathrm{can}}}
is finite.

For the first item, fix
\tau,A
. Write
u:=u_{\tau}
. It is obvious that

h(\tau_{\mathrm{out}})
is a rational polyhedral cone of codimension one by
assumption. We need to check that the parallel transport of
(-u,A)

to
\mathcal{P}_{y}
lies in
\mathcal{P}^{+}_{y}
for each point

y\in\mathfrak{p}_{\tau,A}\setminus\Delta
.
Since
\Delta
is the union of all codimension two cones of
\mathscr{P}
, this
is only an issue if
y\in\rho\subseteq\sigma
where
\rho
is
codimension one and
\mathfrak{p}_{\tau,A}\not\subseteq\rho
. In this case,

\dim{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(L_{\mathrm{out}})=n
. Let
v\in V(G)
be the vertex adjacent to
L_{\mathrm{out}}
,

\tau_{v}\in\Gamma(G,\ell)
the corresponding cone.
We divide the analysis into three cases, depending on the relationship
between
y
and
h(\tau_{v})
.

Case 1:

y\not\in h(\tau_{v})
.
From (
2.1
), necessarily
-u\in T_{\rho}\sigma
.
If
y\in\partial B
,
then it is immediate from (
3.2
) that

(-u,A)\in\mathcal{P}^{+}_{y}
.
If instead
y\not\in\partial B
, we may choose

\xi\in\Lambda_{y}
pointing into
\sigma
as in the description of
\mathcal{P}^{+}_{y}

of (
3.3
), and then write
-u=u^{\prime}+a\xi
for some
a>0

and
u^{\prime}\in\Lambda_{\rho}
.
Thus by (
3.4
),
(-u,A)
is identified with the
element
(u^{\prime},aZ_{+},0,A)
of
\mathcal{P}^{+}_{y}
.

Case 2:

y\in h(\tau_{v})\cap\partial B
.
We are thus in the relative case.
By Proposition
3.7
,
\beta
must
be defined over
S
, which in particular means that
u
is
tangent to the fibres of
g_{\mathrm{trop}}
, and hence
u
is tangent
to
\partial B
. However, by (
3.2
), it then
follows that
(-u,A)\in\mathcal{P}^{+}_{y}
.

Case 3:

y\in h(\tau_{v})\setminus\partial B
. Here we assume that

W_{\tau,A}\not=0
, (as otherwise such a wall would not be included in

\mathscr{S}^{\mathrm{undec}}_{{\mathrm{can}}}
). Thus there is necessarily a punctured map

f:C^{\circ}/W\rightarrow X
over a geometric log point with type
\tau^{\prime}

equipped with a contraction to
\tau
. Thus we may mark
f
with
\tau
, and
this leads to a decorated type
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}=(\tau,{\bf A})
with
{\bf A}(v)
given by
the curve class of the map
f_{v}:C^{\circ}_{v}\rightarrow X
, where
C^{\circ}_{v}

is the subcurve of
C^{\circ}
corresponding to
v\in V(G)
, as in the proof of
Lemma
2.1
. In this case,
u
points into
\sigma
, and in the
notation
\delta
of Lemma
2.3
, (2),
{\bf A}(v)=d[X_{\rho}]
with
d\geq\delta(u)
. Thus the total curve class
A

satisfies
A=\delta(u)[X_{\rho}]+A^{\prime}
for some
A^{\prime}\in Q
.

We may now use the description (
3.3
) along with
(
3.4
) to test whether
(-u,A)\in\mathcal{P}^{+}_{x}
lies in the image of

\mathfrak{t}_{\rho\sigma}:\mathcal{P}^{+}_{y}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}^{+}_{x}
. Choosing
\xi\in\Lambda_{y}
as
before, we may now write
u=u^{\prime}+\delta(u)\xi
for some
u^{\prime}\in\Lambda_{\rho}
.
Thus
(-u,A)
equals
\mathfrak{t}_{\rho\sigma}
applied to

(-u^{\prime},0,\delta(u)Z_{-},A-\delta(u)\kappa_{\rho})=(-u^{\prime},0,\delta(u)Z_{-},A^{\prime})\in\mathcal{P}^{+}_{y}.

We have now covered all possible cases for the location of
y
,
and hence
\mathfrak{p}_{\tau,A}
is a wall.

To show
\mathscr{S}^{\mathrm{undec}}_{{\mathrm{can}}}
is finite, we first observe that as

Q\setminus I
is finite by assumption on
Q
and
I
, there are only a finite
number of choices for
A
. For determining
\tau
, there are only a finite
number of possibilities for
{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(L_{\mathrm{out}})\in\mathscr{P}
. Given a choice of
A
and
wall type
\tau
with given
{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(L_{\mathrm{out}})
,
W_{\tau,A}\not=0
implies that

\mathscr{M}_{\tau}(X,\beta)
is non-empty, and then
[
GS7
, Cor. 1.14]
shows

u_{\tau}
is determined by the choice of
A
and
{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(L_{\mathrm{out}})
. If

\beta
is the punctured curve class determined by
A
and a given
u_{\tau}
,
then, since
\mathscr{M}(X,\beta)
is finite type, there are only a finite number of
types of tropical maps
\tau
appearing in the tropicalizations of curves in

\mathscr{M}(X,\beta)
, i.e., there are only a finite number of
\tau
such that

\mathscr{M}_{\tau}(X,\beta)
is non-empty.
∎

The main result of the paper, to be proved in §
5
, can then
be stated:

Theorem 3.12
.

\mathscr{S}^{\mathrm{undec}}_{{\mathrm{can}}}
is a consistent wall structure in the sense of

[
GHS
, Def. 3.9]
.

The above definition of the canonical wall structure is conceptually
the simplest and most useful in practice (see
[
AG
]
for some explicit
examples). However, for the proofs of this paper, it is convenient
to replace
\mathscr{S}^{\mathrm{undec}}_{{\mathrm{can}}}
with an equivalent
wall structure
\mathscr{S}_{{\mathrm{can}}}
using decorated types as follows.

Construction 3.13
.

Fix a decorated wall type
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}=(\tau,{\bf A})
, and
A=\sum_{v\in V(G)}{\bf A}(v)
the total curve class.
As
\tau
is realizable, we may view it equivalently as a global type.
Hence we obtain a morphism of moduli spaces
\varepsilon:\mathscr{M}(X,{\boldsymbol{\tau}})\rightarrow\mathfrak{M}(\mathcal{X},{\boldsymbol{\tau}})
. From the proof of
Lemma
3.9
, one sees that
\mathfrak{M}(\mathcal{X},{\boldsymbol{\tau}})
is
pure-dimensional and
[\mathscr{M}(X,{\boldsymbol{\tau}})]^{\mathrm{virt}}
is a zero dimensional
cycle. Hence we may define

W_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}}:={\deg[\mathscr{M}(X,{\boldsymbol{\tau}})]^{\mathrm{virt}}\over|\operatorname{Aut}({\boldsymbol{\tau}})|}.

We may then define a wall

(3.12)

\boxed{\mathfrak{p}_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}}:=\big(h(\tau_{\mathrm{out}}),\exp(k_{\tau}W_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}}t^{A}z^{-u_{\tau}})\big)}

and

(3.13)

\mathscr{S}_{{\mathrm{can}}}:=\left\{\mathfrak{p}_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}}\,\Big|\,\begin{subarray}{c}\hbox{${\boldsymbol{\tau}}$ an isomorphism class of
decorated wall}\\
\hbox{type with total curve class lying in
$Q\setminus I$}\end{subarray}\right\}.

We note here we do not exclude walls with
W_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}}=0
,
so this wall structure may include an infinite set of
trivial walls, i.e., with attached function
1
. So technically
this is not a wall structure, but if we remove all trivial walls,
it becomes a finite set as in Proposition
3.11
.
However, for bookkeeping purposes, it will prove useful to
include these trivial walls.

To see that this gives a wall structure equivalent (in the
sense of Definition
3.5
) to the previous definition,
fix a wall type
\tau
and a curve class
A\in Q\setminus I

such that
W_{\tau,A}\not=0
; this data will
give one wall in the earlier definition of
\mathscr{S}_{{\mathrm{can}}}
. With

\beta=(\{u_{\tau}\},A)
the associated class of punctured map, we have the
canonical morphism
\mathfrak{M}(\mathcal{X},\tau)\rightarrow\mathfrak{M}_{\tau}(\mathcal{X},\beta)
, which
is surjective and finite of degree
|\operatorname{Aut}(\tau)|
. Further, we have a
Cartesian diagram in all categories
[
ACGS2
, Prop. 5.19]

\textstyle{\coprod_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}=(\tau,{\bf A})}\mathscr{M}(X,{\boldsymbol{\tau}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon_{\tau}}
\textstyle{\mathscr{M}(X,\beta)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{M}(\mathcal{X},\tau)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{M}(\mathcal{X},\beta)}

Here
{\bf A}
runs over all decorations of
\tau
with total curve class
A
.
As
j
is a map of degree
|\operatorname{Aut}(\tau)|
onto
\mathfrak{M}_{\tau}(\mathcal{X},\beta)
,
we see that

(3.14)

\displaystyle W_{\tau,A}={\deg\varepsilon^{!}[\mathfrak{M}_{\tau}(\mathcal{X},\beta)]}={\deg\varepsilon^{!}j_{*}[\mathfrak{M}(\mathcal{X},\tau)]\over|\operatorname{Aut}(\tau)|}={\deg j^{\prime}_{*}\varepsilon_{\tau}^{!}[\mathfrak{M}(\mathcal{X},\tau)]\over|\operatorname{Aut}(\tau)|}=\sum_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}=(\tau,{\bf A})}{\deg[\mathscr{M}(X,{\boldsymbol{\tau}})]^{\mathrm{virt}}\over|\operatorname{Aut}(\tau)|}.

Here the third equality holds by push-pull of
[
Mn
, Thm. 4.1]
, and
the last summation runs over all choices of decorations
{\bf A}

of
\tau
with total curve class
A
. Note however that

\operatorname{Aut}(\tau)
acts on the set of all choices of decoration
{\bf A}
,
with orbits of this action giving isomorphism classes of decorated
types
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}
.
The stabilizer of a given
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}
is the subgroup
\operatorname{Aut}({\boldsymbol{\tau}})\subseteq\operatorname{Aut}(\tau)
, and hence the orbit containing
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}
is of size

|\operatorname{Aut}(\tau)|/|\operatorname{Aut}({\boldsymbol{\tau}})|
. Thus the final expression of
(
3.14
) can be now expressed as a sum over isomorphism
classes of decorations
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}
of
\tau
with total class
A
:

(3.15)

W_{\tau,A}=\sum_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}}{\deg[\mathscr{M}(X,{\boldsymbol{\tau}})]^{\mathrm{virt}}\over|\operatorname{Aut}(\tau)|}\cdot{|\operatorname{Aut}(\tau)|\over|\operatorname{Aut}({\boldsymbol{\tau}})|}=\sum_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}}{\deg[\mathscr{M}(X,{\boldsymbol{\tau}})]^{\mathrm{virt}}\over|\operatorname{Aut}({\boldsymbol{\tau}})|}.

From this, we see the collection of walls in the new definition
of
\mathscr{S}_{{\mathrm{can}}}
coming from all
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}
with underlying type
\tau

and total curve class
A
is equivalent to the corresponding
single wall in the original definition of
\mathscr{S}_{{\mathrm{can}}}
.

Example 3.14
.

The construction of
\mathscr{S}_{{\mathrm{can}}}
agrees (up to equivalence)
with the construction of
the canonical scattering diagram of
[
GHK
]
when
\dim X=2
and

K_{X}+D=0
, so that
B=\Sigma(X)
. In other words,
the construction given here is a generalization
of the construction of
[
GHK
]
.

To see this, we analyze decorated wall types
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}
in dimension
two. Since we require
\dim\tau=\dim X-2
, in fact such types
will be rigid. Thus there is a unique tropical map
h:G\rightarrow B
of type
\tau
, and all vertices of
G
are mapped to
0\in B
;
otherwise, rescaling
B
provides a non-trivial deformation of
h
.
Further, we require
\dim h(\tau_{\mathrm{out}})=1
. So

h(\tau_{\mathrm{out}})
is a ray in
B
with endpoint
0
.
Hence there is no choice but for
u_{\tau}\in B(\mathbb{Z})\setminus\{0\}
and
h(\tau_{\mathrm{out}})=\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}u_{\tau}
.
Further, any edge of
G
contracted
by
h
has length a free parameter of the tropical curve, and hence
again by rigidity, there are no edges. Thus the only possibility for

{\boldsymbol{\tau}}
is that
G
has one vertex, with an attached curve class

A
, and one leg,
L_{\mathrm{out}}
, with
{\bf u}(L_{\mathrm{out}})=u_{\tau}\in B(\mathbb{Z})
. Note further that
k_{\tau}
is then just the index of

u_{\tau}
, i.e., the degree of divisibility of
u_{\tau}
in
B(\mathbb{Z})
.
Of course
|\operatorname{Aut}({\boldsymbol{\tau}})|=1
.

To make a comparison with the setup of
[
GHK
]
, it is then convenient,
for
u\in B(\mathbb{Z})
primitive, to define

W_{A,u,k}:=W_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}},

where
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}
is the decorated wall type as described above with
curve class
A
and
u_{\tau}=ku
. In
[
GHK
]
, only one wall
for each ray
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}u
of rational slope occurs, and hence it
is then more useful to write the canonical wall structure in the
equivalent form

\mathscr{S}^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{can}}}:=\left\{\Big(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}u,\exp\Big(\sum_{A,k}kW_{A,u,k}t^{A}z^{-ku}\Big)\Big)\,\Big|\,\hbox{$u\in B(\mathbb{Z})\setminus\{0\}$ primitive}\right\}.

Here the sum is over all positive integers
k
and all curve classes

A
. However, if we write
u=aD_{i}^{*}+bD_{j}^{*}
for some non-negative

a,b
with
a+b>0
, then by the balancing condition as expressed in

[
GS7
, Cor. 1.14]
, we may in fact sum over only those curve
classes with
A\cdot D_{i}=a
,
A\cdot D_{j}=b
,
A\cdot D_{k}=0
for
k\not=i,j
.

In
[
GHK
]
, a wall function is associated to each ray
\mathfrak{d}=\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}u

of rational slope using invariants defined as follows. Assuming
\mathfrak{d}
does not
coincide with a ray of
\mathscr{P}
, one performs a toric (log étale) blow-up

\pi:\widetilde{X}\rightarrow X
by refining
(B,\mathscr{P})
along the given ray
\mathfrak{d}
.
Otherwise, if
\mathfrak{d}
coincides with a ray of
\mathscr{P}
, we may take
\pi
to be the
identity. Let
\widetilde{A}
be a curve class on
\widetilde{X}
with
the following properties, depending on whether or not
\pi
is the identity.
If
\pi
is not the identity, then we require
that
\widetilde{A}
has trivial intersection number with all
boundary components of
\widetilde{X}
except for the exceptional divisor
E
of

\pi
. If
\pi
is the identity, let
E
be the
component of
D
corresponding to the ray
\mathfrak{d}
. We then require
that
\widetilde{A}
has zero intersection number with each component of

D
except for
E
. Then
[
GHK
]
, following
[
GPS
, §4]
,
defines a
number
N_{\widetilde{A}}
. This number is defined as a
relative

Gromov-Witten invariant for the non-compact pair
(\widetilde{X}^{\circ},E^{\circ})
where
\widetilde{X}^{\circ}\subseteq\widetilde{X}
is
obtained by removing the closure of
\pi^{-1}(D)\setminus E
from
\widetilde{X}
, and
E^{\circ}=E\cap\widetilde{X}^{\circ}
. This relative invariant counts
rational curves of class
\widetilde{A}
with one marked point, with contact
order
k_{\widetilde{A}}:=\widetilde{A}\cdot E
with
E^{\circ}
.

To compare
N_{\widetilde{A}}
with the type of number considered in this paper,
note that the data of
\widetilde{A}
and the contact order
k_{\widetilde{A}}

also specifies a type
\widetilde{\beta}
of logarithmic map to the pair

(\widetilde{X},\pi^{-1}(D))
, and we may compare the logarithmic Gromov-Witten
invariant
\deg[\mathscr{M}(\widetilde{X},\widetilde{\beta})]^{\mathrm{virt}}
with

N_{\widetilde{A}}
. In fact, it follows from
[
AMW
]
that these two numbers
will agree provided that every stable log map in the moduli space

\mathscr{M}(\widetilde{X},\widetilde{\beta})
factors through
\widetilde{X}^{\circ}
.
However, it is an elementary exercise in tropical geometry to show that if given
a stable log map
f:C/W\rightarrow\widetilde{X}
of type
\widetilde{\beta}
with

W
a log point, any tropical map in the corresponding family of tropical maps
has image
\mathfrak{d}
. This may be proved using an argument similar to the argument
given in Lemma
2.5
, but a similar tropical argument in
two dimensions has already appeared in the proof of
[
Bo1
, Lem. 12]
,
which may also be viewed as a tropical interpretation of
[
GPS
, Prop. 4.2]
.
On the other hand, any stable log map of type
\widetilde{\beta}
which does not
factor through
\widetilde{X}^{\circ}
will necessarily have a tropicalization
whose image does not coincide with
\mathfrak{d}
, as follows immediately from the
construction of tropicalization.

Finally, let
A=\pi_{*}\widetilde{A}
. Note that in the case
\pi
is not the
identity, the curve class
\widetilde{A}
is uniquely determined by
A
and the
constraint that
\widetilde{A}
has zero intersection number with components of
the strict transform of
D
. Then if
u
is the primitive generator of
\mathfrak{d}\cap B(\mathbb{Z})
, we may interpret
k_{\widetilde{A}}u\in B(\mathbb{Z})
as a contact order, and
the class
A
and the contact order
k_{\widetilde{A}}u
determines a type of log
map
\beta
to
X
. It then follows from the birational invariance of log
Gromov-Witten theory of
[
AW
, Thm. 1.1.1]
that
\deg[\mathscr{M}(\widetilde{X},\widetilde{\beta})]^{\mathrm{virt}}=\deg[\mathscr{M}(X,\beta)]^{\mathrm{virt}}
. In particular,

N_{\widetilde{A}}=W_{A,u,k_{\widetilde{A}}}
.

Conversely, given the data of
A
,
u
and
k
giving a wall type
\tau
with

\mathscr{M}(X,\tau)
non-empty, we obtain via the above blow-up procedure
a curve class
\widetilde{A}
. Thus we have a one-to-one correspondence between
the set of data contributing to
\mathscr{S}^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{can}}}
and the data contributing to the
canonical scattering diagram of
[
GHK
]
. Further, we have just observed an
equality of the invariants. The equivalence of
\mathscr{S}^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{can}}}
with the
canonical scattering diagram of
[
GHK
]
now follows by inspection of the
definition of the canonical scattering diagram in
[
GHK
]
.

Example 3.15
.

Returning to Examples
1.4
and

1.11
, we first introduce notation for
the relevant curve classes. Let
e_{i}
be the class of a fibre
of
\pi|_{E_{i}}:E_{i}\rightarrow Z_{i}
. Let
f
be the class of
a curve of the form
\{a\}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\{b\}
disjoint from
the centers
Z_{1},Z_{2}
.

One can show that the only decorated wall types
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}
with

W_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}}\not=0
are of the following 5 types. In each case,
the underlying graph
G
of
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}
has one vertex
v
, no edges,
and of course a unique leg
L_{\mathrm{out}}
.

(1)

For some
k>0
, we have
u_{\tau}=kD_{2,0}^{*}
,
{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(L_{\mathrm{out}})=\mathfrak{p}_{1}:=\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}D_{2,0}^{*}+\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}D_{1,0}^{*}
,
and
{\bf A}(v)=ke_{1}
.

(2)

For some
k>0
, we have
u_{\tau}=kD_{2,0}^{*}
,
{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(L_{\mathrm{out}})=\mathfrak{p}_{2}:=\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}D_{2,0}^{*}+\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}D_{1,\infty}^{*}
,
and
{\bf A}(v)=ke_{1}
.

(3)

For some
k>0
, we have
u_{\tau}=k(E_{2}^{*}-D_{1,\infty}^{*})
,

{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(L_{\mathrm{out}})=\mathfrak{p}_{3}:=\mathbb{R}_{\geq}E_{2}^{*}+\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}D_{1,\infty}^{*}
,
and
{\bf A}(v)=k(f-e_{1}-e_{2})
.

(4)

For some
k>0
, we have
u_{\tau}=kD_{2,\infty}^{*}
,

{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(L_{\mathrm{out}})=\mathfrak{p}_{4}:=\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}E_{2}^{*}+\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}D_{2,\infty}^{*}
,
and
{\bf A}(v)=k(f-e_{1})
.

(5)

For some
k>0
, we have
u_{\tau}=kD_{2,\infty}^{*}
,

{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(L_{\mathrm{out}})=\mathfrak{p}_{5}:=\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}D_{1,0}^{*}+\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}D_{2,\infty}^{*}
,
and
{\bf A}(v)=k(f-e_{1})
.

In all cases,
h(\tau_{\mathrm{out}})={\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(L_{\mathrm{out}})
, so walls only have five
possible supports. Further, in every case,
k_{\tau}=k
and

W_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}}=(-1)^{k-1}/k^{2}
. After
passing to an equivalent scattering diagram with only one wall with a
given support, we see, for example, that we have a wall

\left(\mathfrak{p}_{1},\exp\Big(\sum_{k>0}k{(-1)^{k-1}\over k^{2}}t^{ke_{1}}z^{-kD_{2,0}^{*}}\Big)\right)=\left(\mathfrak{p}_{1},1+t^{e_{1}}z^{-D_{2,0}^{*}}\right).

Similarly, we have four other walls:

\left(\mathfrak{p}_{2},1+t^{e_{1}}z^{-D_{2,0}^{*}}\right),\left(\mathfrak{p}_{3},1+t^{f-e_{1}-e_{2}}z^{D_{1,\infty}^{*}-E_{2}^{*}}\right),\left(\mathfrak{p}_{4},1+t^{f-e_{1}}z^{-D_{2,\infty}^{*}}\right),\left(\mathfrak{p}_{5},1+t^{f-e_{1}}z^{-D_{2,\infty}^{*}}\right).

Together, these walls cover the affine plane contained in

B
which is the union of all two-dimensional cones of
\mathscr{P}
not
containing
D_{3,0}^{*}
or
D_{3,\infty}^{*}
.
We omit a derivation of these results. Showing (1)–(5) are the only
possibilities is not difficult using
[
GS7
, Cor. 1.14]
to encode
balancing requirements for punctured maps. A direct calculation of

W_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}}
has not been carried out, but presumably these multiple
cover calculations can be carried out as in the two-dimensional
case of
[
GPS
, Prop. 5.2]
. Instead, the above formulas for the
wall functions are proved in a more general context in
[
AG
]
. In
fact, the formulas for these wall functions follows from the consistency
of
\mathscr{S}_{{\mathrm{can}}}
proved in the present paper.

The walls
\mathfrak{p}_{i}
for
i\not=3
arise from zero-dimensional strata
in one-dimensional moduli spaces of ordinary (non-punctured) stable
log maps. For example, the inclusion of
every fibre of
\pi|_{E_{1}}:E_{1}\rightarrow Z_{1}
into
X
may be viewed as a
stable log map. The walls
\mathfrak{p}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{p}_{2}
capture the curves in
this family which are degenerate with respect to the log structure
on
X
, i.e., fall into
D_{1,0}
or
D_{1,\infty}
. For
k>1
, we count
multiple covers of these fibres.

The same holds for
\mathfrak{p}_{4}
and
\mathfrak{p}_{5}
, with the curves in question
being, in general, strict transforms of curves of the form

\{a\}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\{1\}\subseteq\overline{X}
. Note as these
curves intersect
Z_{1}
at one point, the class of the strict transform
is indeed
f-e_{1}
. However, as
a\rightarrow\infty
, this curve
degenerates to a union
C=C_{1}\cup C_{2}
of two irreducible components,
of class
f-e_{1}-e_{2}
(the strict transform
C_{1}
of
\{\infty\}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\{1\}
) and of class
e_{2}
(the curve
C_{2}=\pi^{-1}(\infty,\infty,1)
).
It is this degenerate curve and its multiple covers which contribute
to
\mathfrak{p}_{4}
. Meanwhile the strict transform of

\{0\}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\{1\}
and its multiple covers
contribute to the wall
\mathfrak{p}_{5}
.

Finally,
\mathfrak{p}_{3}
arises not from a family of curves with one marked point
of positive contact order with the boundary, but from the punctured
log map whose image is
C_{1}
. This involves a negative contact order with
the divisor
D_{1,\infty}
, which contains
C_{1}
. This curve is rigid,
and if we did not include a wall for this curve, we would not get
a consistent scattering diagram. This shows how it is essential,
unlike in the case that
\dim X=2
, to take
into account punctured curves rather than just marked curves, as
without
\mathfrak{p}_{3}
,
\mathscr{S}_{{\mathrm{can}}}
would not be consistent.

Construction 3.16

(The torus action)
.

As with the canonical wall structure in two dimensions in
[
GHK
, §5]
,
there is a natural torus action on the mirror family constructed
using
\mathscr{S}_{{\mathrm{can}}}
in all dimensions. We refer the reader to

[
GHS
, §4.4]
for the general setup for torus actions in the
context of families of varieties built via wall structures, and do not
review the notation here.

In this case, there is an action of a torus with character lattice

\Gamma:=\operatorname{Div}_{D}(X),

i.e., the free abelian group generated by boundary divisors.
In the language of
[
GHS
, §4.4]
, the base ring
A
is taken
here to be the ground field
\Bbbk
. To specify the torus action,
we must specify the maps
\delta_{Q}
and
\delta_{B}
of the diagram

[
GHS
, (4.8)]
. Note that each irreducible component
D_{i}

of
D
defines an
\mathbb{R}
-valued PL function on
B
; indeed, by
construction
B\subseteq\operatorname{Div}_{D}(X)^{*}_{\mathbb{R}}
, and the linear functional

D_{i}
on this vector space restricts to a piecewise linear function
on
B
. We continue to denote this PL function as
D_{i}
. We may then define

\delta_{Q}:Q\longrightarrow\Gamma,\quad\quad\delta_{Q}(A)=\sum_{i}(D_{i}\cdot A)D_{i}

for
A\in Q
,
and

\delta_{B}:\operatorname{PL}(B)^{*}\longrightarrow\Gamma,\quad\quad\delta_{B}(\beta)=\sum_{i}\beta(D_{i})D_{i}

for
\beta\in\operatorname{PL}(B)^{*}
. Commutativity of
[
GHS
, (4.8)]
then
follows by direct computation. Indeed, note that in our current context,

Q_{0}
is the free monoid
with generators
e_{\rho}
,
e_{\rho}\in\mathscr{P}^{[n-1]}
, and
h:Q_{0}\rightarrow Q
is defined by

h(e_{\rho})=[X_{\rho}]
, the kink of our choice of MPL function

\varphi
along
\rho
. Then

\delta_{Q}(h(e_{\rho}))=\sum_{i}(D_{i}\cdot X_{\rho})D_{i}.

On the other hand,
g:Q_{0}\rightarrow\operatorname{PL}(B)^{*}
takes
e_{\rho}

to the linear functional on
\operatorname{PL}(B)
taking
\psi\in\operatorname{PL}(B)
to the
kink of
\psi
along
\rho
. It is easy to check from toric geometry
that the kink of
the PL function induced by
D_{i}
along
\rho
is
D_{i}\cdot X_{\rho}

(noting that if
D_{i}
is not good, it induces the zero function on
B
).
The claimed equality
\delta_{Q}\circ h=\delta_{B}\circ g
then follows.

Thus, by
[
GHS
, Thm. 4.17]
, it follows there is an induced

\operatorname{Spec}\Bbbk[\Gamma]
-action on the flat family
\check{\mathfrak{X}}\rightarrow\operatorname{Spec}(\Bbbk[Q]/I)
constructed from the wall structure
\mathscr{S}_{{\mathrm{can}}}

provided that
\mathscr{S}_{{\mathrm{can}}}
is homogeneous in the sense of

[
GHS
, Def. 4.16]
. For this, it is enough to check that
if
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}
is a decorated wall type with
W_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}}\not=0
and
total curve class
A
, then
\deg_{\Gamma}t^{A}z^{-u_{\tau}}=0
.
Here
\deg_{\Gamma}t^{A}=\delta_{Q}(A)=\sum_{i}(D_{i}\cdot A)D_{i}
.
If we write
u_{\tau}=\sum u_{i}D_{i}^{*}
as a tangent vector to

{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(L_{\mathrm{out}})
then
\deg_{\Gamma}(z^{-u_{\tau}})
is
computed from
[
GHS
, (4.9)]
as
-\sum_{i}u_{i}D_{i}
.
It then follows from
[
GS7
, Cor. 1.14]
that necessarily

u_{i}=D_{i}\cdot A
, and hence
\deg_{\Gamma}t^{A}z^{-u_{\tau}}=0
as
desired.

3.2.3.
The relative case

Construction 3.17
.

Suppose we are in the situation of Proposition

1.19
.
It is useful (see
[
AG
]
) to compare the wall structures
for
(X,D)
and
(X_{s},D_{s})
, which we do as follows.

Recall from that proposition that after extending the
affine structure on
B
across the interior of cells
\omega\in\mathscr{P}^{[n-2]}_{\partial}
,
\partial B
may be viewed as the
polyhedral affine pseudomanifold corresponding to the pair
(X_{s},D_{s})
.
As such, write
(\partial B)_{0}
for the complement in
\partial B

of the union of cones of
\mathscr{P}^{[n-3]}_{\partial}
.
Let
\Lambda_{\partial B}
denote the sheaf on
(\partial B)_{0}

of integral tangent vectors. We may view

\Lambda_{\partial B}
as a subsheaf of
\Lambda|_{\partial B}
, where the
latter sheaf is now extended across the interiors of cells

\omega\in\mathscr{P}^{[n-2]}_{\partial}
.

Further,
Proposition
1.19
, (3) then implies
that if
\mathcal{P}_{\partial B}
is defined using the MPL function

\varphi|_{\partial B}
on
\partial B
, we obtain a natural
inclusion
\mathcal{P}_{\partial B}\subseteq\mathcal{P}|_{\partial B}
.
Again, the latter sheaf is viewed as extending across the interiors of

\omega\in\mathscr{P}^{[n-2]}_{\partial}
. Note that
\mathcal{P}_{\partial B}

consists of those sections
m
of
\mathcal{P}|_{\partial B}
such that

\bar{m}
is tangent to
\partial B
.

On the other hand, we also have the MPL function
\varphi_{(X_{s},D_{s})}

taking values in
H_{2}(X_{s})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{R}
. This leads to a sheaf

\mathcal{P}_{(X_{s},D_{s})}
on
(\partial B)_{0}
, and the map
\iota:H_{2}(X_{s})\rightarrow H_{2}(X)
then induces a map

\iota_{*}:\mathcal{P}_{(X_{s},D_{s})}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}_{\partial B}.

This allows us to define a map, for
x\in(\partial B)_{0}
,

\iota_{*}:\Bbbk[\mathcal{P}_{(X_{s},D_{s}),x}^{+}]\rightarrow\Bbbk[\mathcal{P}_{\partial B,x}^{+}],

where
\mathcal{P}_{\partial B,x}^{+}:=\mathcal{P}_{\partial B,x}\cap\mathcal{P}^{+}_{x}
.
Finally, we may define

\iota(\mathscr{S}_{{\mathrm{can}},s}):=\big\{(\mathfrak{p},\iota_{*}(f_{\mathfrak{p}}))\,|\,(\mathfrak{p},f_{\mathfrak{p}})\in\mathscr{S}_{(X_{s},D_{s})}\big\}.

Like
\mathscr{S}_{{\mathrm{can}},s}
, this is a wall structure on
\partial B
.

On the other hand, we may define the

asymptotic wall structure
of the wall structure
\mathscr{S}_{{\mathrm{can}}}

on
B
by

(3.16)

\mathscr{S}^{\mathrm{as}}_{{\mathrm{can}}}:=\{(\mathfrak{p}\cap\partial B,f_{\mathfrak{p}})\,|\,\hbox{$(\mathfrak{p},f_{\mathfrak{p}})\in\mathscr{S}$ with $\dim\mathfrak{p}\cap\partial B=n-2$}\}.

We note that for each such wall,
f_{\mathfrak{p}}\in\Bbbk[\mathcal{P}^{+}_{\partial B,x}]\subseteq\Bbbk[\mathcal{P}^{+}_{x}]
. Indeed, this follows from
Proposition
3.7
, which implies that for
a wall type
\tau
for
X
,
u_{\tau}
is tangent to fibres
of
g_{\mathrm{trop}}
, and in particular tangent to
\partial B
.
Hence
\mathscr{S}^{\mathrm{as}}_{{\mathrm{can}}}

may be viewed as a wall structure on
\partial B
.

We then have:

Proposition 3.18
.

Suppose that all good divisors contained in
g^{-1}(0)
have
multiplicity one in
g^{-1}(0)
. Then
the wall structures
\iota(\mathscr{S}_{{\mathrm{can}},s})
and
\mathscr{S}^{\mathrm{as}}_{{\mathrm{can}}}

are equivalent.

Proof.

Note
\iota(\mathscr{S}_{{\mathrm{can}},s})
is equal to the canonical wall structure
for
(X_{s},D_{s})
constructed using the curve class group
H_{2}(X)
rather
than
H_{2}(X_{s})
.
In the proof, we generally use
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{\prime}
to denote a decorated wall type for

(X,D)
and
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}
for a decorated wall type for
(X_{s},D_{s})
, with
curve class decoration
{\bf A}
taking values in
H_{2}(X)
.

Given a decorated wall type
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{\prime}
, the corresponding cone
\tau^{\prime}

comes with a structure
map
p:\tau^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
coming from the fact the type
is defined over
S
. Note
p
is surjective as
u_{\tau}

is tangent to fibres of
g_{\mathrm{trop}}
by
Proposition
3.7
and

h_{\tau^{\prime}}(\tau^{\prime}_{\mathrm{out}})\not\subseteq\partial B

by Definition
3.6
,(3).
Then
p^{-1}(0)
is a proper face of
\tau^{\prime}
, and hence
corresponds to a contraction morphism
\phi:{\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{\prime}\rightarrow{\boldsymbol{\tau}}

of decorated types.
The type
\tau
is realizable provided that the length of
L_{\mathrm{out}}\in L(G^{\prime})

isn’t zero on the face
p^{-1}(0)
. Thus
\tau
realizable and

\dim\tau=n-3
is equivalent to

\dim\mathfrak{p}_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{\prime}}\cap\partial B=n-2
. From
this it is immediate that
\tau
is a wall type for
(X_{s},D_{s})

if and only if

\dim\mathfrak{p}_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{\prime}}\cap\partial B=n-2
.

Consider the sets

\displaystyle S^{\prime}:={}

\displaystyle\{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{\prime}\,|\,\hbox{${\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{\prime}$ is a decorated wall type for $X$
with $\dim\mathfrak{p}_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{\prime}}\cap\partial B=n-2$}\},

\displaystyle S:={}

\displaystyle\{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}\,|\,\hbox{${\boldsymbol{\tau}}$ is a decorated wall type for $X_{s}$}\}.

The discussion of the previous paragraph has constructed a map

\Phi:S^{\prime}\rightarrow S
. To show the desired equivalence of wall
structures, it is
enough to show that for
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}\in S
we have

(3.17)

k_{\tau}W_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}}=\sum_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{\prime}\in\Phi^{-1}({\boldsymbol{\tau}})}k_{\tau^{\prime}}W_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{\prime}}.

To do so, we use the decomposition setup of

[
ACGS2
, Thm. 5.21, Def. 5.22, Thm. 5.23]
.
Note that for
0\not=s\in S
,
s
is a trivial log point, so
in fact
\mathscr{M}(X_{s},{\boldsymbol{\tau}})=\mathscr{M}(X_{s}/s,{\boldsymbol{\tau}})
.
Thus in particular we have

W_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}}={\deg[\mathscr{M}(X_{s}/s,{\boldsymbol{\tau}})]^{\mathrm{virt}}\over|\operatorname{Aut}({\boldsymbol{\tau}})|}={\deg[\mathscr{M}(X_{0}/0,{\boldsymbol{\tau}})]^{\mathrm{virt}}\over|\operatorname{Aut}({\boldsymbol{\tau}})|},

the first equality by definition and the second equality by

[
ACGS2
, Thm. 5.23,(1)]
. Further, by

[
ACGS2
, Thm. 5.23,(2)]
,
we have

(3.18)

\deg[\mathscr{M}(X_{0}/0,{\boldsymbol{\tau}})]^{\mathrm{virt}}=\sum_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{\prime}}{m_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{\prime}}\over|\operatorname{Aut}({\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{\prime}/{\boldsymbol{\tau}})|}\deg[\mathscr{M}(X_{0}/0,{\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{\prime})]^{\mathrm{virt}},

where the sum is over codimension one degenerations

{\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{\prime}
of
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}
in the sense
of
[
ACGS2
, Def. 5.22,(2)]
, and
m_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{\prime}}
is the order of the
cokernel of
p_{*}:\Lambda_{\tau^{\prime}}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
. It is
immediate from that definition that if
\mathscr{M}(X_{0}/0,{\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{\prime})\not=\emptyset
then
such a
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{\prime}
is a decorated wall type for
X
. (We need to assume
non-emptiness to guarantee that
\tau^{\prime}
is balanced.)
It also follows from Proposition
3.7

and the proof of Lemma
3.9

that
\mathscr{M}(X_{0}/0,{\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{\prime})=\mathscr{M}(X/S,{\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{\prime})=\mathscr{M}(X,{\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{\prime})
. Thus we may
rewrite (
3.18
) as

k_{\tau}{\deg[\mathscr{M}(X_{0}/0,{\boldsymbol{\tau}})]^{\mathrm{virt}}\over|\operatorname{Aut}({\boldsymbol{\tau}})|}=\sum_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{\prime}}{m_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{\prime}}k_{\tau}\over|\operatorname{Aut}({\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{\prime}/{\boldsymbol{\tau}})||\operatorname{Aut}({\boldsymbol{\tau}})|}\deg[\mathscr{M}(X,{\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{\prime})]^{\mathrm{virt}}.

Certainly,
|\operatorname{Aut}({\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{\prime}/{\boldsymbol{\tau}})||\operatorname{Aut}({\boldsymbol{\tau}})|=|\operatorname{Aut}({\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{\prime})|
.
Further,
\Lambda_{\tau^{\prime}_{\mathrm{out}}}=\Lambda_{\tau_{\mathrm{out}}}\oplus\mathbb{Z}
and

p_{*}(\Lambda_{\tau^{\prime}_{\mathrm{out}}})=m_{\tau^{\prime}}\mathbb{Z}
.
Next
\Lambda_{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{\prime}(L_{\mathrm{out}})}=\Lambda_{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(L_{\mathrm{out}})}\oplus\mathbb{Z}
,
with the induced map
g_{\mathrm{trop},*}:\Lambda_{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{\prime}(L_{\mathrm{out}})}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
being surjective because of the assumption that

g^{-1}(0)
is reduced.
Thus an elementary diagram chase gives a short exact sequence

0\rightarrow\operatorname{coker}(\Lambda_{\tau_{\mathrm{out}}}\rightarrow\Lambda_{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(L_{\mathrm{out}})})\rightarrow\operatorname{coker}(\Lambda_{\tau^{\prime}_{\mathrm{out}}}\rightarrow\Lambda_{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{\prime}(L_{\mathrm{out}})})\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}/m_{\tau^{\prime}}\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow 0,

so
m_{\tau^{\prime}}k_{\tau}=k_{\tau^{\prime}}
, giving
(
3.17
).
∎

3.3.
Logarithmic broken lines and theta functions

Wall types defined in the previous subsection allowed
us to define the canonical wall structure. Consistency
of a wall structure is defined partly via
broken lines

(reviewed in Definition
4.2
). A key point of
our proof of consistency is a correspondence result that
associates counts of broken lines to certain punctured invariants.
In this subsection, we shall define these punctured invariants.
We proceed quite analogously with the notion of wall types.

Definition 3.19
.

A
(non-trivial) broken line type
is a
type
\tau=(G,{\boldsymbol{\sigma}},{\mathbf{u}})

of tropical map to
\Sigma(X)
such that:

(1)

G
is a genus zero graph with
L(G)=\{L_{\mathrm{in}},L_{\mathrm{out}}\}
with

{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(L_{\mathrm{out}})\in\mathscr{P}
and

u_{\tau}:={\bf u}(L_{\mathrm{out}})\not=0,\quad p_{\tau}:={\bf u}(L_{\mathrm{in}})\in{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(L_{\mathrm{in}})\setminus\{0\}

(so that
L_{\mathrm{in}}
represents a marked rather than punctured point).

(2)

\tau
is realizable and balanced.

(3)

Let
h:\Gamma(G,\ell)\rightarrow\Sigma(X)
be the corresponding
universal family of tropical maps, and let
\tau_{\mathrm{out}}\in\Gamma(G,\ell)

be the cone corresponding to
L_{\mathrm{out}}
. Then
\dim\tau=n-1
and

\dim h(\tau_{\mathrm{out}})=n
.

We also consider the possibility of a

trivial broken line type

\tau
,
which is not an actual type: the underlying graph
G
consists
of just one leg
L_{\mathrm{in}}=L_{\mathrm{out}}
and no vertices, with the convention
that
u_{\tau}=-p_{\tau}
. Note this does not correspond to
an actual punctured curve.
8
8

8

Such a broken line type will correspond, as discussed in
§
4
, to a broken line contained entirely in

{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(L_{\mathrm{in}})

and which does not bend.

A
decorated broken line type
is a decorated type

{\boldsymbol{\tau}}=(\tau,\mathbf{A})
with
\tau
a broken line type.
In the trivial case, as there are no vertices, this does not
involve any extra information, and in this case, the total curve class
is taken to be
0
.

A
degenerate broken line type
is a type
\tau
which satisfies
conditions (1) and (2) above and instead of (3),

(3^{\prime})

\dim\tau=n-2
and
\dim h(\tau_{\mathrm{out}})=n-1
.

Lemma 3.20
.

Let
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}
be a non-trivial decorated broken line type. Then

\mathscr{M}(X,{\boldsymbol{\tau}})
is
proper over
\operatorname{Spec}\Bbbk
and carries a virtual fundamental class of
dimension zero.

Proof.

The argument is essentially identical to that of the proof of
Lemma
3.9
, and we leave it to the reader
to make the necessary modifications, except for one point.
In that proof, we appealed to Proposition
3.7

to argue that in the relative case, for each
v\in V(G)
, we have

\Sigma(g):h(\tau_{v})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
surjective. Crucially,
a broken line type is not in general a type of tropical map to

X/S
, so a different argument is necessary. However, note that by
the assumption that
\tau
is a balanced type,
for any
s\in\operatorname{Int}(\tau)
,
\Sigma(g)\circ h_{s}:G\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}

is a balanced tropical map. Necessarily
\Sigma(g)_{*}(\mathbf{u}(L_{\mathrm{in}}))=\Sigma(g)(p_{\tau})\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, and hence by balancing,

\Sigma(g)_{*}(u_{\tau})\in\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}
. This implies, again by
balancing, that if
v_{\mathrm{in}}
,
v_{\mathrm{out}}
are the vertices adjacent
to
L_{\mathrm{in}}
,
L_{\mathrm{out}}
respectively, then
a=\Sigma(g)\circ h_{s}(v_{\mathrm{out}})\leq\Sigma(g)\circ h_{s}(v_{\mathrm{in}})=b
, and for any other vertex

v\in V(G)
,
\Sigma(g)\circ h_{s}(v)\in[a,b]
. Further, since

\dim h(\tau_{\mathrm{out}})=n
, necessarily
h(\tau_{\mathrm{out}})\not\subseteq\partial B
, and thus
\Sigma(g):h(\tau_{\mathrm{out}})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
is
surjective, and so
\Sigma(g):h(\tau_{v_{\mathrm{out}}})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}

is also surjective. Putting this together, we see that

\Sigma(g):h(\tau_{v})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
is surjective for
any vertex
v\in V(G)
. This is sufficient to complete the argument
of the proof of Proposition
3.9
for properness
of the moduli spaces involved.
∎

We now define, for
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}
a non-trivial decorated broken line type,

(3.19)

N_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}}:={\deg[\mathscr{M}(X,{\boldsymbol{\tau}})]^{\mathrm{virt}}\over|\operatorname{Aut}({\boldsymbol{\tau}})|}.

We also have a map
h_{*}:\Lambda_{\tau_{\mathrm{out}}}\rightarrow\Lambda_{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(L_{\mathrm{out}})}
, necessarily of finite index, and
define

(3.20)

k_{\tau}:=|\operatorname{coker}h_{*}|=|\Lambda_{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(L_{\mathrm{out}})}/h_{*}(\Lambda_{\tau_{\mathrm{out}}})|.

For a trivial decorated broken line type
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}
, we set

N_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}}:=1,\quad k_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}}:=1.

Definition 3.21
.

Fix
p\in B(\mathbb{Z})\setminus\{0\}
,
\sigma\in\mathscr{P}_{{\operatorname{max}}}
,

x\in\operatorname{Int}(\sigma)
not contained in any rationally defined hyperplane
in
\sigma
. We then define

\vartheta^{\log}_{p}(x)=\sum_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}}k_{\tau}N_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}}t^{A}z^{-u_{\tau}}\in\Bbbk[\mathcal{P}^{+}_{x}]/I_{x}

where the sum is over all isomorphism classes of
decorated broken line types
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}
with

p_{\tau}=p
,
x\in h(\tau_{\mathrm{out}})
. Here

A
is the total curve class of
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}
, and we require
A\in Q\setminus I
.

Lemma 3.22
.

For
p\in B(\mathbb{Z})\setminus\{0\}
,
the number of decorated broken line types
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}
with
p_{\tau}=p
,
total curve class in
Q\setminus I
, and
N_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}}\not=0
is finite. In
particular, the sum defining
\vartheta^{\log}_{p}(x)
is finite.

Proof.

As
Q\setminus I
is finite, there are only a finite number of total
curve classes. Further, there are only a finite number of possibilities
for
{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(L_{\mathrm{out}})
. For each curve class
A
and choice
of
{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(L_{\mathrm{out}})
, it follows from

[
GS7
, Cor. 1.14]
that
u_{\tau}
is determined by
p

if
\mathscr{M}(X,{\boldsymbol{\tau}})
is non-empty. If
\beta
is the punctured curve
class given by contact orders
p
and
u_{\tau}
and curve
class
A
, then
\mathscr{M}(X,\beta)
is of finite type,
and there are only a finite number of decorated types
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}

appearing as the types of tropicalizations of punctured maps
in
\mathscr{M}(X,\beta)
. Thus there are only a finite number of possible
choices of
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}
with given total curve class
A
and

{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(L_{\mathrm{out}})
satisfying
\mathbf{u}(L_{\mathrm{in}})=p

and
\mathbf{u}(L_{\mathrm{out}})=u_{\tau}
.
∎

4.
A correspondence theorem for broken lines

4.1.
Broken lines and statement of the correspondence theorem

We first recall the definition of
broken lines

[
GHS
, Def. 3.3]
for a wall structure
\mathscr{S}
on
B
.
We continue to work with
the specific choice of
(B,\mathscr{P})
derived from
(X,D)
, a monoid

Q\subset H_{2}(X)
and monoid ideal
I\subset Q
with
Q\setminus I

finite as usual.

Definition 4.1
.

Let
\gamma:(a,b)\rightarrow B_{0}
be a path with

t\in(a,b)
such that
\gamma(t)\in|\mathscr{S}|\setminus\operatorname{Sing}(\mathscr{S})
.
Let
n\in\check{\Lambda}_{\gamma(t)}

be a primitive cotangent vector annihilating the tangent space to

|\mathscr{S}|
at
\gamma(t)
. Thinking of
n
as defining a linear function
locally near
\gamma(t)
which is zero along
|\mathscr{S}|
, we assume
further that
n
is positive on

\gamma(t-\epsilon,t)
and negative on
\gamma(t,t+\epsilon)
for some

\epsilon>0
. Suppose
\gamma(t-\epsilon)\in\sigma
,
\gamma(t+\epsilon)\in\sigma^{\prime}
with
\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}\in\mathscr{P}_{{\operatorname{max}}}
.
Given an expression
az^{m}
with
a\in\Bbbk[Q]/I
,
m\in\Lambda_{\sigma}
,
\langle m,n\rangle>0
,
we define a
result of
transport along
\gamma
of
az^{m}
to be
a_{i}z^{m_{i}}

chosen as follows. Write

f_{\gamma(t)}^{\langle n,m\rangle}\cdot\mathfrak{t}_{\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}}(az^{m})=\sum_{i}a_{i}z^{m_{i}}

inside
(\Bbbk[Q]/I)[\Lambda_{\sigma^{\prime}}]
,
with the
m_{i}\in\Lambda_{\sigma^{\prime}}
mutually distinct.
We then take
a_{i}z^{m_{i}}
to be one of the terms in this sum.
Here
\mathfrak{t}
is as defined in (
3.7
)
and
f_{\gamma(t)}
is as defined in (
3.8
), which
may be viewed via transport as an element of

(\Bbbk[Q]/I)[\Lambda_{\sigma^{\prime}}]
.

Definition 4.2
.

A
broken line
for a wall structure
\mathscr{S}
on
(B,\mathscr{P})

is a proper continuous map

\beta:(-\infty,0]\rightarrow B_{0}

with image disjoint from
\operatorname{Sing}(\mathscr{S})
, along with a sequence

-\infty=t_{0}<t_{1}<\cdots<t_{r}=0
for some
r\geq 1
with
\beta(t_{i})\in|\mathscr{S}|
for
1\leq i\leq r-1
, and for each
i=1,\ldots,r
an
expression
a_{i}z^{m_{i}}
with
a_{i}\in\Bbbk[Q]/I
,
m_{i}\in\Lambda_{\beta(t)}

for any
t\in(t_{i-1},t_{i})
, subject to the following conditions:

(1)

\beta|_{(t_{i-1},t_{i})}
is a non-constant affine map with
image disjoint from
|\mathscr{S}|
, hence contained in the interior
of some
\sigma\in\mathscr{P}_{{\operatorname{max}}}
, and

\beta^{\prime}(t)=-m_{i}
for all
t\in(t_{i-1},t_{i})
.

(2)

For each
i=1,\ldots,r-1
the expression

a_{i+1}z^{m_{i+1}}
is a result of transport of
a_{i}z^{m_{i}}

along
\beta|_{(t_{i-1},t_{i+1})}
.

(3)

a_{1}=1
.

The
asymptotic monomial
of
\beta
is
m_{1}
. Note that
since
\beta((-\infty,t_{1}])
is an affine half-line contained in
a cone
\sigma\in\mathscr{P}
, we may identify
m_{1}
with an integral point
of
\sigma
, and hence view
m_{1}\in B(\mathbb{Z})
.
