Given a broken line
\beta
, we write
a_{\beta}=a_{r}
,
m_{\beta}=m_{r}
.

Remark 4.3
.

This only differs in one way from
[
GHS
, Def. 3.3]
, namely
the requirement that
a_{1}=1
.
In
[
GHS
]
, broken lines satisfying this additional condition are
called
normalized
broken lines.
As we never use any other type of broken line here, we include
this in the definition.

Broken lines define theta functions:

Definition 4.4
.

Fix
p\in B(\mathbb{Z})\setminus\{0\}
,
\sigma\in\mathscr{P}_{{\operatorname{max}}}
,
x\in\operatorname{Int}(\sigma)

not contained in any rationally defined hyperplane in
\sigma
.
We then define

\vartheta_{p}(x)=\sum_{\beta}a_{\beta}z^{m_{\beta}}\in\Bbbk[\mathcal{P}_{x}^{+}]/I_{x},

where the sum is over all broken lines
\beta
with asymptotic
monomial
p
and
\beta(0)=x
.

We note this sum is finite, e.g., by
[
GHS
, Lem. 3.7]
.

The main result of this section may now be stated as:

Theorem 4.5
.

For
p\in B(\mathbb{Z})\setminus\{0\}
,
\sigma\in\mathscr{P}_{{\operatorname{max}}}
,
x\in\operatorname{Int}(\sigma)

not contained in any rationally defined hyperplane, we have

\vartheta_{p}(x)=\vartheta^{\log}_{p}(x).

Before proving this theorem, we first recast it as a tropical correspondence
theorem between broken lines and punctured log curves.
To do so, we need to deal with a certain amount of book-keeping. To
this end, we first refine the notion of broken line. This
refinement keeps track of which wall types cause the bending of the
broken line.

Definition 4.6
.

A
decorated broken line
for
\mathscr{S}_{{\mathrm{can}}}
is a proper
continuous map

\beta:(-\infty,0]\rightarrow B_{0}

with image disjoint from
\operatorname{Sing}(\mathscr{S}_{{\mathrm{can}}})
, along with a sequence

-\infty=t_{0}<t_{1}<\cdots<t_{r}=0
for some
r\geq 1
with
\beta(t_{i})\in|\mathscr{S}_{{\mathrm{can}}}|
for
1\leq i\leq r-1
, along with additional data:

(i)

For each
i=1,\ldots,r
an
expression
a_{i}z^{m_{i}}
with
a_{i}\in\Bbbk[Q]/I
,
m_{i}\in\Lambda_{\beta(t)}

for any
t\in(t_{i-1},t_{i})
.

(ii)

For each
i=1,\ldots,r-1
, let
\mathscr{S}_{i}:=\{\mathfrak{p}_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}}\in\mathscr{S}_{{\mathrm{can}}}\,|\,\beta(t_{i})\in\mathfrak{p}_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}}\}
.
Then we are given in addition a function

\mu_{i}:\mathscr{S}_{i}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
.

This data is subject to the following conditions:

(1)

\beta|_{(t_{i-1},t_{i})}
is a non-constant affine map, and

\beta^{\prime}(t)=-m_{i}
for all
t\in(t_{i-1},t_{i})
.

(2)

Define the
support
of
\mu_{i}
to
be the subset of
\mathscr{S}_{i}

on which
\mu_{i}
takes non-zero value. Then the support of
\mu_{i}

must be finite, and non-empty if
\beta(t_{i})
lies in the interior
of a maximal cone of
\mathscr{P}
. Further, if
\beta(t)\in\rho\in\mathscr{P}^{[n-1]}
,
then
t=t_{i}
for some
i
.

(3)

For each
i=1,\ldots,r-1
, if
\beta(t_{i}-\epsilon)\in\sigma
,

\beta(t_{i}+\epsilon)\in\sigma^{\prime}
, we have

(4.1)

a_{i+1}z^{m_{i+1}}=\mathfrak{t}_{\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}}(a_{i}z^{m_{i}})\prod_{\mathfrak{p}_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}}\in\mathscr{S}_{i}}{(\langle n_{i},m_{i}\rangle k_{\tau}W_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}}t^{A}z^{-u_{\tau}})^{\mu_{i}(\mathfrak{p}_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}})}\over\mu_{i}(\mathfrak{p}_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}})!}.

Here
n_{i}\in\check{\Lambda}_{\beta(t_{i})}
is primitive,
vanishing on the tangent space to
|\mathscr{S}_{{\mathrm{can}}}|
at

\beta(t_{i})
, and positive on
m_{i}
.

(4)

a_{1}=1
.

Remark 4.7
.

There are a couple of points of distinction between the above
definition and Definition
4.2
. First, it is possible
that
\beta(t)\in|\mathscr{S}_{{\mathrm{can}}}|
with
t\not=t_{i}
for
any
i
provided
\beta(t)
lies in the interior of a maximal cell.
Second, the data of the
\mu_{i}
completely determines the change
of monomial. To explain the expression
(
4.1
),
note that by the description (
3.13
) of
\mathscr{S}_{{\mathrm{can}}}
,

f_{\beta(t_{i})}^{\langle n_{i},m_{i}\rangle}=\exp\left(\sum_{\mathfrak{p}_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}}\in\mathscr{S}_{i}}\langle n_{i},m_{i}\rangle k_{\tau}W_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}}t^{A}z^{-u_{\tau}}\right)=\sum_{\mu_{i}}\prod_{\mathfrak{p}_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}}\in\mathscr{S}_{i}}{(\langle n_{i},m_{i}\rangle k_{\tau}W_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}}t^{A}z^{-u_{\tau}})^{\mu_{i}(\mathfrak{p}_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}})}\over\mu_{i}(\mathfrak{p}_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}})!}

where the sum is over all functions
\mu_{i}:\mathscr{S}_{i}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}

with finite support. Thus
(
4.1
) arises from one
of the summands, and if
\beta(t_{i})
falls in the interior
of a maximal cell, we do not allow the trivial summand
1
(with
\mu_{i}\equiv 0
). This would correspond, in Definition
4.2
,
to a situation where
a_{i}z^{m_{i}}=a_{i+1}z^{m_{i+1}}
.

It is then clear that we have a refined description

\vartheta_{p}(x)=\sum_{\beta}a_{\beta}z^{m_{\beta}}\in\Bbbk[\mathcal{P}^{+}_{x}]/I_{x}

as a sum over decorated broken lines with asymptotic monomial
p\in B(\mathbb{Z})
and
\beta(0)=x
. Each term in the summation
of Definition
4.4
is now
split up into a sum over a number of decorated broken lines.

Construction 4.8
.

We may now associate to a decorated broken line
\beta
an
isomorphism class of a decorated broken line type
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{\beta}=(G_{\beta},{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\beta},{\bf u}_{\beta},{\bf A}_{\beta})
.
This is done as follows.

First, we adopt the following notation.
For each
\mathfrak{p}_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}}\in\mathscr{S}_{i}
,
write
(G_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}},{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}},{\bf u}_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}})

and
{\bf A}_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}}
for the data giving the decorated type

{\boldsymbol{\tau}}
. Let
L_{\mathrm{out},{\boldsymbol{\tau}}}\in L(G_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}})
be the unique leg, adjacent
to a unique
v_{\mathrm{out},{\boldsymbol{\tau}}}\in V(G_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}})
.

The graph
G_{\beta}
has a spine comprising
of the legs
L_{\mathrm{in}}
and
L_{\mathrm{out}}

and the edges
E_{1},\ldots,E_{r-2}
and their vertices

v_{1},\ldots,v_{r-1}
, with the vertices of
E_{i}
being
v_{i}
,
v_{i+1}

and the vertices of
L_{\mathrm{in}}
and
L_{\mathrm{out}}
being
v_{1}

and
v_{r-1}
respectively. At the vertex
v_{i}
we glue

\mu_{i}(\mathfrak{p}_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}})
copies of
G_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}}
to
v_{i}
along the unique
leg of
G_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}}
.

Next, we define
{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\beta}
as follows.
We define
{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\beta}(v_{i})
or
{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\beta}(E_{i})
to be the minimal
cone of
\mathscr{P}
containing
\beta(t_{i})
or
\beta((t_{i},t_{i+1}))

respectively, and
{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\beta}(L_{\mathrm{in}})
and
{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\beta}(L_{\mathrm{out}})

to be the minimal cones containing

\beta((-\infty,t_{1}))
or
\beta((t_{r-1},t_{r}))
respectively.
Further, for each
i
,
\mathfrak{p}_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}}\in\mathscr{S}_{i}
, we may view
each of the
\mu_{i}(\mathfrak{p}_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}})
copies
of
G_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}}

as a subgraph of
G_{\beta}
, and we take
{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\beta}
to
agree with
{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}}
on each copy of
G_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}}
.

To define
\mathbf{u}_{\beta}
, we again take it to agree with

\mathbf{u}_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}}
on any copy of
G_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}}
,
and take
\mathbf{u}_{\beta}(E_{i})=-m_{i+1}

with
E_{i}
oriented from
v_{i}
to
v_{i+1}
. We also set

\mathbf{u}_{\beta}(L_{\mathrm{in}})=m_{1}
,
\mathbf{u}_{\beta}(L_{\mathrm{out}})=-m_{r}
(using
the standard convention that legs are oriented away from their vertex).

This defines the global type
\tau_{\beta}
. For the decoration

{\bf A}_{\beta}
, it similarly agrees with
{\bf A}_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}}

on each copy of
G_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}}
.
If
v=v_{i}
,
we take
{\mathbf{A}}_{\beta}(v_{i})=0
if
\beta(t_{i})
lies in the interior
of a maximal cell of
\mathscr{P}
, but if
\beta(t_{i})\in\operatorname{Int}(\rho)

with
\rho\in\mathscr{P}^{[n-1]}
, then we take

{\mathbf{A}}_{\beta}(v_{i})=\langle n_{i},m_{i}\rangle[X_{\rho}]\in H_{2}(X)
.

Lemma 4.9
.

Let
\beta
be a decorated broken line with
x=\beta(0)\in\operatorname{Int}(\sigma)
,

\sigma\in\mathscr{P}_{{\operatorname{max}}}
,
x
not contained in any rationally defined
hyperplane. Then
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{\beta}
is a decorated broken line type.

Proof.

Condition (1) of Definition
3.19
is
immediate.

For condition (2), we first show
\tau_{\beta}
is realizable, i.e.,
there is a tropical map
h:G_{\beta}\rightarrow\Sigma(X)
of type

\tau_{\beta}
. On the spine
G^{\prime}_{\beta}
of
G_{\beta}
,
consisting of vertices
v_{1},\ldots,v_{r-1}
, edges
E_{1},\ldots,E_{r-2}
and legs
L_{\mathrm{in}},L_{\mathrm{out}}
,
we define
h
to agree with
\beta
, extending
\beta

linearly on
L_{\mathrm{out}}
if necessary.
For each
i
and for each
\mathfrak{p}_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}}\in\mathscr{S}_{i}
,
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}

is a wall type, and hence is realizable with an
n-2
-dimensional
universal family with output leg tracing out the wall
\mathfrak{p}_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}}
.
Further,
\beta(t_{i})\in\operatorname{Int}(\mathfrak{p}_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}})
.
Thus there is a unique tropical map

h_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}}:G_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}}\rightarrow\Sigma(X)
of type
\tau
with
\beta(t_{i})\in h_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}}(L_{\mathrm{out},{\boldsymbol{\tau}}})
. Thus we may define
h
restricted
to any copy of
G_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}}
in
G_{\beta}
to be given by
h_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}}
,
appropriately truncating the edge
L_{\mathrm{out},{\boldsymbol{\tau}}}
as necessary.
This shows realizability.

Next, we observe that
\tau_{\beta}
is balanced. Because this is
true for each wall type
\tau
appearing in
\tau_{\beta}
, this
only needs to be checked at the vertices
v_{i}
, but such balancing
follows from (
4.1
).
Indeed, by considering exponents,
that equation tells us that

m_{i+1}=m_{i}-\sum_{\mathfrak{p}_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}}\in\mathscr{S}_{i}}\mu_{i}(\mathfrak{p}_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}})u_{\tau},

which is the balancing condition at
v_{i}
given the specified contact
orders in Construction
4.8
of edges
adjacent to
v_{i}
.
In the relative case, we also need to verify that
\Sigma(g)\circ h

is balanced as a map to
\Sigma(S)=\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
. However, this again
holds at all vertices of
G_{\beta}
except for the
v_{i}
’s because
the same is assumed true of the wall types
\tau
. At a vertex
v_{i}
,
the result follows from the balancing in
B
and the fact (Lemma

1.15
) that
g_{\mathrm{trop}}
is an affine submersion.

For condition (3),
observe that, by elementary convex geometry,

\beta
varies in an
n
-dimensional family: each
\beta(t_{i})

is constrained to live in an
n-1
-dimensional subspace, and the
location of
\beta(t_{1})
and the
m_{i}
completely determine the
map
\beta
, up to the location of
\beta(0)
, which gives an
extra parameter. In particular, the dimension of the universal
family of tropical maps of type
\tau_{\beta}
is then clearly

n-1
, and
h(\tau_{\beta,\mathrm{out}})
is
n
-dimensional.
This shows
\tau_{\beta}
is a broken line type.
∎

We note that the broken line type
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{\beta}
had a very
specific form for the curve classes associated to the vertices
v_{i}
.
We codify this as follows:

Definition 4.10
.

Let
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}=(G,{\boldsymbol{\sigma}},{\mathbf{u}},{\mathbf{A}})

be a decorated broken line type with
spine
G^{\prime}\subseteq G
(see Definition
2.4
),
the latter having vertices

v_{1},\ldots,v_{r-1}
and edges
E_{i}
with vertices
v_{i},v_{i+1}
.
By Lemma
2.5
,
{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(v_{i})\in\mathscr{P}^{[n]}\cup\mathscr{P}^{[n-1]}
. We say
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}
is
admissible

if
\mathbf{A}(v_{i})=0
whenever
{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(v_{i})\in\mathscr{P}^{[n]}
,
and
\mathbf{A}(v_{i})=|\langle n_{i},\mathbf{u}(E_{i})\rangle|[X_{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(v_{i})}]
whenever
{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(v_{i})\in\mathscr{P}^{[n-1]}
,
where
n_{i}\in\check{\Lambda}_{x}
for
x\in\operatorname{Int}({\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(v_{i}))
is a choice
of primitive normal vector to
{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(v_{i})
.

By construction, if
\beta
is a decorated broken line, then

{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{\beta}
is admissible. The following is immediate from
Lemma
2.3
:

Lemma 4.11
.

Let
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}
be a decorated broken line type. Then
N_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}}\not=0

implies that
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}
is admissible.

We now reverse the procedure of
Construction
4.8

and go from decorated broken line types
to decorated broken lines.

Construction 4.12
.

Let
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}=(G,{\boldsymbol{\sigma}},{\bf u},{\bf A})
be a decorated broken line type,

h
the universal tropical map parameterized by the cone
\tau
, and

x\in\operatorname{Int}(h(\tau_{\mathrm{out}}))
.
We may construct a broken line
\beta_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}},x}
as follows. There
exists a unique
s\in\operatorname{Int}(\tau)
such that

x\in h_{s}(L_{\mathrm{out}})
. Let
G^{\prime}
be the spine
of
G
.
Label the vertices of
G^{\prime}
as
v_{1},\ldots,v_{r-1}

with
L_{\mathrm{in}}
adjacent to
v_{1}
,
L_{\mathrm{out}}
adjacent to
v_{r-1}
,
and edges
E_{1},\ldots,E_{r-2}
, with
E_{i}
having vertices
v_{i}
and
v_{i+1}
.
Shrink
L_{\mathrm{out}}
so that the non-vertex endpoint of
L_{\mathrm{out}}

maps to
x
under
h_{s}
.
We may then choose an identification
\psi:\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}\rightarrow G^{\prime}

with the following property. Let
t_{i}\in\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}
satisfy

\psi(t_{i})=v_{i}
,
1\leq i\leq r-1
, take
t_{r}=0
,
and set
\beta_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}},x}=h_{s}|_{G^{\prime}}\circ\psi
. We require that
condition (1) of Definition

4.6
is satisfied for
\beta_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}},x}
with

m_{i}=-\mathbf{u}(E_{i})
, with
E_{i}
oriented from

v_{i}
to
v_{i+1}
. Note by construction that
\beta_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}},x}(0)=x
.

We now specify the decorations, notably the functions
\mu_{i}
.
Let
G_{i1},\ldots,G_{is_{i}}

be the closures of the connected components of
G\setminus\{v_{i}\}

not containing
L_{\mathrm{in}}
or
L_{\mathrm{out}}
. These are the graphs
attached to
v_{i}
but only intersecting
G^{\prime}
at
v_{i}
. We view
the edge of
G_{ij}
adjacent to the vertex
v_{i}
as a leg

L_{ij,\mathrm{out}}
of

G_{ij}
, so that
v_{i}
is not a vertex of
G_{ij}
.
This gives rise to decorated types
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{i1},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{is_{i}}

by restriction of
{\boldsymbol{\sigma}},\mathbf{u},\mathbf{A}
to each graph
G_{ij}
.

We claim that each
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{ij}
is a wall type.
Indeed, condition (1) of Definition
3.6

is immediate except for the statement that
\mathbf{u}(L_{ij,\mathrm{out}})\not=0
,
which we rule out below. For condition (2), note
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{ij}

is realizable and balanced since
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}
is realizable and balanced.

For condition (3), let
h_{ij}:\Gamma(G_{ij},\ell_{ij})\rightarrow\Sigma(X)
be the universal tropical map of type

{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{ij}
, defined over the cone
\tau_{ij}
.
First note that necessarily
h(\tau_{v_{i}})\subseteq h_{ij}(\tau_{ij,\mathrm{out}})
, and by
Lemma
2.5
, (2),
\dim h(\tau_{v_{i}})=n-1
.
Thus
\dim h_{ij}(\tau_{ij,\mathrm{out}})\geq n-1
. However
by Lemma
2.5
, (1), still assuming that

\mathbf{u}(L_{ij,\mathrm{out}})\not=0
,
\dim h_{ij}(\tau_{ij,\mathrm{out}})\leq n-1

and thus this dimension is
n-1
, as desired.

Suppose now that
\dim\tau_{ij}>n-2
.
Thus for any fixed
s^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Int}(\tau)
,
there is a positive dimensional subset
\omega\subseteq\tau_{ij}
such that for
s^{\prime\prime}\in\omega
,
h_{s^{\prime}}(v_{i})\in h_{ij,s^{\prime\prime}}(L_{ij,\mathrm{out}})
,
where
L_{ij,\mathrm{out}}
is the unique leg of
G_{ij}
. Thus
for each point
y\in h(\tau_{v_{i}})
, there is a positive dimensional
family of tropical maps of type
\tau
taking
v_{i}
to
y
.
Again since
\dim h(\tau_{v_{i}})=n-1
, this shows
\dim\tau\geq n
,
a contradiction.

We now eliminate the case that
{\bf u}(L_{ij,\mathrm{out}})=0
. Indeed,
if this is the case and
v
is the unique vertex of
L_{ij,\mathrm{out}}

in
G_{ij}
, then
h_{ij}(\tau_{ij,v})=h_{ij}(\tau_{ij,L_{ij,\mathrm{out}}})
,
and this is again
n-1
-dimensional as above. Thus
\dim\tau_{ij}\geq n-1
.
On the other hand, if
E_{ij}
is the edge of
G
corresponding to
the leg
L_{ij,\mathrm{out}}
of
G_{ij}
, we have
{\bf u}(E_{ij})={\bf u}(L_{ij,\mathrm{out}})=0
, so the length
of
E_{ij}
is arbitrary, and thus we obtain again that
\dim\tau\geq n
. Thus we conclude that
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{ij}
is a wall type.

We may now define
\mu_{i}:\mathscr{S}_{i}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
by
defining, for
\mathfrak{p}_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{\prime}}\in\mathscr{S}_{i}
,

\mu_{i}(\mathfrak{p}_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{\prime}})=|\{j\,|\,{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{ij}\simeq{\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{\prime}\}|
.

Having defined the
\mu_{i}
, (
4.1
)
defines the monomials
a_{i}z^{m_{i}}
. There is one thing to check:
as we have already defined
m_{i}
in terms of
{\bf u}
, we need
to check that (
4.1
) yields
the same values for
m_{i}
. However, this is immediate inductively
by balancing at the vertices
v_{i}
.

Hence we have constructed a decorated broken line
\beta_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}}
.

Constructions
4.8
and

4.12
now immediately give:

Proposition 4.13
.

Given
x\in B_{0}
not
contained in any rationally defined hyperplane
and
p\in B(\mathbb{Z})\setminus\{0\}
,
there is a one-to-one correspondence

\displaystyle\{\beta\,|\,\hbox{
$\beta$ is a decorated broken line with endpoint $x$ and asymptotic
monomial $p$}\}

\displaystyle\leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\left\{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}\,\Big|\,\begin{subarray}{c}\hbox{${\boldsymbol{\tau}}$ is an isomorphism
class of admissible decorated}\\
\hbox{broken line
types with $x\in\operatorname{Int}(h(\tau_{\mathrm{out}}))$ and $p_{\tau}=p$
}\end{subarray}\right\}

given by
\beta\mapsto{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{\beta}
,
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}\mapsto\beta_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}},x}
.

The key result, to be proved via gluing of punctured curves in
the next subsection, is then:

Theorem 4.14
.

Let
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}
be an admissible decorated broken line type, and let

\beta_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}},x}
be the corresponding broken line with
endpoint
x\in\operatorname{Int}(h(\tau_{\mathrm{out}}))
. Then we have

a_{\beta_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}},x}}z^{m_{\beta_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}},x}}}=k_{\tau}N_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}}t^{A}z^{-u_{\tau}},

where
A
is the total degree of
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}
.

Proof of Theorem
4.5

given Theorem
4.14
.

This follows immediately from the definitions
Definition
4.4
and

3.21
of the two types of theta functions,
Proposition
4.13
,
and Theorem
4.14
.
∎

4.2.
Proof of the correspondence theorem, Theorem
4.14

The proof is by induction on the length of
\beta_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}},x}
, that is, the number

r\geq 1
in Definition
4.2
. The base case
r=1
is the case of
a straight broken line ending at some point
x
in the interior of a maximal
cell. The corresponding broken line type is then a trivial one
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{\mathrm{in}}

without a vertex and trivial curve class
A=0
. Thus
N_{\boldsymbol{\tau}}=1
,
k_{\boldsymbol{\tau}}=1

by definition and the claimed equality holds trivially.

For the inductive step, let
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}=(\tau,\bf A)
,
\tau=(G,{\boldsymbol{\sigma}},\bf u)
be an
admissible decorated broken line type as in the theorem, with associated broken
line
\beta_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}},x}
of length
r
. Let
v\in V(G)
be the vertex adjacent to

L_{\mathrm{out}}
,
\tau_{v}\in\Gamma(G,\ell)
the corresponding cone and

\sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}={\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(v)
the smallest cell containing
h(\tau_{v})
. Here we
denote by
\mathfrak{p}
the set-theoretic intersection of all walls or codimension one
cells of
\mathscr{P}
containing
h(\tau_{v})
. Thus
\mathfrak{p}
is a polyhedral subset of
B

of dimension
n-1
. Note that
\dim\sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}\in\{n-1,n\}
, and we refer to the
two cases as codimension one and codimension zero, respectively. We work in an
affine chart of
B_{0}
containing
\operatorname{Int}\sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}
, with
\Lambda
denoting the
lattice of integral tangent vector fields by
\Lambda_{\mathfrak{p}}\subset\Lambda

the corank one lattice of vectors tangent to
h(\tau_{v})
. Let
E_{\mathrm{in}}\in E(G)

be the unique edge adjacent to
v
and belonging to the spine of
\tau
, and

E_{1},\ldots,E_{l}
the remaining edges adjacent to
v
, all oriented toward
v
.
The case
l=0
means that no such edges are present and then
E_{1},\ldots,E_{l}

denotes the empty sequence. Denote further
u_{\mathrm{in}}=\mathbf{u}(E_{\mathrm{in}})
,

u_{\mathrm{out}}=\mathbf{u}(L_{\mathrm{out}})=u_{\tau}
,
u_{i}=\mathbf{u}(E_{i})
, and
\sigma_{\mathrm{in}}={\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(E_{\mathrm{in}})

the maximal cell containing
h_{s}(E_{\mathrm{in}})
for all
s\in\tau
.

We now split
G
at all the edges
E_{\mathrm{in}},E_{1},\ldots,E_{l}
adjacent to
v
. See

[
ACGS2
, §5.1]
for a formal treatment of splitting. Splitting an edge
E

with vertices
v_{1},v_{2}
leads to a pair of legs that we denote
(E,v_{1})
,

(E,v_{2})
. By Construction
4.12
the types obtained
from
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}
after splitting are as follows.

(1)

A decorated broken line type
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{\mathrm{in}}=(G_{\mathrm{in}},{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\mathrm{in}},\mathbf{u}_{\mathrm{in}},\mathbf{A}_{\mathrm{in}})
, with the unique leg
(E_{\mathrm{in}},v_{\mathrm{in}})
obtained from
splitting and
\mathbf{u}_{\mathrm{in}}(E_{\mathrm{in}},v_{\mathrm{in}})=u_{\mathrm{in}}
.

(2)

Decorated wall types
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{i}=(G_{i},{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{i},\mathbf{u}_{i},\mathbf{A}_{i})
,
i=1,\ldots,l
, with
legs
(E_{i},v_{i})\in L(G_{i})
obtained from splitting, and
\mathbf{u}_{i}(E_{i},v_{i})=u_{i}
.

(3)

{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{0}=(G_{0},{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{0},\mathbf{u}_{0},\mathbf{A}_{0})
, the type of a decorated punctured map
with only one vertex
v
with
{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{0}(v)=\sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}={\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(v)
, no edges,
and legs
(E_{\mathrm{in}},v)
,
(E_{1},v),\ldots,(E_{l},v)
,
(E_{\mathrm{out}},v)=L_{\mathrm{out}}
with

\mathbf{A}_{0}(v)=\mathbf{A}(v)
and

\mathbf{u}_{0}(E_{\mathrm{in}},v)=-u_{\mathrm{in}},\quad\mathbf{u}_{0}(E_{\mathrm{out}},v)=u_{\mathrm{out}},\quad\mathbf{u}_{0}(E_{i},v)=-u_{i},\ i=1,\ldots,l.

As usual the corresponding undecorated types are denoted
\tau_{\mathrm{in}}
and

\tau_{i}
,
i=0,1,\ldots,l
. Grouping together identical
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{i}
we may relabel
and assume the
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{i}
are pairwise non-isomorphic, but each
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{i}
to
occur
\mu_{i}
-times in the splitting for some
\mu_{i}\in\mathbb{N}
.

The propagation rule (
4.1
) for
monomials in the definition of decorated broken lines
(Definition
4.6
) together with the induction
hypothesis shows that the claimed equality

a_{\beta_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}}}z^{m_{\beta_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}}}}=k_{\tau}N_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}}t^{A}z^{-u_{\mathrm{out}}}

is equivalent to the following

(4.2)

k_{\tau}N_{\boldsymbol{\tau}}t^{A}z^{-u_{\mathrm{out}}}=k_{\tau_{\mathrm{in}}}N_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{\mathrm{in}}}t^{A_{\mathrm{in}}}t^{d[X_{\sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}}]}z^{-u_{\mathrm{in}}}\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{l}\frac{\big(dk_{\tau_{i}}W_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{i}}t^{A_{i}}z^{-u_{\tau_{i}}}\big)^{\mu_{i}}}{\mu_{i}!}.

Note that
[X_{\sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}}]=0
in the codimension zero case and

[X_{\sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}}]
is the class of the curve corresponding to the
(n-1)
-cell
containing
\mathfrak{p}
in the codimension one case. Furthermore,

(4.3)

d=|\delta(u_{\mathrm{in}})|

for
\delta:\Lambda\to\mathbb{Z}
the quotient by
\Lambda_{\mathfrak{p}}
, and
k_{\tau_{\mathrm{in}}}
,

k_{\tau_{i}}
are defined in (
3.20
) and (
3.10
), respectively.

To prove this equality, we employ the numerical splitting formula for punctured
Gromov-Witten invariants proved by Yixian Wu
[
Wu
]
and recalled in the
appendix. Assumption
A.1
of toric gluing strata is trivially
fulfilled in the codimension zero case, while in codimension one it follows from
Lemma
1.10
. In the following discussion, we freely use the
notation introduced in the appendix, but note that we do not split all edges of

\tau
, but just the subset
E(G,v)\subseteq E(G)
of
l+1
edges adjacent to

v
. Thus for any
E\in E(G,v)
the vector space
(\Lambda_{E})_{\mathbb{R}}
holding the

E
-component of the displacement vector equals
\Lambda_{\mathbb{R}}
. For the time
being we also revert to the original labelling of the
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{i}
with possibly
several identical wall types. The numerator
\mu_{i}!
in (
4.2
) arises only at the very end when taking the automorphism group of the
glued decorated type
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}
into account.

To invoke Theorem
A.4
we need to choose a general
displacement vector
\nu=(\nu_{E})_{E\in E(G,v)}
and then determine the
corresponding set
\Delta(\nu)
of decorated transverse types

({\boldsymbol{\omega}}_{\mathrm{in}},{\boldsymbol{\omega}}_{0},{\boldsymbol{\omega}}_{1},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{\omega}}_{l})

for
\nu
, with
{\boldsymbol{\omega}}_{\bullet}
a decorated type marked by
\tau_{\bullet}
.
Lemma
4.15
below shows that for a
certain choice of
\nu
there is a unique decorated transverse type with

{\boldsymbol{\omega}}_{\mathrm{in}}={\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{\mathrm{in}}
,
{\boldsymbol{\omega}}_{i}={\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{i}
for all
i
, and with

(4.4)

{\boldsymbol{\omega}}_{0}=(\hat{G}_{0},\hat{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{0},\hat{\mathbf{u}}_{0},\hat{\mathbf{A}}_{0})=(\omega_{0},\hat{\mathbf{A}}_{0})

a certain maximal decorated global type marked by
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{0}
.

Figure 4.1.
The tropical type
\omega_{0}
(codimension zero case).

If
l=0
we take
{\boldsymbol{\omega}}_{0}={\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{0}
. To define
{\boldsymbol{\omega}}_{0}
for
l\geq 1
, assume
first
\sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}=\sigma_{\mathrm{in}}
, that is, we are in the situation of codimension
zero. Take for
\hat{G}_{0}
the graph with
l
trivalent vertices

v^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,v^{\prime}_{l}
connected in the given order by
l-1
edges

E^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,E^{\prime}_{l-1}
to form a chain, and with legs distributed as

(E_{\mathrm{in}},v^{\prime}_{1}),\quad(E_{\mathrm{out}},v^{\prime}_{l}),\quad(E_{i},v^{\prime}_{i}),\,i=1,\ldots,l,

see Figure
4.1
.
The notation
E_{\mathrm{in}}
,
E_{i}
,
E_{\mathrm{out}}
indicates that we identify these legs
with the legs of
\tau_{0}
via the contraction morphism
\omega_{0}\to\tau_{0}

contracting all edges of
\omega_{0}
. All strata are given by
\sigma_{\mathrm{in}}
and
the contact orders for each edge are determined inductively from the contact
orders
\mathbf{u}_{0}
of
\tau_{0}
by enforcing the balancing condition
Lemma
2.1
. Necessarily
\hat{\mathbf{A}}_{0}=0
.

If
\sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}
is of codimension one, we further split
v^{\prime}_{l}
into two
vertices
v^{\prime}_{l}
and
v^{\prime}_{\mathrm{out}}
connected by another edge
E^{\prime}_{l}
, with
\hat{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{0}(v^{\prime}_{\mathrm{out}})=\sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}
, and the legs at
v^{\prime}_{l}
and
v^{\prime}_{\mathrm{out}}

distributed as
(E_{l},v^{\prime}_{l})
,
(E_{\mathrm{out}},v^{\prime}_{\mathrm{out}})
. For
l=1
the leg

(E_{\mathrm{in}},v^{\prime}_{1})=(E_{\mathrm{in}},v^{\prime}_{l})
is also adjacent to
v^{\prime}_{l}
. The other strata are
forced to be one of the two maximal cells
\sigma_{\mathrm{in}}={\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(E_{\mathrm{in}},v)
or

{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(L_{\mathrm{out}})
containing
\sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}
, namely
\hat{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{0}(E_{\mathrm{out}},v^{\prime}_{\mathrm{out}})={\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(L_{\mathrm{out}})
and all other strata equal to
\sigma_{\mathrm{in}}
.
The curve classes are determined by the contraction morphism as

\hat{\mathbf{A}}(v^{\prime}_{\mathrm{out}})=\mathbf{A}(v)=d\cdot[X_{\sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}}]

and all other curves classes trivial. Note that in any
case the curve classes are determined by the contraction morphism. It is
therefore enough to drop the decoration in the following classification of
transverse types according to Definition
A.2
.

Lemma 4.15
.

There exists a displacement vector
\nu=(\nu_{E})_{E}\in\prod_{E\in E(G)}\Lambda_{\mathbb{R}}
that is general for
\tau
such that any
\nu
-transverse type

\omega
for
\tau
(Definition
A.2
,2) with

\mathscr{M}(X,\omega^{\prime}_{0})\neq\emptyset
, for
\omega^{\prime}_{0}
the part of
\omega
marked
by
\tau_{0}
, is isomorphic to

\omega=(\tau_{\mathrm{in}},\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{l},\omega_{0}),

where
\omega_{0}
is as described in (
4.4
).

Proof.

Take the quotient
\delta:\Lambda\to\mathbb{Z}
by

\Lambda_{\mathfrak{p}}
to evaluate non-negatively on vectors pointing from
\mathfrak{p}
to the
incoming direction, and let
m\in\Lambda
be a vector with
\delta(m)=1
. Then in
particular
\Lambda=\Lambda_{\mathfrak{p}}\oplus\mathbb{Z}m
. Choose
\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{l}\in\mathbb{R}

with

\nu_{1}>\ldots>\nu_{l}>0,

and define

\nu_{E_{\mathrm{in}}}=0,\quad\nu_{E_{i}}=\nu_{i}\cdot m,\quad i=1,\ldots,l.

Thus
\nu_{E_{i}}=(0,\nu_{i})
when identifying
\Lambda
with

\Lambda_{\mathfrak{p}}\oplus\mathbb{Z}
. Following the interpretation in Remark
A.3
of
\Delta(\nu)
, we have to show that any

\nu
-broken tropical punctured map marked by
\tau
fulfilling

\mathscr{M}(X,\omega^{\prime}_{0})\neq\emptyset
has components of types

\omega_{\mathrm{in}}=\tau_{\mathrm{in}},\ \omega_{1}=\tau_{1},\ldots,\omega_{l}=\tau_{l},\ \omega_{0}.

We first consider the component marked by
\tau_{0}
. Denote this component by

\omega^{\prime}_{0}=(G^{\prime}_{0},{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{\prime}_{0},\mathbf{u}^{\prime}_{0})
and by

(E_{\mathrm{in}},v^{\prime}_{\mathrm{in}}),\ (E_{\mathrm{out}},v^{\prime}_{\mathrm{out}}),\ (E_{1},v^{\prime}_{1}),\ldots,(E_{l},v^{\prime}_{l})

the legs, with some of the vertices
v^{\prime}_{\mathrm{in}},v^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,v^{\prime}_{l},v^{\prime}_{\mathrm{out}}
possibly
coinciding. Denote further by
E^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,E^{\prime}_{r}\in E(G^{\prime}_{0})
the unique sequence
of edges connecting
v^{\prime}_{\mathrm{in}}
to
v^{\prime}_{\mathrm{out}}
, oriented away from
v^{\prime}_{\mathrm{in}}
. Note
also that
d=-\delta(u_{\mathrm{in}})
for
d
as introduced in (
4.3
)
and that
E_{\mathrm{in}}\in E(G)
is oriented away from
v
. Now since

\mathscr{M}(X,\omega^{\prime}_{0})\neq\emptyset
, Lemma
2.1
shows that

\omega^{\prime}_{0}
fulfills the balancing condition. Thus
u_{1},\ldots,u_{l}\in\Lambda_{\mathfrak{p}}=\operatorname{ker}(\delta)
implies that the images under
\delta
of the
contact orders of all edges are determined as follows:

-\delta(\mathbf{u}^{\prime}_{0}(E))=\begin{cases}d,&E=E^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,E^{\prime}_{r}\\
0,&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Moreover, by the perturbed matching condition (
A.9
),
the fact that the outgoing vertex for each
\tau_{i}
maps to
\mathfrak{p}
and the
choice of
\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{l}
, it follows that
\delta
is constant on the
subtree connecting the leg
(E_{i},v_{i})
to the chain of edges
E^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,E^{\prime}_{r}
,
with value
\nu_{i}
. Since all
\nu_{i}
are disjoint, it follows that all of these
subtrees are disjoint. Note that there are
r+1
vertices on the
path connecting
v^{\prime}_{\mathrm{in}}
and
v^{\prime}_{\mathrm{out}}
(including these latter two
vertices). Thus necessarily
r+1\geq l
. However, if
\dim\sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}=n-1
,

{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{0}^{\prime}(E_{\mathrm{in}},v^{\prime}_{\mathrm{in}})
and
{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{0}^{\prime}(E_{\mathrm{out}},v^{\prime}_{\mathrm{out}})
are distinct
maximal cones containing
\sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}
, and thus there must be
at least one vertex
v_{j}^{\prime}
on this path contained in
\sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}
. By the
choice of displacement vectors, none of the subtrees connecting
an edge
(E_{i},v_{i})
to the chain of edges
E^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,E^{\prime}_{r}
is connected
to
v_{j}^{\prime}
. Thus in this case,
r\geq l
.

Now by the dimension formula (
A.8
) we require

(4.5)

\textstyle\dim\widetilde{\omega}_{\mathrm{in}}+\dim\widetilde{\omega}^{\prime}_{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{l}\dim\widetilde{\omega}_{i}=\dim\widetilde{\tau}+\sum_{E}\operatorname{rk}\Lambda=(n+l)+(l+1)\cdot n=ln+2n+l.

Note that if
\dim\sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}=n
, then
\dim\omega_{0}^{\prime}\geq n+r
,
as there is an
n
-dimensional choice of location for one vertex,
and an additional choice of length for the edges
E_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,E_{r}^{\prime}
.
Thus, in this first case,
\dim\widetilde{\omega}_{0}^{\prime}\geq n+r+l+1
.
On the other hand, if
\dim\sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}=n-1
, then

\dim\omega_{0}^{\prime}\geq n-1+r
, as there is an
(n-1)
-dimensional choice
of location for the vertex
v_{j}^{\prime}
. Thus, in this second case,

\dim\widetilde{\omega}_{0}^{\prime}\geq n+r+l
.
The left-hand side of (
4.5
) now
has dimension at least

\begin{cases}n+(n+l+r+1)+l\cdot(n-1)=ln+2n+r+1,&\dim\sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}=n\\
n+(n+l+r)+l\cdot(n-1)=ln+2n+r,&\dim\sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}=n-1.\end{cases}

Note in both cases, this quantity is then
\geq ln+2n+l
,
with equality with the right-hand side of
(
4.5
) precisely if
r+1=l
or
r=l
in the
two cases,

\omega_{\mathrm{in}}=\tau_{\mathrm{in}}
,
\omega_{i}=\tau_{i}
,
i=1,\ldots,l
, and the mentioned
subtrees in
\omega^{\prime}_{0}
trivial. Comparing with the right-hand side of
(
4.5
) shows that we indeed have equality. In
particular,
G^{\prime}_{0}
agrees with the graph
G_{0}
in (
4.4
). Then
also
{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{\prime}_{0}=\hat{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{0}
since each vertex, except
v^{\prime}_{\mathrm{out}}
in the
codimension one case, maps to the maximal cell
{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(E_{\mathrm{in}})
. Furthermore,
the contact orders
\mathbf{u}^{\prime}_{0}
agree with
\hat{\mathbf{u}}_{0}
due to the balancing
condition.

Taken together this shows
\omega^{\prime}_{0}=\omega_{0}
, and hence

(\tau_{\mathrm{in}},\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{l},\omega_{0})
is indeed the only transverse type
for
\nu
.
∎

It remains to compute the multiplicity
m({\boldsymbol{\omega}})=m(\omega)
occurring in
Theorem
A.4
.

Lemma 4.16
.

The multiplicity according to Definition
A.2
,(3)
for the single element
\omega=(\tau_{\mathrm{in}},\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{l},\omega_{0})
of

\Delta(\nu)
in Lemma
4.15
equals

m(\omega)=\begin{cases}k_{\tau}^{-1}k_{\tau_{\mathrm{in}}}\prod_{i=1}^{l}(dk_{\tau_{i}}),&\dim\sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}=n\\
dk_{\tau}^{-1}k_{\tau_{\mathrm{in}}}\prod_{i=1}^{l}(dk_{\tau_{i}}),&\dim\sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}=n-1.\end{cases}

Proof.

The multiplicity in question is the index of
\operatorname{im}(\Phi^{\operatorname{gp}})
, where

\textstyle\Phi:(\widetilde{\tau}_{\mathrm{in}})_{\mathbb{Z}}\times(\widetilde{\omega}_{0})_{\mathbb{Z}}\times\prod_{i=1}^{l}(\widetilde{\tau}_{i})_{\mathbb{Z}}\longrightarrow\Lambda\times\Lambda^{l}

is the map describing the matching at the gluing edges, denoted

\varepsilon_{\omega_{0}}
in (
A.7
). In
particular,
\Phi^{-1}(0)=\widetilde{\tau}
is the enlarged basic cone for
the glued type
\tau
. For the case of a trivial incoming broken line type
define
\widetilde{\tau}_{\mathrm{in}}
as the maximal cone containing the incoming vector
and
\Phi
on this factor as the inclusion to the first copy of
\Lambda
.

We first treat the case
\dim\sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}=n
. In this case, by the description of
the type
\omega_{0}
, we can write

(4.6)

(\widetilde{\omega}_{0}^{\operatorname{gp}})_{\mathbb{Z}}=\Lambda\times\mathbb{Z}^{l}\times\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}^{l-1}=\Lambda\times\mathbb{Z}^{l}\times\mathbb{Z}^{l},

with the
\mathbb{Z}
-factors holding the lengths

\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{l},\ell_{\mathrm{in}},\ell^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,\ell^{\prime}_{l-1}

of the legs
(E_{1},v^{\prime}_{1}),\ldots,(E_{l},v^{\prime}_{l})
,
(E_{\mathrm{in}},v^{\prime}_{1})
and edges

E^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,E^{\prime}_{l-1}
, in this order, while the
\Lambda
-factor records the
image of the point on the incoming leg. For uniformity of notation write

E^{\prime}_{0}=(E_{\mathrm{in}},v^{\prime}_{1})
,
u^{\prime}_{0}=u_{\mathrm{in}}
,
\ell^{\prime}_{0}=\ell_{\mathrm{in}}
and

u^{\prime}_{i}=\hat{\mathbf{u}}_{0}(E^{\prime}_{i})=u_{\mathrm{in}}+u_{1}+\cdots+u_{i},\quad i=1,\ldots,l-1.

Thus the image of
v^{\prime}_{i}\in V(\hat{G}_{0})
under the tropical punctured map of type

\omega_{0}
defined by

(m,\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{l},\ell^{\prime}_{0},\ldots,\ell^{\prime}_{l-1})\in\Lambda\times\mathbb{Z}^{l}\times\mathbb{Z}^{l}

equals

m+\ell^{\prime}_{0}u^{\prime}_{0}+\ell^{\prime}_{1}u^{\prime}_{1}+\ldots+\ell^{\prime}_{i-1}u^{\prime}_{i-1}.

Denote further by
{\operatorname{ev}}_{\mathrm{in}}:(\widetilde{\tau}_{\mathrm{in}}^{\operatorname{gp}})_{\mathbb{Z}}\to\Lambda
and

{\operatorname{ev}}_{i}:(\widetilde{\tau}_{i}^{\operatorname{gp}})_{\mathbb{Z}}\to\Lambda_{\mathfrak{p}}\subset\Lambda
the linear
extensions of the evaluation maps at the point on the respective unique leg.

We first compute the cokernel
Q
of the restriction of
\Phi^{\operatorname{gp}}
to

(\widetilde{\tau}_{\mathrm{in}})^{\operatorname{gp}}\times(\{0\}\times\mathbb{Z}^{l})\times\prod_{i=1}^{l}(\widetilde{\tau}_{i})^{\operatorname{gp}}.

The further restriction to the subspace spanned by the last copy of
\mathbb{Z}
in (
4.6
) and
(\widetilde{\tau}_{l})^{\operatorname{gp}}
leads to the map

\varepsilon_{l}:\mathbb{Z}\times(\widetilde{\tau}_{l}^{\operatorname{gp}})_{\mathbb{Z}}\longrightarrow\Lambda,\quad(\ell^{\prime}_{l-1},h_{l})\longmapsto{\operatorname{ev}}_{l}(h_{l})-\ell^{\prime}_{l-1}\cdot u^{\prime}_{l-1}

to the last copy of
\Lambda
in the codomain of
\Phi^{\operatorname{gp}}
.
Now since
|\delta(u^{\prime}_{l-1})|=d
we can fit
\varepsilon_{l}

into the diagram with exact rows and injective columns

. An induction on

. We have thus shown

The snake lemma yields the exact sequence

(4.9)

0\longrightarrow\widetilde{\tau}^{\operatorname{gp}}_{\mathbb{Z}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Lambda\times\mathbb{Z}^{l}\longrightarrow Q\longrightarrow\operatorname{coker}(\Phi^{\operatorname{gp}})\longrightarrow 0.

Note also that
\widetilde{\tau}^{\operatorname{gp}}_{\mathbb{Z}}=\tau^{\operatorname{gp}}_{\mathbb{Z}}\times\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}^{l}
with the
\mathbb{Z}
-factors from the additional points at the
l+1
gluing edges,
and the map
\alpha
is a product of the injection of lattices

\bar{\alpha}:\tau^{\operatorname{gp}}_{\mathbb{Z}}\times\mathbb{Z}\longrightarrow\Lambda,\quad(h,\lambda)\longmapsto V(h)-\lambda\cdot u_{\mathrm{in}}

and
\operatorname{id}_{\mathbb{Z}^{l}}
. Here
V:\tau^{\operatorname{gp}}_{\mathbb{Z}}\to\Lambda_{\mathfrak{p}}
is the evaluation at the
outgoing vertex. Replacing
u_{\mathrm{in}}
in this formula by
u_{\mathrm{out}}
yields

{\operatorname{ev}}_{\mathrm{out}}:\tau^{\operatorname{gp}}_{\mathbb{Z}}\times\mathbb{Z}\to\Lambda
with index of the image defining

k_{\tau}
. But
|\delta(u_{\mathrm{out}})|=|\delta(u_{\mathrm{in}})|=d
. By considering two diagrams
similar to (
4.7
), it follows that

|\operatorname{coker}(\alpha)|=|\operatorname{coker}(\bar{\alpha})|=|\operatorname{coker}({\operatorname{ev}}_{\mathrm{out}})|=k_{\tau}.

Together with (
4.8
) the exact sequence (
4.9
) thus yields

\textstyle m(\omega)=\big|\operatorname{coker}(\Phi^{\operatorname{gp}})\big|=k_{\tau}^{-1}\cdot|Q|=k_{\tau}^{-1}k_{\tau_{\mathrm{in}}}\prod_{i=1}^{l}(dk_{\tau_{i}}),

as claimed.

If
\dim\sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}=n-1
we have an additional vertex
v^{\prime}_{\mathrm{out}}
mapping to

\sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}
and an additional edge
E^{\prime}_{l}
connecting
v^{\prime}_{l}
to
v^{\prime}_{\mathrm{out}}
, with contact order
u^{\prime}_{l}
. Thus
in this case we have

(\widetilde{\omega}_{0})^{\operatorname{gp}}_{\mathbb{Z}}=\Lambda_{\mathfrak{p}}\times\mathbb{Z}^{l}\times\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}^{l},

with
\Lambda_{\mathfrak{p}}
recording the position of
v^{\prime}_{\mathrm{out}}
in
\mathfrak{p}
.
We can now exhibit
(\widetilde{\omega}_{0})^{\operatorname{gp}}_{\mathbb{Z}}
as a sublattice of
\Lambda\times\mathbb{Z}^{l}\times\mathbb{Z}^{l}
from (
4.6
)
by the map

\displaystyle\Psi:\Lambda_{\mathfrak{p}}\times\mathbb{Z}^{l}\times\mathbb{Z}^{l+1}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow

\displaystyle\Lambda\times\mathbb{Z}^{l}\times\mathbb{Z}^{l},

\displaystyle(m,\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{l},\ell^{\prime}_{0},\ldots,\ell^{\prime}_{l})

\displaystyle\longmapsto

\displaystyle\textstyle\big(m-\sum_{i=0}^{l}\ell^{\prime}_{i}u^{\prime}_{i},\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{l},\ell^{\prime}_{0},..,\ell^{\prime}_{l-1}\big).

Here
u^{\prime}_{0}=u_{\mathrm{in}}
,
\ell^{\prime}_{0}=\ell_{\mathrm{in}}
are defined analogously to the
codimension zero case. Since the images of
u^{\prime}_{i}
in
\Lambda/\Lambda_{\mathfrak{p}}\simeq\mathbb{Z}
generate
d\mathbb{Z}
, we see that
\Psi
is an inclusion of lattices of index

d
. The stated formula in codimension one now follows by observing that

\Phi^{\operatorname{gp}}
is the composition of
\Phi^{\operatorname{gp}}
in the codimension zero case with

\operatorname{id}_{(\tilde{\tau}_{\mathrm{in}})_{\mathbb{Z}}}\times\Psi\times\operatorname{id}_{\prod_{i}(\tilde{\tau}_{i})_{\mathbb{Z}}}
.
∎

Finally we need to compute one simple punctured invariant.

Lemma 4.17
.

\deg\big[\mathscr{M}(X,{\boldsymbol{\omega}}_{0})\big|^{\mathrm{virt}}=\begin{cases}1,&\operatorname{codim}\sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}=n\\
1/d,&\operatorname{codim}\sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}=n-1.\end{cases}

Proof.

The graph
\hat{G}_{0}
given by
{\boldsymbol{\omega}}_{0}
is a chain with
l
or
l+1
trivalent
vertices of genus
0
, depending if
\dim\sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}=n
or

\dim\sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}=n-1
. In any case,
l
vertices are marked by
\sigma_{\mathrm{in}}
,
the maximal cell containing the image of
E_{\mathrm{in}}
. Thus if
\dim\sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}=n
,
the underlying stable maps in
\mathscr{M}(X,{\boldsymbol{\omega}}_{0})
are contracted trees of
l

copies of
\mathbb{P}^{1}
with three special points each, with
l-1
pairs of such
special points identified at the nodes. With the labelling of the
l+2

remaining points, this is a rigid curve with trivial automorphism
group. Moreover, the pull-back of
\Theta_{X}

is trivial. Hence
\mathscr{M}(X,{\boldsymbol{\omega}}_{0})
is a reduced point, giving the result in
the case
\dim\Lambda_{\mathfrak{p}}=n
.

In the codimension one case,
\mathfrak{p}
is contained in the
(n-1)
-cell

\rho=\sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}
with stratum
X_{\rho}\subset X
isomorphic to
\mathbb{P}^{1}
and

\mathcal{M}_{X}|_{X_{\rho}}
admitting a lift of the torus action. In particular, it holds

\Theta_{X}|_{X_{\rho}}\simeq\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}^{n}
. The incoming and outgoing legs of

G_{0}
are labelled by the maximal cells
\sigma_{\mathrm{in}},{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(L_{\mathrm{out}})\in\Sigma(X)
containing
\rho
. By the description of

{\boldsymbol{\omega}}_{0}
, only the outgoing vertex maps to
\rho
, all others map to

\sigma_{\mathrm{in}}
. Thus the stable maps underlying objects in
\mathscr{M}(X,{\boldsymbol{\omega}}_{0})

are contracted trees of
l
copies of
\mathbb{P}^{1}
with three special points each as
before, joined at the outgoing point with another copy of
\mathbb{P}^{1}
mapping to

X_{\rho}\simeq\mathbb{P}^{1}
. The tropical picture shows that the map
\mathbb{P}^{1}\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
is
totally branched at the two zero-dimensional strata
X_{\sigma_{\mathrm{in}}},X_{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(L_{\mathrm{out}})}\subset X_{\rho}
with branching order
d=|\delta(u_{\mathrm{in}})|
the
constant from (
4.3
). Hence the associated curve class is

d\cdot[X_{\rho}]
(as follows also from Lemma
2.3
).
Thus there is a unique underlying stable map over
\underline{W}=\operatorname{Spec}\Bbbk
.
Arguing similarly as in
[
GS7
, Claim 3.22]
, there is then
a unique enhancement, up to isomorphism, of this
underlying stable map to a basic punctured map
f:C^{\circ}/W\rightarrow X

of type
{\boldsymbol{\omega}}_{0}

with
\underline{W}=\operatorname{Spec}\Bbbk
.
Moreover, by the triviality of
\Theta_{X}|_{X_{\rho}}
this is again a
logarithmically unobstructed and rigid situation, now with automorphism group

\mu_{d}
. Hence
\mathscr{M}(X,{\boldsymbol{\omega}}_{0})
is the quotient of a reduced point by

\mu_{d}
, proving the stated result.
∎

Proof of (
4.2
).

For applying the gluing formula we now use the labelling with pairwise different
\tau_{i}
and multiplicities
\mu_{i}
. Let

\delta:\mathscr{M}(X,{\boldsymbol{\tau}})\longrightarrow\mathscr{M}(X,{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{\mathrm{in}})\times\mathscr{M}(X,{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{0})\times\prod_{i=1}^{l}\mathscr{M}(X,{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{i})^{\mu_{i}}

be the splitting map from (
A.1
) for
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}
. Applying
Theorem
A.4
with the general displacement vector
\nu

from Lemma
4.15
and noting that
\operatorname{Aut}({\boldsymbol{\omega}}/{\boldsymbol{\tau}})=\{1\}
yields

(4.10)

\delta_{*}\big[\mathscr{M}(X,{\boldsymbol{\tau}})\big]^{\mathrm{virt}}=m(\omega)\cdot\big[\mathscr{M}(X,{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{\mathrm{in}})^{\mathrm{virt}}\big]\times(j_{{\boldsymbol{\omega}}_{0}})_{*}[\mathscr{M}(X,{\boldsymbol{\omega}}_{0})]^{\mathrm{virt}}\times\prod_{i=1}^{l}\big[\mathscr{M}(X,{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{i})^{\mu_{i}}\big]^{\mathrm{virt}}.

Next observe

\operatorname{Aut}({\boldsymbol{\tau}})\simeq\operatorname{Aut}({\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{\mathrm{in}})\times\prod_{i=1}^{l}\big(\operatorname{Aut}({\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{i})\rtimes S_{\mu_{i}}),

with the symmetric group
S_{\mu_{i}}
acting by permutation of the
\mu_{i}
copies
of
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{i}
attached at
v
. Taking degrees in (
4.10
) and applying Lemma
4.17
, plugging in

m(\omega)
from Lemma
4.16
, multiplying by

k_{\tau}
, and dividing by
|\operatorname{Aut}({\boldsymbol{\tau}})|
now leads to

\displaystyle k_{\tau}N_{\boldsymbol{\tau}}

\displaystyle=k_{\tau_{\mathrm{in}}}\cdot{\textstyle\prod_{i=1}^{l}(dk_{\tau_{i}})^{\mu_{i}}}\cdot\frac{\deg[\mathscr{M}(X,{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{\mathrm{in}})]^{\mathrm{virt}}}{|\operatorname{Aut}({\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{\mathrm{in}})|}\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{l}\frac{\deg[\mathscr{M}(X,{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{i})^{\mu_{i}}]^{\mathrm{virt}}}{|\operatorname{Aut}({\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{i})\rtimes S_{\mu_{i}}|}

\displaystyle=k_{\tau_{\mathrm{in}}}N_{\tau_{\mathrm{in}}}\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{l}\frac{(dk_{\tau_{i}}W_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{i}})^{\mu_{i}}}{\mu_{i}!}.

The total curve class and contact orders are computed from the decorations as

A=A_{\mathrm{in}}+d[X_{\sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}}]+\sum_{i=1}^{l}\mu_{i}A_{i},

and

u_{\mathrm{out}}=u_{\mathrm{in}}+\sum_{i=1}^{l}\mu_{i}u_{i}.

The last three equalities together imply the claimed
scattering equation (
4.2
).
∎

5.
Consistency of the canonical wall structure

We now prove the main result of the paper, namely that

\mathscr{S}_{{\mathrm{can}}}
is a consistent wall structure. The actual
definition of consistency, while summarized in
[
GHS
, Def. 3.9]
,
is a bit complicated. However, the main point of the definition is
to enable the construction of so-called theta functions. In other
words, consistency guarantees that the local description

\vartheta_{p}(x)
for theta functions
patch together to give global functions on
a scheme constructed from the wall structure.

Here, we proceed in the opposite direction. We will first show
that the
\vartheta_{p}(x)
satisfy the necessary patching criteria,
and then show this patching implies consistency as defined in

[
GHS
, Def. 3.9]
.

5.1.
Patching of theta functions

As the discussion from now on largely follows the notions of

[
GHS
]
, we now adopt the conventions and notation of
that paper with regards to wall structures. In particular, we
now work with a wall structure
\mathscr{S}
satisfying precisely the
properties of
[
GHS
, Def. 2.11,2]
, by assuming that the
walls of
\mathscr{S}
comprise the codimension one cells of a polyhedral
cone
refinement
\mathscr{P}_{\mathscr{S}}
of
\mathscr{P}
. As mentioned in Remark
3.4
,
this may be achieved for
\mathscr{S}_{{\mathrm{can}}}
by subdividing walls, combining
walls with the same support, and adding walls with attached function
1

if necessary. In particular, as in
[
GHS
]
, we use the notation

\mathfrak{u}
for a maximal cell of
\mathscr{P}_{\mathscr{S}}
and
\mathfrak{b}
for a codimension
one cell of
\mathscr{P}_{\mathscr{S}}
contained in
\rho\in\mathscr{P}^{[n-1]}
. We call

\mathfrak{u}
a
chamber
and
\mathfrak{b}
a
slab
or

codimension one wall
, while we call
those walls intersecting the interior of a maximal cell a

codimension zero wall
. A
joint

\mathfrak{j}
is a
codimension two cell in
\mathscr{P}_{\mathscr{S}}
, and the codimension of

\mathfrak{j}
is the codimension of the smallest cell of
\mathscr{P}
containing

\mathfrak{j}
.

Recalling further notation from
[
GHS
]
, if
\mathfrak{u}
is a chamber
contained in
\sigma\subseteq\mathscr{P}_{{\operatorname{max}}}
, we set

R_{\mathfrak{u}}=R_{\sigma}:=\Bbbk[\mathcal{P}_{x}^{+}]/I_{x}=(\Bbbk[Q]/I)[\Lambda_{\sigma}]

for any point
x\in\operatorname{Int}(\mathfrak{u})
.

Given a codimension zero wall
\mathfrak{p}
separating chambers
\mathfrak{u},\mathfrak{u}^{\prime}
,
we obtain a
\Bbbk[Q]/I
-algebra homomorphism

(5.1)

\theta_{\mathfrak{p}}:R_{\mathfrak{u}}\rightarrow R_{\mathfrak{u}^{\prime}},\quad z^{m}\mapsto f_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\langle n_{\mathfrak{p}},m\rangle}z^{m},

where
n_{\mathfrak{p}}\in\operatorname{Hom}(\Lambda_{\sigma},\mathbb{Z})
is the
primitive normal vector
to
\mathfrak{p}
positive on
\mathfrak{u}
. Since
f_{\mathfrak{p}}\equiv 1\bmod\mathfrak{m}

and
\sqrt{I}=\mathfrak{m}
,
f_{\mathfrak{p}}
is in fact invertible so that
\theta_{\mathfrak{p}}

is an automorphism.

For a slab
\mathfrak{b}
, after choosing

\xi
as chosen for the isomorphism (
3.3
),
we define

R_{\mathfrak{b}}:=(\Bbbk[Q]/I)[\Lambda_{\rho}][Z_{+},Z_{-}]/(Z_{+}Z_{-}-f_{\mathfrak{b}}t^{\kappa_{\rho}}),

where
f_{\mathfrak{b}}
is the function attached to
\mathfrak{b}
.
If
\mathfrak{u},\mathfrak{u}^{\prime}
are the adjacent chambers to
\mathfrak{b}
, there
are natural localization maps at
Z_{+}
and
Z_{-}
respectively

(5.2)

\chi_{\mathfrak{b},\mathfrak{u}}:R_{\mathfrak{b}}\rightarrow R_{\mathfrak{u}},\quad\chi_{\mathfrak{b},\mathfrak{u}^{\prime}}:R_{\mathfrak{b}}\rightarrow R_{\mathfrak{u}^{\prime}},

given by

\chi_{\mathfrak{b},\mathfrak{u}}(t^{A}z^{m}Z_{+}^{a}Z_{-}^{b})=t^{A+b\kappa_{\rho}}z^{m+(a-b)\xi}f_{\mathfrak{b}}^{b},\quad\chi_{\mathfrak{b},\mathfrak{u}^{\prime}}(t^{A}z^{m}Z_{+}^{a}Z_{-}^{b})=t^{A+a\kappa_{\rho}}z^{m+(a-b)\xi}f_{\mathfrak{b}}^{a},

where
A\in Q
and
m\in\Lambda_{\rho}
.
Note that these maps differ from those given in
(
3.4
), (
3.5
) only in the powers of

f_{\mathfrak{b}}
appearing here.

We may now make precise the notion that the
\vartheta_{p}(x)
patch.

Theorem 5.1
.

For the canonical wall structure
\mathscr{S}_{{\mathrm{can}}}
and any
p\in B(\mathbb{Z})\setminus\{0\}
, we have

(1)

If
x,x^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Int}(\mathfrak{u})
for a chamber
\mathfrak{u}
, then

\vartheta_{p}(x)=\vartheta_{p}(x^{\prime}).

(2)

If
\mathfrak{p}
is a codimension zero wall separating chambers

\mathfrak{u},\mathfrak{u}^{\prime}
, and
x\in\operatorname{Int}(\mathfrak{u})
,
x^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Int}(\mathfrak{u}^{\prime})
, then

\vartheta_{p}(x^{\prime})=\theta_{\mathfrak{p}}\big(\vartheta_{p}(x)\big).

(3)

If
\mathfrak{b}
is a slab with adjacent chambers
\mathfrak{u},\mathfrak{u}^{\prime}
,

x\in\operatorname{Int}(\mathfrak{u})
,
x^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Int}(\mathfrak{u}^{\prime})
, then
there exists a unique
\vartheta_{p}(\mathfrak{b})\in R_{\mathfrak{b}}
such that

\chi_{\mathfrak{b},\mathfrak{u}}(\vartheta_{p}(\mathfrak{b}))=\vartheta_{p}(x),\quad\chi_{\mathfrak{b},\mathfrak{u}^{\prime}}(\vartheta_{p}(\mathfrak{b}))=\vartheta_{p}(x^{\prime}).

Proof.

Step 1.

Establishing the setup.

We fix
p\in B(\mathbb{Z})\setminus\{0\}
once and for all.
We work throughout
with
\vartheta^{\log}_{p}(x)
using Theorem
4.5
.
Let
\mathscr{B}
be the set of isomorphism classes of decorated broken line types
given as

\mathscr{B}:=\{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}=(\tau,\mathbf{A})\,|\,\hbox{$p_{\tau}=p$, the total curve class of
$\mathbf{A}$ lies in $Q\setminus I$ and $N_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}}\not=0$}\}.

\mathscr{B}
is a finite set by Lemma
3.22
.
Thus we may choose a polyhedral cone complex
\mathscr{P}_{\mathscr{B}}

refining
\mathscr{P}_{\mathscr{S}}
with the property that for each
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}\in\mathscr{B}
,

h({\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{\mathrm{out}})
is a union of maximal cells

\mathfrak{u}\in\mathscr{P}_{\mathscr{B}}
. At the same time, we refine the wall structure

\mathscr{S}
so that we may take
\mathscr{P}_{\mathscr{S}}=\mathscr{P}_{\mathscr{B}}
.

It is immediate from the definition of the log theta
functions that if
\mathfrak{u}\in\mathscr{P}_{\mathscr{B}}
is a maximal cone then

\vartheta^{\log}_{p}(x)=\vartheta^{\log}_{p}(x^{\prime})
for any
x,x^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Int}(\mathfrak{u})
. Thus, it is now sufficient to verify that
if
\mathfrak{u},\mathfrak{u}^{\prime}\in\mathscr{P}_{\mathscr{B}}
are two chambers
separated by a codimension one cell
\mathfrak{p}
, then statements
(2) or (3) of the theorem
hold, depending on whether
\mathfrak{p}
is a codimension zero wall or a slab.

Step 2.

Broken lines transversal to
\mathfrak{p}
.

For
y\in\operatorname{Int}(\mathfrak{p})
, let
n_{\mathfrak{p}}\in\check{\Lambda}_{y}
be a primitive
normal vector to
\mathfrak{p}
, positive on
\mathfrak{u}
. We may then decompose,
for
x\in\mathfrak{u}
,
x^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{u}^{\prime}
,

\displaystyle\vartheta^{\log}_{p}(x)={}

\displaystyle\vartheta_{+}+\vartheta_{-}+\vartheta_{0}

\displaystyle\vartheta^{\log}_{p}(x^{\prime})={}

\displaystyle\vartheta^{\prime}_{+}+\vartheta^{\prime}_{-}+\vartheta^{\prime}_{0}

where
\vartheta_{+}
(resp.
\vartheta_{-}
,
\vartheta_{0}
) consists
of a sum of those monomials
at^{A}z^{m}
appearing in

\vartheta^{\log}_{p}(x)
with
\langle n_{\mathfrak{p}},m\rangle>0

(resp.
\langle n_{\mathfrak{p}},m\rangle<0
and
\langle n_{\mathfrak{p}},m\rangle=0
).
The terms
\vartheta^{\prime}_{\pm}
,
\vartheta^{\prime}_{0}
are defined analogously.

Using the broken line description of these theta functions, the
now standard argument of the proof of
[
Gr2
, Thm. 4.12]

or
[
CPS
, Lem. 4.9]
,
shows immediately that
if
\mathfrak{p}
is a codimension zero wall, then

(5.3)

\theta_{\mathfrak{p}}(\vartheta_{+})=\vartheta_{+}^{\prime},\quad\theta_{\mathfrak{p}}(\vartheta_{-})=\vartheta_{-}^{\prime}.

We briefly recall the argument. Given a decorated broken line
contributing to
\vartheta_{+}
with endpoint in
\mathfrak{u}
very close
to
\mathfrak{p}
, we may perturb the broken line by moving its endpoint to
a nearby point in
\mathfrak{p}
. We may then add an additional line segment
(or simply extend the final line segment through
\mathfrak{p}
) following
the definition of a decorated broken line to obtain a decorated
broken line with endpoint nearby in
\mathfrak{u}^{\prime}
. If this is done
in all possible ways, it then follows from the propagation rule
for monomials (
4.1
)
that
\theta_{\mathfrak{p}}(\vartheta_{+})=\vartheta_{+}^{\prime}
. Finally,
any monomial in
\vartheta_{0}
or
\vartheta_{0}^{\prime}
has exponent tangent
to
\mathfrak{p}
, and hence is left invariant under transport from
\mathfrak{u}
to

\mathfrak{u}^{\prime}
. Thus in this case, it is sufficient to show that

\vartheta_{0}=\vartheta_{0}^{\prime}
under transport
\mathfrak{t}_{\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}}
.

If
\mathfrak{p}=\mathfrak{b}
is a slab contained in
\rho
,
\mathfrak{u}\subseteq\sigma
,

\mathfrak{u}^{\prime}\subseteq\sigma^{\prime}
, then we may write

\vartheta_{+}=\sum_{i}a_{i}z^{m_{i}+\alpha_{i}\xi},\quad\vartheta_{-}^{\prime}=\sum_{i}a_{i}^{\prime}z^{m_{i}^{\prime}-\alpha_{i}^{\prime}\xi}

with
a_{i},a_{i}^{\prime}\in\Bbbk[Q]/I
,
m_{i},m_{i}^{\prime}\in\Lambda_{\rho}
and

\alpha_{i},\alpha_{i}^{\prime}>0
.
We may then define

\vartheta_{+}(\mathfrak{b}):=\sum_{i}a_{i}z^{m_{i}}Z_{+}^{\alpha_{i}},\quad\quad\vartheta_{-}(\mathfrak{b}):=\sum_{i}a^{\prime}_{i}z^{m^{\prime}_{i}}Z_{-}^{\alpha^{\prime}_{i}}.

By construction,

\chi_{\mathfrak{b},\mathfrak{u}}(\vartheta_{+}(\mathfrak{b}))=\vartheta_{+}

and

\chi_{\mathfrak{b},\mathfrak{u}^{\prime}}(\vartheta_{-}(\mathfrak{b}))=\vartheta_{-}^{\prime}
.
On the other hand, it follows from exactly the same
argument as outlined above and the formulas for

\chi_{\mathfrak{b},\mathfrak{u}^{\prime}}(Z_{+})
and
\chi_{\mathfrak{b},\mathfrak{u}}(Z_{-})
of
(
5.2
) that

(5.4)

\chi_{\mathfrak{b},\mathfrak{u}^{\prime}}(\vartheta_{+}(\mathfrak{b}))=\vartheta^{\prime}_{+},\quad\quad\chi_{\mathfrak{b},\mathfrak{u}}(\vartheta_{-}(\mathfrak{b}))=\vartheta_{-}.

As before, any monomial in
\vartheta_{0}
or
\vartheta_{0}^{\prime}

is left invariant under transport
from
\mathfrak{u}
to
\mathfrak{u}^{\prime}
. Thus again it is sufficient to show that

\vartheta_{0}=\vartheta_{0}^{\prime}
under
transport
\mathfrak{t}_{\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}}
, as the expression

\vartheta_{p}(\mathfrak{b}):=\vartheta_{+}(\mathfrak{b})+\vartheta_{-}(\mathfrak{b})+\vartheta_{0}

then satisfies the desired properties.

Step 3.

Reduction to a non-virtual linear equivalence
relation on moduli spaces.

Let
\mathscr{B}_{\mathfrak{u}}
(resp.
\mathscr{B}_{\mathfrak{u}^{\prime}}
) denote
the collection of isomorphism classes of
decorated broken line types
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}
contributing to

\vartheta_{0}
(resp.
\vartheta_{0}^{\prime}
) such that
\mathfrak{u}\subseteq h(\tau_{\mathrm{out}}),\mathfrak{u}^{\prime}\not\subseteq h(\tau_{\mathrm{out}})
(resp.
\mathfrak{u}^{\prime}\subseteq h(\tau_{\mathrm{out}}),\mathfrak{u}\not\subseteq h(\tau_{\mathrm{out}})
). Any other broken line type

{\boldsymbol{\tau}}
contributing to either
\vartheta_{0}
or
\vartheta_{0}^{\prime}
must then
have
\mathfrak{u}\cup\mathfrak{u}^{\prime}\subseteq h(\tau_{\mathrm{out}})
, and hence contributes
equally to both
\vartheta_{0}
and
\vartheta_{0}^{\prime}
. Hence it is sufficient
to show that

\sum_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}\in\mathscr{B}_{\mathfrak{u}}}k_{\tau}N_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}}t^{A}z^{-u_{\tau}}=\sum_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}\in\mathscr{B}_{\mathfrak{u}^{\prime}}}k_{\tau}N_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}}t^{A}z^{-u_{\tau}}.

Let
\mathscr{D}
be the set of isomorphism classes of degenerate
decorated broken line types (see
Definition
3.19
)

{\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{\prime}
for which there exists a
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}\in\mathscr{B}
and a contraction
morphism
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}\rightarrow{\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{\prime}
.
Recall that for a degenerate broken line type
\tau^{\prime}
,

\dim\tau^{\prime}=n-2
and
\dim h_{\tau^{\prime}}(\tau^{\prime}_{\mathrm{out}})=n-1
. Let

\mathscr{D}_{\mathfrak{p}}:=\{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{\prime}\in\mathscr{D}\,|\,\mathfrak{p}\subseteq h_{\tau^{\prime}}(\tau^{\prime}_{\mathrm{out}})\}.

Note by the defining assumption on
\mathscr{P}_{\mathscr{B}}
, if
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}\in\mathscr{B}\cup\mathscr{D}
is a
broken line type or a degenerate broken line type and

\dim\mathfrak{p}\cap h(\tau_{\mathrm{out}})=n-1
, then
\mathfrak{p}\subseteq h(\tau_{\mathrm{out}})
.

For any
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}\in\mathscr{B}_{\mathfrak{u}}\cup\mathscr{B}_{\mathfrak{u}^{\prime}}
, since
u_{\tau}

is tangent to
\mathfrak{p}
and
\mathfrak{p}
is contained in a codimension one
face of
h(\tau_{\mathrm{out}})
, necessarily there is a unique choice
of contraction morphism of decorated types

\phi:{\boldsymbol{\tau}}\rightarrow{\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{\prime}
with
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{\prime}\in\mathscr{D}_{\mathfrak{p}}
.
Note further the isomorphism class of
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{\prime}
only depends
on the isomorphism class of
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}
.
This gives maps
\Psi:\mathscr{B}_{\mathfrak{u}}\rightarrow\mathscr{D}_{\mathfrak{p}}
,

\Psi^{\prime}:\mathscr{B}_{\mathfrak{u}^{\prime}}\rightarrow\mathscr{D}_{\mathfrak{p}}
taking
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}
to the
corresponding degenerate decorated broken line type
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{\prime}
.
It is now sufficient
to show that for any
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{\prime}\in\mathscr{D}_{\mathfrak{p}}
, we have

(5.5)

\sum_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}\in\Psi^{-1}({\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{\prime})}k_{\tau}N_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}}=\sum_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}\in(\Psi^{\prime})^{-1}({\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{\prime})}k_{\tau}N_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}}.

To prove this, we now fix
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{\prime}\in\mathscr{D}_{\mathfrak{p}}
with
underlying type
\tau^{\prime}
. We consider the moduli space

\mathfrak{M}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}(\mathcal{X},\tau^{\prime}):=\mathfrak{M}(\mathcal{X},\tau^{\prime})\times_{\underline{\mathcal{X}}}\underline{X},

where the morphism
\mathfrak{M}(\mathcal{X},\tau^{\prime})\rightarrow\underline{\mathcal{X}}
is
given by schematic evaluation at the section of the universal curve
corresponding to
L_{\mathrm{out}}
.
For any contraction morphism
\tau\rightarrow\tau^{\prime}
,
we obtain by
[
ACGS2
, Prop. 5.19]
a Cartesian
diagram

(5.6)

\textstyle{\coprod_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}=(\tau,{\bf A})}\mathscr{M}(X,{\boldsymbol{\tau}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon_{\tau}}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{\tau}^{\prime}}
\textstyle{\mathscr{M}(X,{\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon_{\tau^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{M}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}(\mathcal{X},\tau)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{\tau}}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{M}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}(\mathcal{X},\tau^{\prime})}

Here the disjoint union is over all decorations
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}=(\tau,{\bf A)}
of
\tau

such that the contraction morphism
\tau\rightarrow\tau^{\prime}
induces a contraction
morphism
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}\rightarrow{\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{\prime}
. The maps
\iota_{\tau},\iota^{\prime}_{\tau}
are
induced by the contraction morphisms
\tau\rightarrow\tau^{\prime}
and

{\boldsymbol{\tau}}\rightarrow{\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{\prime}
. Note that

(5.7)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sum_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}\in\Psi^{-1}({\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{\prime})}k_{\tau}N_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}}={}&\sum_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}\in\Psi^{-1}({\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{\prime})}k_{\tau}{\deg[\mathscr{M}(X,{\boldsymbol{\tau}})]^{\mathrm{virt}}\over|\operatorname{Aut}({\boldsymbol{\tau}})|}\\
={}&\sum_{\tau\rightarrow\tau^{\prime}\atop{\boldsymbol{\tau}}=(\tau,{\bf A})}k_{\tau}{\deg[\mathscr{M}(X,{\boldsymbol{\tau}})]^{\mathrm{virt}}\over|\operatorname{Aut}({\boldsymbol{\tau}})||\operatorname{Aut}(\tau/\tau^{\prime})||\operatorname{Aut}({\boldsymbol{\tau}}/{\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{\prime})|^{-1}}\\
={}&\sum_{\tau\rightarrow\tau^{\prime}\atop{\boldsymbol{\tau}}=(\tau,{\bf A})}k_{\tau}{\deg[\mathscr{M}(X,{\boldsymbol{\tau}})]^{\mathrm{virt}}\over|\operatorname{Aut}({\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{\prime})||\operatorname{Aut}(\tau/\tau^{\prime})|}.\end{split}

The last two sums are over (1) isomorphism classes of
types
\tau
which are the underlying type of some element of

\Psi^{-1}({\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{\prime})
and (2) decorations
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}=(\tau,{\bf A})

of
\tau
yielding an induced contraction map
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}\rightarrow{\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{\prime}
.
The factor
|\operatorname{Aut}(\tau/\tau^{\prime})||\operatorname{Aut}({\boldsymbol{\tau}}/{\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{\prime})|^{-1}
arises in
the third summation because
we are now summing over multiple representatives for an
isomorphism class in
\Psi^{-1}({\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{\prime})
. Indeed, giving
a fixed representative
\tau
equipped with its unique contraction
morphism
\tau\rightarrow\tau^{\prime}
,

\operatorname{Aut}(\tau/\tau^{\prime})
now acts on the set of
decorations
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}=(\tau,\mathbf{A})
of
\tau

such that
\tau\rightarrow\tau^{\prime}
induces a contraction morphism

{\boldsymbol{\tau}}\rightarrow{\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{\prime}
. The stabilizer of this action is
the subgroup
\operatorname{Aut}({\boldsymbol{\tau}}/{\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{\prime})\subseteq\operatorname{Aut}(\tau/\tau^{\prime})
, and hence
each
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}\in\Psi^{-1}(\tau^{\prime})
appears

|\operatorname{Aut}(\tau/\tau^{\prime})|/|\operatorname{Aut}({\boldsymbol{\tau}}/{\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{\prime})|
times in the last two sums.
For the last equality, we use

|\operatorname{Aut}({\boldsymbol{\tau}})|=|\operatorname{Aut}({\boldsymbol{\tau}}/{\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{\prime})||\operatorname{Aut}({\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{\prime})|
.

Using (
5.6
), if we fix
\tau\rightarrow\tau^{\prime}
,
we may write

\sum_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}=(\tau,{\bf A})}\deg[\mathscr{M}(X,{\boldsymbol{\tau}})]^{\mathrm{virt}}=\deg(\varepsilon_{\tau})^{!}[\mathfrak{M}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}(\mathcal{X},\tau)].

Thus, with
all sums below being over isomorphism classes of broken line types

\tau
which are the underlying type of elements of

\Psi^{-1}({\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{\prime})
,
the quantity of (
5.7
) may now be written as

\displaystyle\sum_{\tau}k_{\tau}{\deg\iota^{\prime}_{\tau,*}(\varepsilon_{\tau})^{!}[\mathfrak{M}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}(\mathcal{X},\tau)]\over|\operatorname{Aut}({\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{\prime})||\operatorname{Aut}(\tau/\tau^{\prime})|}={}

\displaystyle\sum_{\tau}k_{\tau}{\deg\varepsilon_{\tau^{\prime}}^{!}\iota_{\tau,*}[\mathfrak{M}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}(\mathcal{X},\tau)]\over|\operatorname{Aut}({\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{\prime})||\operatorname{Aut}(\tau/\tau^{\prime})|}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\sum_{\tau}k_{\tau}{|\operatorname{Aut}(\tau/\tau^{\prime})|\deg\varepsilon_{\tau^{\prime}}^{!}[\mathfrak{M}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}_{\tau}(\mathcal{X},\tau^{\prime})]\over|\operatorname{Aut}({\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{\prime})||\operatorname{Aut}(\tau/\tau^{\prime})|}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\sum_{\tau}k_{\tau}{\deg\varepsilon_{\tau^{\prime}}^{!}[\mathfrak{M}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}_{\tau}(\mathcal{X},\tau^{\prime})]\over|\operatorname{Aut}({\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{\prime})|}.

Here the first equality follows from
[
Mn
, Thm. 4.1]
,
while the second equality follows from the morphism

\iota_{\tau}:\mathfrak{M}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}(\mathcal{X},\tau)\rightarrow\mathfrak{M}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}_{\tau}(\mathcal{X},\tau^{\prime})

being of degree
|\operatorname{Aut}(\tau/\tau^{\prime})|
.
Thus it will be sufficient to show the following relation
in the codimension one Chow group of
\mathfrak{M}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}(\mathcal{X},\tau^{\prime})

(5.8)

\sum_{\tau\rightarrow\tau^{\prime}\atop\mathfrak{u}\subseteq h_{\tau}(\tau_{\mathrm{out}})}k_{\tau}[\mathfrak{M}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}_{\tau}(\mathcal{X},\tau^{\prime})]=\sum_{\tau\rightarrow\tau^{\prime}\atop\mathfrak{u}^{\prime}\subseteq h_{\tau}(\tau_{\mathrm{out}})}k_{\tau}[\mathfrak{M}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}_{\tau}(\mathcal{X},\tau^{\prime})],

where the sums are over isomorphism classes of broken line
types
\tau
with a contraction morphism
\tau\rightarrow\tau^{\prime}

and with the stated inclusion.

Step 4.

Evaluation maps and local structure of moduli spaces.

We continue with
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{\prime}\in\mathscr{D}_{\mathfrak{p}}
fixed as in the previous step. If

\mathfrak{C}^{\circ}\rightarrow\mathfrak{M}(\mathcal{X},\tau^{\prime})
is the universal punctured curve over
the moduli space
\mathfrak{M}(\mathcal{X},\tau^{\prime})
, with section
x_{\mathrm{out}}
corresponding to
the leg
L_{\mathrm{out}}
, we write

\widetilde{\mathfrak{M}}(\mathcal{X},\tau^{\prime}):=(\underline{\mathfrak{M}(\mathcal{X},\tau^{\prime})},x_{\mathrm{out}}^{*}\mathcal{M}_{\mathfrak{C}^{\circ}})^{{\operatorname{sat}}},

the saturation of the log structure of
\mathfrak{C}^{\circ}
pulled back via

x_{\mathrm{out}}
: see
[
ACGS2
, §5.2]
.
Denote the reduction by

\overline{\mathfrak{M}}(\mathcal{X},\tau^{\prime}):=\widetilde{\mathfrak{M}}(\mathcal{X},\tau^{\prime})_{{\operatorname{red}}}.

By
[
ACGS2
, Prop. 5.5]
, the composition

\overline{\mathfrak{M}}(\mathcal{X},\tau^{\prime})\rightarrow\widetilde{\mathfrak{M}}(\mathcal{X},\tau^{\prime})\rightarrow\mathfrak{M}(\mathcal{X},\tau^{\prime})
induces an isomorphism on underlying
stacks, as
\mathfrak{M}(\mathcal{X},\tau^{\prime})
is already reduced by

[
ACGS2
, Prop. 3.28]
.

By composing the universal morphism

f:\mathfrak{C}^{\circ}\rightarrow\mathcal{X}
with the section
x_{\mathrm{out}}
,
we obtain an evaluation map
{\operatorname{ev}}:\overline{\mathfrak{M}}(\mathcal{X},\tau^{\prime})\rightarrow\mathcal{X}
.
As the former space is reduced, this evaluation map
factors through the stratum
\mathcal{X}_{\sigma}
of
\mathcal{X}

where
\sigma\in\mathscr{P}
is the minimal cell containing
\mathfrak{p}
.

We recall from
[
ACGS2
, Def. 3.22]
that

\mathfrak{M}(\mathcal{X},\tau^{\prime})
carries an idealized structure given
by a coherent sheaf of monoid ideals
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathcal{M}_{\mathfrak{M}(\mathcal{X},\tau^{\prime})}
.
This sheaf may be described by giving the stalks of the
image sheaf
\overline{\mathcal{I}}
of
\mathcal{I}
in the ghost sheaf

\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathfrak{M}(\mathcal{X},\tau^{\prime})}
.
By
[
ACGS2
, Prop. 3.23]
, because
\tau^{\prime}
is realizable, for
a geometric point
\bar{w}
of
\mathfrak{M}(\mathcal{X},\tau^{\prime})
, the monoid ideal

\overline{\mathcal{I}}_{\bar{w}}

has the following description. Let
Q_{\bar{w}}
be the stalk of
the ghost sheaf at
\bar{w}
, and
Q_{\tau^{\prime}}
the stalk of the ghost
sheaf at a generic point of
\mathfrak{M}(\mathcal{X},\tau^{\prime})
. Then there is
a well-defined generization map

\chi_{\bar{w}}:Q_{\bar{w}}\rightarrow Q_{\tau^{\prime}}
, and

\overline{\mathcal{I}}_{\bar{w}}=\chi_{\bar{w}}^{-1}(Q_{\tau^{\prime}}\setminus\{0\})
.

Similarly, we may put an idealized structure on

\overline{\mathfrak{M}}(\mathcal{X},\tau^{\prime})
, with ideal sheaf
\mathcal{J}
.
For a geometric point
\bar{w}
,
let
Q^{\circ,{\operatorname{sat}}}_{\bar{w}}
be the stalk of the ghost sheaf of
this latter log structure; necessarily, this monoid is
contained in
Q_{\bar{w}}\oplus\mathbb{Z}

and contains
Q_{\bar{w}}\oplus\mathbb{N}
. Then we take the stalk of the corresponding
monoid ideal
\overline{\mathcal{J}}_{\bar{w}}

to be the monoid ideal generated by
\overline{\mathcal{I}}_{\bar{w}}\oplus 0
,

Q^{\circ,{\operatorname{sat}}}\setminus(Q\oplus\mathbb{N})
and
(0,1)
.
It is straightforward to check that
\mathcal{J}
yields an idealized structure
on
\overline{\mathfrak{M}}(\mathcal{X},\tau^{\prime})
. Further, the natural morphism

\overline{\mathfrak{M}}(\mathcal{X},\tau^{\prime})\rightarrow\mathfrak{M}(\mathcal{X},\tau^{\prime})

is idealized log étale.

[
Wu
, Cor. 2.9]
now shows that the map
{\operatorname{ev}}:\overline{\mathfrak{M}}(\mathcal{X},\tau^{\prime})\rightarrow\mathcal{X}
is idealized log smooth. As this map factors through the
idealized log étale strict closed embedding
\mathcal{X}_{\sigma}\rightarrow\mathcal{X}
,
we write
{\operatorname{ev}}:\overline{\mathfrak{M}}(\mathcal{X},\tau^{\prime})\rightarrow\mathcal{X}_{\sigma}
, also
idealized log smooth.

After a base-change, we also obtain

\overline{\mathfrak{M}}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}(\mathcal{X},\tau^{\prime}):=\overline{\mathfrak{M}}(\mathcal{X},\tau^{\prime})\times_{\mathcal{X}_{\sigma}}X_{\sigma}\rightarrow X_{\sigma}

idealized
log smooth, with the underlying stack of
\overline{\mathfrak{M}}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}(\mathcal{X},\tau^{\prime})
isomorphic to the underlying stack of

\mathfrak{M}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}(\mathcal{X},\tau^{\prime})=\mathfrak{M}(\mathcal{X},\tau^{\prime})\times_{\underline{\mathcal{X}}_{\sigma}}\underline{X}_{\sigma}.

Step 5.

Proof of the linear equivalence relation
(
5.8
)
.
Continuing with
\sigma\in\mathscr{P}
the minimal cone containing
\mathfrak{p}
,
denote by
\sigma_{\mathfrak{u}},\sigma_{\mathfrak{u}^{\prime}}
the cones containing

\mathfrak{u}
and
\mathfrak{u}^{\prime}
respectively. We write
X_{\mathfrak{u}},X_{\mathfrak{u}^{\prime}}
for
the corresponding zero-dimensional strata of
X
, and
write
P_{\sigma},P_{\mathfrak{u}},P_{\mathfrak{u}^{\prime}}
for the stalks
of the ghost sheaf of
X_{\sigma}
,
X_{\mathfrak{u}}
and
X_{\mathfrak{u}^{\prime}}

at their generic points.

Our goal is to construct a rational function
\psi
on

\overline{\mathfrak{M}}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}(\mathcal{X},\tau^{\prime})
and determine its divisor of zeros and
poles. We note that as
\overline{\mathfrak{M}}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}(\mathcal{X},\tau^{\prime})
is idealized log
smooth over
\operatorname{Spec}\Bbbk
, it is isomorphic, locally in the smooth topology, to a
subscheme of a toric variety defined by a monomial ideal, see

[
ACGS2
, Prop. B.2]
. Further, by the cited proposition and the explicit
description of the idealized structure of
\overline{\mathfrak{M}}(\mathcal{X},\tau^{\prime})
, hence of

\overline{\mathfrak{M}}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}(\mathcal{X},\tau^{\prime})
, in fact
\overline{\mathfrak{M}}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}(\mathcal{X},\tau^{\prime})
is
smooth locally isomorphic to a toric stratum of a toric variety, and hence is
normal.

Let
\bar{s}\in\Gamma(X_{\sigma},\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{{\operatorname{gp}}}_{X_{\sigma}})
be defined
as follows. Note that giving such a section is equivalent to giving an integral
piecewise linear function on
\mathrm{Star}(\sigma)\subseteq B
. Using the affine
structure on
\mathrm{Star}(\sigma)
induced by that on
B
, we take this function in
fact to be linear in this affine structure, primitive, vanishing on
\mathfrak{p}
and
positive on
\mathfrak{u}
. We may then choose a lift of
\bar{s}
to
s\in\Gamma(X_{\sigma},\mathcal{M}^{{\operatorname{gp}}}_{X_{\sigma}})
. The existence of a lift is obvious
when
\dim X_{\sigma}=0
. When
\dim X_{\sigma}=1
, one needs to check that the
corresponding torsor is trivial. However, using the isomorphism of
Lemma
1.10
, we may identify the line bundle associated to the
corresponding torsor with the restriction of a line bundle on

X_{\Sigma_{\sigma}}
. The line bundle on
X_{\Sigma_{\sigma}}

is defined by the linear function
\bar{s}
on
\Sigma_{\sigma}
. However,
it is a standard fact of toric geometry that the line bundle corresponding to a
linear (as opposed to piecewise linear) function is in fact trivial.

Let
U\subseteq\overline{\mathfrak{M}}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}(\mathcal{X},\tau^{\prime})
be the dense
open stratum, i.e., the open substack whose geometric points are
precisely those whose corresponding type is
\tau^{\prime}
.
Note that the image of
{\operatorname{ev}}^{\flat}(s)
in

\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\overline{\mathfrak{M}}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}(\mathcal{X},\tau^{\prime})}^{{\operatorname{gp}}}
vanishes on

U
. Indeed, this follows from the fact that

h_{\tau^{\prime}}(\tau^{\prime}_{\mathrm{out}})\subseteq\mathfrak{p}
and
h_{\tau^{\prime}}|_{\tau^{\prime}_{\mathrm{out}}}

is dual to
\bar{\operatorname{ev}}^{\flat}:P_{\sigma}\rightarrow Q^{\circ,{\operatorname{sat}}}_{\tau^{\prime}}
.
Thus, viewing
\mathcal{O}_{\overline{\mathfrak{M}}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}(\mathcal{X},\tau^{\prime})}^{\times}
as
a subsheaf of
\mathcal{M}_{\overline{\mathfrak{M}}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}(\mathcal{X},\tau^{\prime})}^{{\operatorname{gp}}}

via the inverse of the structure map
\alpha
, we see that
{\operatorname{ev}}^{\flat}(s)

restricts to an invertible function on
U
, and hence defines
a rational function
\psi
on
\overline{\mathfrak{M}}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}(\mathcal{X},\tau^{\prime})
.
We complete the proof
by determining the divisor of zeros and poles of
\psi
.

We now use the identification of underlying stacks of

\overline{\mathfrak{M}}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}(\mathcal{X},\tau^{\prime})
and
\mathfrak{M}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}(\mathcal{X},\tau^{\prime})
.
Since the complement of
U
is a union of strata of

\mathfrak{M}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}(\mathcal{X},\tau^{\prime})
, it is enough to
check the order of vanishing of
\psi
along each codimension
one stratum of
\mathfrak{M}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}(\mathcal{X},\tau^{\prime})
. These strata are given by

\mathfrak{M}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}_{\tau}(\mathcal{X},\tau^{\prime})
with
\phi:\tau\rightarrow\tau^{\prime}

a contraction morphism with
\dim\tau=n-1
, see
[
ACGS2
, Rem. 3.28]
.
We write
\overline{\mathfrak{M}}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}_{\tau}(\mathcal{X},\tau^{\prime})
for
the corresponding stratum of
\overline{\mathfrak{M}}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}(\mathcal{X},\tau^{\prime})
.

Given such a
\phi:\tau\rightarrow\tau^{\prime}
, we have several cases:

Case 1.

\dim h_{\tau}(\tau_{\mathrm{out}})=n-1
. In this case,
necessarily
\bar{s}
still vanishes on
h_{\tau}(\tau_{\mathrm{out}})
, so as above,

\psi
extends as an invertible function across
\mathfrak{M}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}_{\tau}(\mathcal{X},\tau^{\prime})
.

Case 2.

h_{\tau}(\tau_{\mathrm{out}})
intersects the interior of
\mathfrak{u}
.

We adopt the notation from
[
ACGS2
, App. B]
that if
P
is a monoid,

K\subseteq P
a monoid ideal, then
A_{P,K}:=\operatorname{Spec}\Bbbk[P]/K
and

\mathcal{A}_{P,K}:=[A_{P,K}/\operatorname{Spec}\Bbbk[P^{{\operatorname{gp}}}]]
.

Let
Q_{\tau}^{\circ,{\operatorname{sat}}}
be the stalk of the ghost sheaf of

\overline{\mathfrak{M}}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}(\mathcal{X},\tau^{\prime})
at a geometric generic point
\bar{w}
of

\overline{\mathfrak{M}}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}_{\tau}(\mathcal{X},\tau^{\prime})
, and let
J\subseteq Q_{\tau}^{\circ,{\operatorname{sat}}}
be the monoid ideal arising from the idealized structure.
As
{\operatorname{ev}}
is idealized log smooth, smooth locally in a neighbourhood of
\bar{w}
,

{\operatorname{ev}}
concides with the morphism
\mathcal{A}_{Q^{\circ,{\operatorname{sat}}},J}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}_{P_{\mathfrak{u}},K}
, by
[
ACGS2
, Prop. B.4]
. Here
K=P_{\mathfrak{u}}\setminus\chi_{\sigma\mathfrak{u}}^{-1}(0)
where
\chi_{\sigma\mathfrak{u}}:P_{\mathfrak{u}}\rightarrow P_{\sigma}
is the generization map. In particular, the
stratum
X_{\sigma}\subseteq X
, smooth locally in a neighbourhood of the point

X_{\mathfrak{u}}
, is isomorphic to
A_{P_{\mathfrak{u}},K}
.

Let
\chi_{\tau^{\prime}\tau}:Q^{\circ,{\operatorname{sat}}}_{\tau}\rightarrow Q^{\circ,{\operatorname{sat}}}_{\tau^{\prime}}

be the generization map, necessarily a localization along a face
F
of

Q^{\circ,{\operatorname{sat}}}_{\tau}
. As
\dim\tau=\dim\tau^{\prime}+1
we see that
F\cong\mathbb{N}
. Note
that
\bar{s}
is non-negative on
\mathfrak{u}
. Since
h_{\tau}(\tau_{\mathrm{out}})
contains

\mathfrak{u}
, on which
\bar{s}
is non-negative, and
h_{\tau^{\prime}}(\tau^{\prime}_{\mathrm{out}})
is a
face of
h_{\tau}(\tau_{\mathrm{out}})
, on which
\bar{s}
vanishes, it follows that

\bar{s}
is non-negative on
h_{\tau}(\tau_{\mathrm{out}})
. Dually, it follows that the
stalk of
\bar{\operatorname{ev}}^{\flat}(\bar{s})
at
\bar{w}
lies in
Q^{\circ,{\operatorname{sat}}}_{\tau}\subseteq Q^{\circ,{\operatorname{gp}}}_{\tau}
.
Further, the stalk necessarily lies in
\chi_{\tau^{\prime}\tau}^{-1}(0)=F
. The order
of vanishing of
\psi
along the stratum determined by
\tau
is then, by
standard toric geometry, equal to the germ of
\bar{\operatorname{ev}}^{\flat}(\bar{s})
under
the identification of
F
with
\mathbb{N}
. Dually, since by construction
\bar{s}
generates

P_{\mathfrak{u}}^{{\operatorname{gp}}}\cap\Lambda_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\perp}
, this number is the same as

d_{\tau}:=|\operatorname{coker}(\Lambda_{\tau_{\mathrm{out}}}/\Lambda_{\tau^{\prime}_{\mathrm{out}}}\rightarrow\Lambda_{\mathfrak{u}}/\Lambda_{\mathfrak{p}})|.

This is the order of vanishing of
\psi
along the stratum

\mathfrak{M}_{\tau}(\mathcal{X},\tau^{\prime})
.

Note an elementary diagram chase shows that

k_{\tau}=d_{\tau}|\operatorname{coker}(\Lambda_{\tau^{\prime}_{\mathrm{out}}}\rightarrow\Lambda_{\mathfrak{p}})_{\mathrm{tors}}|.

Case 3.

h(\tau_{\mathrm{out}})
intersects the interior of
\mathfrak{u}^{\prime}
.

The same analysis as in Case 2 applies once
\bar{s}
is replaced
by
-\bar{s}
. Hence
\psi
has a pole of order
d_{\tau}
along

\mathfrak{M}_{\tau}(\mathcal{X},\tau^{\prime})
.

Putting these three cases together, we see that
the divisor of zeros and poles of
\psi
gives the relation in
the Chow group of
\mathfrak{M}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}(\mathcal{X},\tau^{\prime})
:

\sum_{\tau\rightarrow\tau^{\prime}\atop\mathfrak{u}\subseteq h_{\tau}(\tau_{\mathrm{out}})}d_{\tau}[\mathfrak{M}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}_{\tau}(\mathcal{X},\tau^{\prime})]=\sum_{\tau\rightarrow\tau^{\prime}\atop\mathfrak{u}^{\prime}\subseteq h_{\tau}(\tau_{\mathrm{out}})}d_{\tau}[\mathfrak{M}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}_{\tau}(\mathcal{X},\tau^{\prime})].

If we then multiply both sides by

|\operatorname{coker}(\Lambda_{\tau^{\prime}_{\mathrm{out}}}\rightarrow\Lambda_{\mathfrak{p}})_{\mathrm{tors}}|
, we get the desired
(
5.8
).
∎

5.2.
Consistency from theta functions

Theorem 5.2
.

\mathscr{S}_{{\mathrm{can}}}
is a consistent wall structure.

Proof.

Consistency as defined in
[
GHS
, Def. 3.9]
involves checking
