consistency in codimensions zero, one and two. We check each case in turn.

Consistency in codimension zero
. Let
\mathfrak{j}
be a codimension zero joint,

\mathfrak{p}_{1},\ldots,\mathfrak{p}_{r}
the walls containing
\mathfrak{j}
, taken in cyclic order, with

\mathfrak{p}_{i}
contained in chambers
\mathfrak{u}_{i}
and
\mathfrak{u}_{i+1}
, with indices taken
modulo
r
. With
\theta_{\mathfrak{p}_{i}}:R_{\mathfrak{u}_{i}}\rightarrow R_{\mathfrak{u}_{i+1}}
as
defined in (
5.1
), consistency at the joint means
[
GHS
, Def. 2.13]
that

(5.9)

\theta:=\theta_{\mathfrak{p}_{r}}\circ\cdots\circ\theta_{\mathfrak{p}_{1}}=\operatorname{id}

as an automorphism of
R_{\sigma}
, where
\sigma\in\mathscr{P}
is the
cell containing
\mathfrak{j}
. Note that for
p\in\sigma_{\mathbb{Z}}\setminus\{0\}
and
x\in\operatorname{Int}(\sigma)\setminus|\mathscr{S}_{{\mathrm{can}}}|
,

\vartheta_{p}(x)=z^{p}\mod\mathfrak{m}_{x}
, where
\mathfrak{m}_{x}
is as in
(
3.6
). Indeed, the trivial broken line
with no bends contributes the term
z^{p}
, and any other broken line

\beta
must have
a_{\beta}\in\mathfrak{m}_{x}
. Since the ideal
\mathfrak{m}_{x}
is
nilpotent in
R_{\sigma}
and
z^{p}
is invertible in
R_{\sigma}
,
it follows that

\vartheta_{p}(x)
is invertible in
R_{\sigma}
.
Working inductively modulo powers of
\mathfrak{m}_{x}
, one then sees
that any element of
R_{\sigma}
may be written
as a
\Bbbk[Q]/I
-linear combination of ratios

\vartheta_{p}(x)/\vartheta_{p^{\prime}}(x)
for

p,p^{\prime}\in\sigma_{\mathbb{Z}}
. By Theorem
5.1
,(2),
applied successively for the walls
\mathfrak{p}_{1},\ldots,\mathfrak{p}_{r}
, we
obtain
\theta\big(\vartheta_{p}(x)\big)=\vartheta_{p}(x)
, and hence

\theta
is the identity on
R_{\sigma}
.

Consistency in codimension zero follows, as this just means each
codimension zero joint is consistent.

Consistency in codimension one.
Let
\mathfrak{j}
be a codimension
one joint, contained in slabs
\mathfrak{b}_{1},\mathfrak{b}_{2}\subseteq\rho\in\mathscr{P}^{[n-1]}_{\mathrm{int}}
, maximal cones

\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}
, and codimension zero walls
\mathfrak{p}_{1},\ldots,\mathfrak{p}_{r}\subseteq\sigma
and
\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}_{s}\subseteq\sigma^{\prime}
. We order these
so that

\mathfrak{b}_{1},\mathfrak{p}_{1},\ldots,\mathfrak{p}_{r},\mathfrak{b}_{2},\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}_{s}

are cyclically ordered about
\mathfrak{j}
. Then consistency at the
joint
\mathfrak{j}
is expressed in
[
GHS
, Def. 2.14]
as follows.
We set

\displaystyle\theta:=\theta_{\mathfrak{p}_{r}}\circ\theta_{\mathfrak{p}_{r-1}}\circ\ldots\circ\theta_{\mathfrak{p}_{1}}:

\displaystyle R_{\sigma}\to R_{\sigma}

\displaystyle\theta^{\prime}:=\theta_{\mathfrak{p}_{1}^{\prime}}\circ\theta_{\mathfrak{p}_{2}^{\prime}}\circ\ldots\circ\theta_{\mathfrak{p}_{s}^{\prime}}:

\displaystyle R_{\sigma^{\prime}}\to R_{\sigma^{\prime}}.

We use the notation
\chi_{\mathfrak{b}_{i},\sigma},\chi_{\mathfrak{b}_{i},\sigma^{\prime}}

for the localization maps of (
5.2
).
Then consistency is the statement that

(\theta\times\theta^{\prime})\big((\chi_{\mathfrak{b}_{1},\sigma},\chi_{\mathfrak{b}_{1},\sigma^{\prime}})(R_{\mathfrak{b}_{1}})\big)=(\chi_{\mathfrak{b}_{2},\sigma},\chi_{\mathfrak{b}_{2},\sigma^{\prime}})(R_{\mathfrak{b}_{2}}).

To show this, let
x_{i}\in\sigma
be a point in the chamber adjacent
to
\mathfrak{b}_{i}
, and similarly
x_{i}^{\prime}\in\sigma^{\prime}
.
If
p\in\rho_{\mathbb{Z}}\setminus\{0\}
, then

\vartheta_{p}(x_{i})=z^{p}\mod\mathfrak{m}_{x_{i}}
, and similarly for
x_{i}^{\prime}
,
as in the codimension zero case.
Thus
\vartheta_{p}(\mathfrak{b}_{i})
, which exists by
Theorem
5.1
, (3), satisfies

\chi_{\mathfrak{b}_{i},\sigma}(\vartheta_{p}(\mathfrak{b}_{i})-z^{p})
nilpotent,
and the same for
\chi_{\mathfrak{b}_{i},\sigma^{\prime}}
. By the
discussion preceding
[
GHS
, Def. 2.14]
,
the map
(\chi_{\mathfrak{b}_{i},\sigma},\chi_{\mathfrak{b}_{i},\sigma^{\prime}}):R_{\mathfrak{b}_{i}}\rightarrow R_{\sigma}\times R_{\sigma^{\prime}}
is injective.
Thus we conclude that
\vartheta_{p}(\mathfrak{b}_{i})-z^{p}
is nilpotent in

R_{\mathfrak{b}_{i}}
, and hence

\vartheta_{p}(\mathfrak{b}_{i})
is invertible, as
z^{p}
is invertible. Further,
if
p\in\sigma_{\mathbb{Z}}\setminus\{0\}
, we may write
p=p^{\prime}+a\xi
with

a\geq 0

p^{\prime}\in\Lambda_{\rho}
, and then
\vartheta_{p}(\mathfrak{b}_{1})=z^{p^{\prime}}Z_{+}^{a}\mod\mathfrak{m}
, and similarly if
p\in\sigma^{\prime}_{\mathbb{Z}}\setminus\{0\}
,

p=p^{\prime}-a\xi
, we have
\vartheta_{p}(\mathfrak{b}_{1})=z^{p^{\prime}}Z_{-}^{a}\mod\mathfrak{m}
.
From this we see that any element of
R_{\mathfrak{b}_{1}}
may be
written as a
\Bbbk[Q]/I
-linear combination of Laurent monomials in theta
functions of the form

(\prod\vartheta_{p_{i}}(\mathfrak{b}_{1})^{a_{i}})/\vartheta_{p}(\mathfrak{b}_{1})
with

p\in\rho_{\mathbb{Z}}
,
p_{i}\in\sigma_{\mathbb{Z}}\cup\sigma^{\prime}_{\mathbb{Z}}
.
Note here we only have control over the expressions

\vartheta_{p}(\mathfrak{b}_{1})
for
p\in\sigma_{\mathbb{Z}}\cup\sigma^{\prime}_{\mathbb{Z}}
, so we need such
Laurent monomials in order to get all elements of
R_{\mathfrak{b}_{1}}
.

Now it follows from Theorem
5.1
,(2) that for
p\in B(\mathbb{Z})\setminus\{0\}

\displaystyle(\theta\times\theta^{\prime})\big((\chi_{\mathfrak{b}_{1},\sigma},\chi_{\mathfrak{b}_{1},\sigma^{\prime}})(\vartheta_{p}(\mathfrak{b}_{1}))\big)=(\theta\times\theta^{\prime})\big(\vartheta_{p}(x_{1}),\vartheta_{p}(x^{\prime}_{1})\big)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\big(\vartheta_{p}(x_{2}),\vartheta_{p}(x_{2}^{\prime})\big)=(\chi_{\mathfrak{b}_{2},\sigma}\times\chi_{\mathfrak{b}_{2},\sigma^{\prime}})(\vartheta_{p}(\mathfrak{b}_{2})).

Combined with the generation statement of the previous paragraph,
we obtain the desired equality.

This shows consistency at codimension one joints, noting there
is no condition for codimension one joints contained in
\partial B
.
Thus we obtain consistency in codimension one.

Consistency in codimension two.
Consistency at codimension
two joints is defined in
[
GHS
, §3.2]
. If
\mathfrak{j}
is
a boundary joint, i.e., contained in
\partial B
, then it is
easy to check that
\mathscr{S}_{{\mathrm{can}}}
is convex at
\mathfrak{j}

in the sense of
[
GHS
, Def. 3.12]
. Indeed, if

\partial B\not=\emptyset
, it follows
from Propositions
1.15

and
3.7
that if
\tau
is a wall type,
then
u_{\tau}
is tangent to
\partial B
.
Thus by
[
GHS
, Thm. 3.13]
,
the joint is consistent.

In case
\mathfrak{j}
is an interior joint, we refer the reader to

[
GHS
, §3.2]
for the definition of a corresponding polyhedral
affine manifold
(B_{\mathfrak{j}},\mathscr{P}_{\mathfrak{j}})
,
with
\mathscr{P}_{\mathfrak{j}}
consisting of tangent cones to elements of
\mathscr{P}

containing
\mathfrak{j}
along
\mathfrak{j}
. Further,
\mathscr{S}_{{\mathrm{can}}}
induces
a wall structure on
B_{\mathfrak{j}}
we denote as
\mathscr{S}_{\mathfrak{j}}
.
For
p\in B_{\mathfrak{j}}(\mathbb{Z})\setminus\{0\}
, broken lines on
B_{\mathfrak{j}}

then define for general
x\in B_{\mathfrak{j}}
theta functions

\vartheta^{\mathfrak{j}}_{p}(x)
as before. Consistency is then the statements
(1)–(3) of Theorem
5.1
for
B_{\mathfrak{j}}
and
\mathscr{S}_{\mathfrak{j}}

rather than
B
and
\mathscr{S}_{{\mathrm{can}}}
.

We do not prove consistency directly from Theorem
5.1

as it is not clear what the relationship between theta functions
on
B
and on
B_{\mathfrak{j}}
is. Instead, we proceed as follows.
Write
\mathrm{Star}(\mathfrak{j})
for the open star of
\mathfrak{j}
with respect to
the polyhedral cone decomposition
\mathscr{P}_{\mathscr{B}}
. Then there is a natural
embedding of
\mathrm{Star}(\mathfrak{j})
in
B_{\mathfrak{j}}
, identifying a cone of

\mathscr{P}_{\mathscr{B}}
containing
\mathfrak{j}
and contained in
\sigma\in\mathscr{P}

with a cone in the tangent wedge of
\sigma
along
\mathfrak{j}
, with
vertex at the origin of the tangent wedge.
Note the walls of

\mathscr{S}_{\mathfrak{j}}
are in one-to-one correspondence with the walls of

\mathscr{S}_{{\mathrm{can}}}
intersecting
\mathrm{Star}(\mathfrak{j})
.

The tangent space
\Lambda_{\mathfrak{j},\mathbb{R}}
to

\mathfrak{j}
acts by translation on
B_{\mathfrak{j}}
and

\Lambda_{\mathfrak{j}}
acts on
B_{\mathfrak{j}}(\mathbb{Z})
. Further, all walls of

\mathscr{S}_{\mathfrak{j}}
are invariant under this translation, and translation
takes broken lines to broken lines. In particular,
if
p\in B_{\mathfrak{j}}(\mathbb{Z})
,
v\in\Lambda_{\mathfrak{j},\mathbb{R}}
, it is immediate
that

\vartheta^{\mathfrak{j}}_{p}(x)=\vartheta^{\mathfrak{j}}_{p}(x+v)

for general
x
. Further, since the monomials
z^{v}
for
v\in\Lambda_{\mathfrak{j}}

are invariant under crossing all walls in
\mathscr{S}_{\mathfrak{j}}
, we in fact
have

\vartheta_{p+v}^{\mathfrak{j}}(x)=z^{v}\vartheta_{p}^{\mathfrak{j}}(x).

Note that the broken lines defining the two theta functions are
not precisely the same; rather, if
a_{i}z^{m_{i}}
is a monomial
attached to a segment of a broken line contributing to

\vartheta_{p}^{\mathfrak{j}}(x)
, then the corresponding broken line
contributing to
\vartheta_{p+v}^{\mathfrak{j}}(x)
has attached monomial

a_{i}z^{m_{i}+v}
, and thus the actual maps
\beta
are different
as they have different derivatives. However, there is clearly a
one-to-one correspondence between such broken lines.
In particular, if items (1)–(3) of Theorem
5.1

hold for a given choice of
p
, for the functions

\vartheta_{p}^{\mathfrak{j}}(x)
rather than
\vartheta_{p}(x)
,
they will also hold for
p+v
for any
v\in\Lambda_{\mathfrak{j}}
.

We will now show items (1)–(3) of Theorem
5.1
for the
functions
\vartheta^{\mathfrak{j}}_{p}(x)
for arbitrary non-zero
p\in B_{\mathfrak{j}}(\mathbb{Z})
,
which is fixed throughout the following discussion. Because we may
replace
p
with
p+v
for any
v\in\Lambda_{\mathfrak{j}}
, we may assume, as we do
from now on in this proof, that
p\in\mathrm{Star}(\mathfrak{j})
. Indeed, for any point
x\in B_{\mathfrak{j}}(\mathbb{Z})
, there is some
v\in\mathfrak{j}\cap B(\mathbb{Z})
such that
x+v\in\mathrm{Star}(\mathfrak{j})
under the canonical embedding of
\mathrm{Star}(\mathfrak{j})
in
B_{\mathfrak{j}}
.

More generally,
it follows easily from this observation that if
Z\subseteq B_{\mathfrak{j}}

is any compact subset,
then there is a
v\in\mathfrak{j}\cap B(\mathbb{Z})
such that
Z+v\subseteq\mathrm{Star}(\mathfrak{j})
.

Now suppose given an open subset
U\subseteq B_{\mathfrak{j}}
whose closure
Z
is
compact. For each
x\in Z
not contained in
|\mathscr{S}_{\mathfrak{j}}|
, we have a finite
set of broken lines contributing to
\vartheta^{\mathfrak{j}}_{p}(x)
, and these all have
asymptotic direction
p
contained in
\mathrm{Star}(\mathfrak{j})
. Thus if
\beta
is a broken
line contributing to this theta function,
\beta([t_{1},0])
is a compact subset
of
B_{\mathfrak{j}}
and we may translate
x
by some
v\in\mathfrak{j}
so that

\beta([t_{1},0])\subseteq\mathrm{Star}(\mathfrak{j})
. As the asymptotic direction
p

necessarily lies in a cone of
\mathscr{P}_{\mathscr{B}}
containing
\beta(t_{1})
, we see
also that
\beta((-\infty,t_{1}])\subseteq\mathrm{Star}(\mathfrak{j})
. By summing over the
v
’s
for the finite number of broken lines contributing to
\vartheta^{\mathfrak{j}}_{p}(x)
we
may thus translate
x
by some
v\in\mathfrak{j}
to guarantee that all broken lines
contributing to
\vartheta^{\mathfrak{j}}_{p}(x)
have image contained in
\mathrm{Star}(\mathfrak{j})
.
Noting that broken lines can be deformed continuously inside some polyhedron
without changing their type (see
[
GHS
, Prop. 3.5]
), we may further in
fact assume that all broken lines contributing to
\vartheta^{\mathfrak{j}}_{p}(x)
for

x\in Z\setminus|\mathscr{S}_{\mathfrak{j}}|
have image in
\mathrm{Star}(\mathfrak{j})
.

From the construction of
\mathscr{S}_{\mathfrak{j}}
,
it is now clear that provided
x
has been translated
sufficiently,
\vartheta^{\mathfrak{j}}_{p}(x)
is the sum of contributions
of those broken lines contributing to
\vartheta_{p}(x)
with image
wholly contained in
\mathrm{Star}(\mathfrak{j})
.

One now checks that the proof of Theorem
5.1
applies
equally well when
p\in\mathrm{Star}(\mathfrak{j})
if one restricts attention to
endpoints
x\in\mathrm{Star}(\mathfrak{j})
translated “sufficiently far” by
an element
v\in\mathfrak{j}
. Indeed, there are two key points of
the argument in the proof of Theorem
5.1
where one needs
to control the behaviour of the broken lines being considered.

First, in Step 2, the proof of (
5.3
) and
(
5.4
) works for any wall structure (not
necessarily a consistent one) and hence works for
\mathscr{S}_{\mathfrak{j}}
.

Second, in Steps 3 and following, we focus on a single degenerate broken
line type
\tau^{\prime}
and the result follows from the relation
(
5.8
). However, if we fix a point
x\in h_{\tau^{\prime}}(\tau^{\prime}_{\mathrm{out}})
and a neighbourhood
U
of
x
with compact closure

Z
,
we then may translate
Z
by some
v\in\mathfrak{j}
so that all broken lines
with endpoints in
Z\setminus|\mathscr{S}_{\mathfrak{j}}|
lie in
\mathrm{Star}(\mathfrak{j})
,
and thus in (
5.8
), we only need to consider
those types
\tau
with contraction
morphisms
\tau\rightarrow\tau^{\prime}
whose corresponding broken lines
with endpoint in
Z
lie entirely in
\mathrm{Star}(\mathfrak{j})
.
∎

We end this section with a brief observation in the relative case

g:X\rightarrow S
,
following on from Proposition
1.16

and Remarks
1.17
,
1.18
. Assume that the hypotheses of
Proposition
1.16
hold, so that
(B^{\prime},\mathscr{P}^{\prime})

is a polyhedral affine pseudomanifold. In this case, the canonical
wall structure
\mathscr{S}_{{\mathrm{can}}}
induces a wall structure

\mathscr{S}^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{can}}}
on
(B^{\prime},\mathscr{P}^{\prime})
. Indeed, first note that

\Lambda_{B^{\prime}}
, the local system of integral tangent vectors on

B^{\prime}
, is a subsheaf of
\Lambda_{B}|_{B^{\prime}}
consisting of those tangent
vectors
v
with
(g_{\mathrm{trop}})_{*}(v)=0
. Using
\varphi
the MPL function
on
B
given by Construction
1.14
, we obtain
also an MPL function
\varphi|_{B^{\prime}}
as in Remark
1.17
.
This induces a local system
\mathcal{P}_{B^{\prime}}
, and

\mathcal{P}_{B^{\prime}}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{B}|_{B^{\prime}}
is the subsheaf consisting of
those sections
m
of
\mathcal{P}_{B}|_{B^{\prime}}
such that
(g_{\mathrm{trop}})_{*}(\bar{m})=0
.

Next, given a wall
(\mathfrak{p},f_{\mathfrak{p}})\in\mathscr{S}_{{\mathrm{can}}}
, we define
the
index
of
\mathfrak{p}
to be

\operatorname{ind}(\mathfrak{p}):=|\operatorname{coker}(g_{\mathrm{trop},*}:\Lambda_{\mathfrak{p}}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z})|.

We may now define

\mathscr{S}_{{\mathrm{can}}}^{\prime}:=\{(\mathfrak{p}\cap B^{\prime},f_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\operatorname{ind}(\mathfrak{p})})\,|\,(\mathfrak{p},f_{\mathfrak{p}})\in\mathscr{S}_{{\mathrm{can}}}\},

noting that for any
x\in B^{\prime}\setminus\Delta
,

f_{\mathfrak{p}}\in\Bbbk[\mathcal{P}^{+}_{B^{\prime},x}]\subseteq\Bbbk[\mathcal{P}^{+}_{B,x}]

by Proposition
3.7
and the construction
of the canonical wall structure.

Proposition 5.3
.

Assuming
g:X\rightarrow S
satisfies the hypotheses of
Proposition
3.7
, then
\mathscr{S}_{{\mathrm{can}}}^{\prime}

is a consistent wall structure.

Proof.

This follows from the definition of consistency and
the consistency of
\mathscr{S}_{{\mathrm{can}}}
. Indeed, checking consistency
of
\mathscr{S}_{{\mathrm{can}}}^{\prime}
in codimensions zero, one and two involves
checking certain properties in rings which are subrings of
the corresponding rings for
B
. For example, for consistency
in codimension zero, to verify (
5.9
)
in the ring
R_{\sigma\cap B^{\prime}}
defined using the data

(B^{\prime},\mathscr{P}^{\prime},\varphi|_{B^{\prime}})
, it is enough to note that

R_{\sigma\cap B^{\prime}}
is a subring of
R_{\sigma}
and that
the ring automorphism
\vartheta_{\mathfrak{p}}
of
R_{\sigma}
restricts to
an automorphism of
R_{\sigma\cap B^{\prime}}
given by the wall

(\mathfrak{p}\cap B^{\prime},f_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\operatorname{ind}(\mathfrak{p})})
. This is
straightforward: see the discussion of
[
GHS
, §4.2]
as
to why the power of
f_{\mathfrak{p}}
is necessary. Indeed,

[
GHS
, §4.2]
goes the opposite way, from a wall structure
for
B^{\prime}
to a wall structure for
\mathbf{C}B^{\prime}=B
, see Remark
1.18
.
Thus there one must pass to a
\operatorname{ind}(\mathfrak{p})^{th}
root
rather than power. We leave the remaining
details to the reader.
∎

6.
Comparison with intrinsic mirror symmetry

We continue with
g:X\rightarrow S
in the absolute or relative cases satisfying
Assumptions
1.1
or
1.2
. Having now constructed a
consistent wall structure
\mathscr{S}_{{\mathrm{can}}}
on
B
, we observe that the data

(B,\mathscr{P})
and
\mathscr{S}_{{\mathrm{can}}}
are
conical
in the sense of
[
GHS
, Def. 3.20]
. Thus this data first specifies a scheme
9
9

9

This scheme is
written as
\mathfrak{X}^{\circ}
in
[
GHS
]
; we include the check here to indicate
it is the mirror to
(X,D)
.

\check{\mathfrak{X}}^{\circ}
flat over
\Bbbk[Q]/I
, as
constructed in
[
GHS
, Prop. 2.4]
by gluing together copies of spectra of
rings
R_{\mathfrak{u}}
and
R_{\mathfrak{b}}
, as well as some additional rings corresponding
to
n-1
dimensional cells of
\mathscr{P}_{\mathscr{S}}
contained in
\partial B
. We
refer the reader to the cited result for details. Most importantly for us,

[
GHS
, Prop. 3.21]
tells us that
R:=\Gamma(\check{\mathfrak{X}}^{\circ},\mathcal{O}_{\check{\mathfrak{X}}^{\circ}})
is freely generated as a
\Bbbk[Q]/I
-module by a basis

\{\vartheta_{p}\,|\,p\in B(\mathbb{Z})\}
. Further,
\check{\mathfrak{X}}:=\operatorname{Spec}R
contains

\check{\mathfrak{X}}^{\circ}
as a dense open subscheme and is also flat over
\operatorname{Spec}\Bbbk[Q]/I
. The theta functions
\vartheta_{p}
restrict on standard pieces
\operatorname{Spec}R_{\mathfrak{u}}
to the expressions
\vartheta_{p}(x)
for
x\in\operatorname{Int}(\mathfrak{u})
defined in
Definition
4.4
.

In the relative case,
R
is naturally a graded ring via

\deg(\vartheta_{p})=g_{\mathrm{trop}}(p)\in\mathbb{N}
. Thus to obtain the mirror
in the relative case, one considers instead the flat family

\operatorname{Proj}R\rightarrow\operatorname{Spec}\Bbbk[Q]/I
; see
[
GHS
, §4]

and
[
GS7
, §1]
for more details on this point of view.

[
GHS
, Thm. 3.24]
gives a description of the structure constants
for
R
. Write, for
p_{1},p_{2}\in B(\mathbb{Z})\setminus\{0\}
,

\vartheta_{p_{1}}\cdot\vartheta_{p_{2}}=\sum_{r\in B(\mathbb{Z})}\alpha^{\mathrm{trop}}_{p_{1}p_{2}r}\cdot\vartheta_{r}

with
\alpha^{\mathrm{trop}}_{p_{1}p_{2}r}\in\Bbbk[Q]/I
. These structure constants,
written as
\alpha_{r}(p_{1},p_{2})
in
[
GHS
]
,
are defined tropically as follows. Let
\mathfrak{u}
be a
chamber of
\mathscr{S}_{{\mathrm{can}}}
such that
r\in\mathfrak{u}
, and let
x\in\mathfrak{u}

be general. Then

(6.1)

\alpha^{\mathrm{trop}}_{p_{1}p_{2}r}=\sum_{(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})}a_{\beta_{1}}a_{\beta_{2}}

where the sum is over all pairs
(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})
of broken lines
with asymptotic monomials
p_{1},p_{2}
respectively, with endpoint
x
,
and such that
m_{\beta_{1}}+m_{\beta_{2}}=r
, viewed as an equation in

\Lambda_{\mathfrak{u}}
. We note that the restriction that
p_{1},p_{2}\not=0

is not important as
\vartheta_{0}
was defined as the unit in the ring
R
.

On the other hand,
[
GS7
]
defines structure constants on
the free
\Bbbk[Q]/I
-module with basis
\{\vartheta_{p}\,|\,p\in B(\mathbb{Z})\}

directly in terms of punctured Gromov-Witten invariants. Following
the notation of
[
GS7
]
, we have a potentially different
product rule

\vartheta_{p_{1}}\cdot\vartheta_{p_{2}}=\sum_{r\in B(\mathbb{Z})}\alpha^{\log}_{p_{1}p_{2}r}\cdot\vartheta_{r}.

These structure constants are written simply as
\alpha_{p_{1}p_{2}r}

in
[
GS7
]
. They are given by a formula

(6.2)

\alpha^{\log}_{p_{1}p_{2}r}=\sum_{A}N^{A}_{p_{1}p_{2}r}t^{A}\in\Bbbk[Q]/I,

where the numbers
N^{A}_{p_{1}p_{2}r}
are defined in

[
GS7
, Def. 3.21]
as certain punctured invariants with contact orders
p_{1},p_{2}
and
-r
.

In this section, we show that in fact we obtain the same algebra,
i.e.,

Theorem 6.1
.

For all
p_{1},p_{2}\in B(\mathbb{Z})\setminus\{0\}
,
r\in B(\mathbb{Z})
,

\alpha^{\mathrm{trop}}_{p_{1}p_{2}r}=\alpha^{\log}_{p_{1}p_{2}r}.

Before proving this theorem, let us introduce some additional terminology.

Definition 6.2
.

A
product type
with inputs
p_{1},p_{2}\in B(\mathbb{Z})
and output
r\in B(\mathbb{Z})

is a type
\tau=(G,{\boldsymbol{\sigma}},{\mathbf{u}})
of tropical map to
\Sigma(X)

such that:

(1)

G
is a genus zero graph with
L(G)=\{L_{1},L_{2},L_{\mathrm{out}}\}
with

p_{i}\in{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(L_{i})
,
\mathbf{u}(L_{i})=p_{i}
,
{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(L_{\mathrm{out}})\in\mathscr{P}
,

r\in{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(L_{\mathrm{out}})
and

{\bf u}(L_{\mathrm{out}})=-r
.

(2)

\tau
is realizable and balanced.

(3)

Let
h:\Gamma(G,\ell)\rightarrow\Sigma(X)
be the corresponding universal
family of tropical maps, and let

\tau_{v_{\mathrm{out}}}\in\Gamma(G,\ell)
be the cone corresponding to the vertex

v_{\mathrm{out}}
of
G
adjacent to
L_{\mathrm{out}}
. Then
\dim\tau=\dim h(\tau_{v_{\mathrm{out}}})=n
.

A
decorated product type
is a decorated type

{\boldsymbol{\tau}}=(\tau,\mathbf{A})
with
\tau
a product type.

Identically to Lemmas
3.9
and

3.20
, we have

Lemma 6.3
.

Let
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}
be a decorated product type. Then
\mathscr{M}(X,{\boldsymbol{\tau}})
is proper
over
\operatorname{Spec}\Bbbk
and carries a virtual fundamental class of dimension zero.

We now define

N_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}}:={\deg[\mathscr{M}(X,{\boldsymbol{\tau}})]^{\mathrm{virt}}\over|\operatorname{Aut}({\boldsymbol{\tau}})|}

as usual. We also have a map
h_{*}:\Lambda_{\tau_{v_{\mathrm{out}}}}\rightarrow\Lambda_{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(v_{\mathrm{out}})}
, necessarily of finite index,
and define

k_{\tau}:=|\operatorname{coker}h_{*}|=|\Lambda_{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(v_{\mathrm{out}})}/h_{*}(\Lambda_{\tau_{v_{\mathrm{out}}}})|.

We split the proof of Theorem
6.1
into
several steps.

Theorem 6.4
.

Fix
p_{1},p_{2},r\in B(\mathbb{Z})
, and let
\mathfrak{u}
be a chamber of

\mathscr{P}_{\mathscr{B}}
containing
r
. Then there exists a top-dimensional
subcone
\mathfrak{u}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathfrak{u}
containing
r
such that

\alpha^{\log}_{p_{1}p_{2}r}=\sum_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}=(\tau,{\bf A})}k_{\tau}N_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}}t^{A}\in\Bbbk[Q]/I,

where the sum is over all isomorphism classes of
decorated product types with inputs
p_{1},p_{2}
,
output
r
, and
\mathfrak{u}^{\prime}\subseteq h(\tau_{v_{\mathrm{out}}})
. Here
A
is the
total class of
{\bf A}
.

Proof.

Throughout the proof, we assume familiarity with the notation of

[
GS7
]
, and do not review it, giving only references where
appropriate.

Step 1.

Alternative description of

N^{A}_{p_{1}p_{2}r}
.
Fix a curve class
A\in Q\setminus I
,
and let
\beta
be the type of punctured map of curve class
A
,
with three punctured points
x_{1},x_{2},x_{\mathrm{out}}
with contact orders given by

p_{1},p_{2}
and
-r
respectively. Thus we obtain moduli spaces of punctured
maps
\mathscr{M}(X,\beta)
and
\mathfrak{M}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}(\mathcal{X},\beta)
, where
evaluation is at
x_{\mathrm{out}}
only.

Let
\sigma_{\mathfrak{u}}\in\mathscr{P}
be the maximal cell containing
\mathfrak{u}
. Let
P_{\mathfrak{u}}

be the stalk of
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{X}
at the zero-dimensional stratum

X_{\sigma_{\mathfrak{u}}}
. Let
R:=\mathbb{N}^{n}
be generated by
e_{1},\ldots,e_{n}
, with dual
monoid generated by
e_{1}^{*},\ldots,e_{n}^{*}
. Choose a monoid homomorphism

\psi:P_{\mathfrak{u}}\rightarrow R
in such a way that the transpose

\psi^{t}:R^{\vee}\rightarrow P_{\mathfrak{u}}^{\vee}=\sigma_{\mathfrak{u}}\cap\Lambda_{\mathfrak{u}}
is
(1) injective; (2) has image contained in
\mathfrak{u}
. Further, if
r\not=0
, we also
require (3)
\psi^{t}(e_{1}^{*})=r
.

If
r\not=0
, define
W
as the log stack quotient of

\operatorname{Spec}(R\rightarrow\Bbbk)
by the
\mathbb{G}_{m}
action which acts
on the torsor associated to
m\in R
with weight
\langle e_{1}^{*},m\rangle
.
Put another way,
\underline{W}=B\mathbb{G}_{m}
,
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{W}=R
, and the
torsor associated to
m\in R
is
\mathcal{U}^{\otimes\langle e_{1}^{*},m\rangle}

where
\mathcal{U}
is the universal torsor on
B\mathbb{G}_{m}
. If, on the other hand,

r=0
, we set

W:=\operatorname{Spec}(R\rightarrow\Bbbk)\times B\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\dagger},

where
B\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\dagger}
denotes the log structure on
B\mathbb{G}_{m}

induced by the inclusion as a divisor
B\mathbb{G}_{m}\subseteq[\mathbb{A}^{1}/\mathbb{G}_{m}]
.

We may then define a morphism
g:W\rightarrow\mathscr{P}(X,r)
, where the latter
stack is as defined in
[
GS7
, §3.1]
. Using the notation of
[
GS7
]
,
write
Z_{r}
for the stratum
X_{\sigma}
where
\sigma\in\mathscr{P}
is the minimal cone
containing
r
. Then
\mathscr{P}(X,r)
may be described as
[Z_{r}/\mathbb{G}_{m}]
, where

\mathbb{G}_{m}
acts trivially on
\underline{Z}_{r}
and acts on the log structure on
Z_{r}
as
described in
[
GS7
, Rem. 3.1]
. Note that since
r\in\sigma_{\mathfrak{u}}
,

X_{\sigma_{\mathfrak{u}}}\subseteq Z_{r}
. There then exists a
morphism
g^{\prime}:\operatorname{Spec}(R\rightarrow\Bbbk)\rightarrow X_{\sigma_{\mathfrak{u}}}

which is an isomorphism on underlying schemes and such that

\overline{(g^{\prime})}^{\flat}=\psi
. We also denote by
g^{\prime}
the composition with the
inclusion into
Z_{r}
. If
r\not=0
, then the action of
\mathbb{G}_{m}
on

\operatorname{Spec}(R\rightarrow\Bbbk)
used to define
W
is compatible with the action of

\mathbb{G}_{m}
on
Z_{r}
using the fact that
\psi^{t}(e_{1}^{*})=r
and the description of

[
GS7
, Rem. 3.1]
, and hence
g^{\prime}
descends to the desired map

g:W\rightarrow\mathscr{P}(X,r)
. If, on the other hand,
r=0
,
\mathscr{P}(X,r)=X\times B\mathbb{G}_{m}
, and hence we obtain the desired morphism
g:W\rightarrow\mathscr{P}(X,r)
as
the product of
g^{\prime}
with the morphism
B\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\dagger}\rightarrow B\mathbb{G}_{m}
which
is an isomorphism on underlying stacks.

We have canonical morphisms
{\operatorname{ev}}_{\mathcal{X}}:\mathfrak{M}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}(\mathcal{X},\beta)\rightarrow\mathscr{P}(X,r)
and
{\operatorname{ev}}_{X}:\mathscr{M}(X,\beta)\rightarrow\mathscr{P}(X,r)
,
see
[
GS7
, Def. 3.5]
. We may then define

\mathfrak{M}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}(\mathcal{X},\beta)_{W}=W\times_{\mathscr{P}(X,r)}^{\mathrm{fs}}\mathfrak{M}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}(\mathcal{X},\beta),\quad\mathscr{M}(X,\beta)_{W}=W\times_{\mathscr{P}(X,r)}^{\mathrm{fs}}\mathscr{M}(X,\beta).

By choosing the
image of
\psi^{t}
to be sufficiently small, we may assume that

g
is transverse to
{\operatorname{ev}}_{\mathcal{X}}
in the sense of
[
GS7
, Def. 2.6]
,
see
[
GS7
, Thm. 2.9]
for the necessary tropical criterion.
By
[
GS7
, Def. 7.9]
, we then obtain a number

N^{A,W}_{p_{1}p_{2}r}=\deg[\mathscr{M}(X,\beta)_{W}]^{\mathrm{virt}}

with the virtual fundamental class defined via an obstruction theory
for

\varepsilon_{W}:\mathscr{M}(X,\beta)_{W}\rightarrow\mathfrak{M}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}(X,\beta)_{W}

pulled back from that for
\mathscr{M}(X,\beta)\rightarrow\mathfrak{M}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}(X,\beta)
. By

[
GS7
, Lem. 7.8]
,
\mathfrak{M}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}(X,\beta)_{W}

is pure-dimensional of dimension zero, so that the virtual pull-back
of its fundamental class gives the virtual fundamental class
of
\mathscr{M}(X,\beta)_{W}
.

The hypotheses of
[
GS7
, Thm. 7.10]
now hold, and we
obtain
N^{A,W}_{p_{1}p_{2}r}=N^{A}_{p_{1}p_{2}r}
. Thus by
(
6.2
), to prove the theorem it is sufficient to take

\mathfrak{u}^{\prime}:=\psi^{t}(R^{\vee}_{\mathbb{R}})
and show that

N^{A,W}_{p_{1}p_{2}r}=\sum_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}}k_{\tau}N_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}}

where the sum is over all isomorphism classes of decorated product types
with total class
A
as in the statement of the theorem.

We now make a slight change in the definition of
W
,
\mathfrak{M}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}(\mathcal{X},\beta)_{W}

and
\mathscr{M}(X,\beta)_{W}
in the case that
r=0
, to make the remainder
of the proof more uniform. Note that in this case,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{M}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}(\mathcal{X},\beta)_{W}={}

\displaystyle(\operatorname{Spec}(R\rightarrow\Bbbk)\times B\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\dagger})\times^{\mathrm{fs}}_{X\times B\mathbb{G}_{m}}\mathfrak{M}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}(\mathcal{X},\beta)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle B\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\dagger}\times_{B\mathbb{G}_{m}}((\operatorname{Spec}(R\rightarrow\Bbbk)\times B\mathbb{G}_{m})\times^{\mathrm{fs}}_{\mathscr{P}(X,r)}\mathfrak{M}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}(\mathcal{X},\beta)).

The stack
\mathfrak{M}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}(\mathcal{X},\beta)_{W}
then has the same underlying stack
as

(\operatorname{Spec}(R\rightarrow\Bbbk)\times B\mathbb{G}_{m})\times_{\mathscr{P}(X,r)}^{\mathrm{fs}}\mathfrak{M}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}(\mathcal{X},\beta),

but with the ghost sheaf on the former being a sum of the ghost sheaf of the
latter and the constant sheaf
\underline{\mathbb{N}}
. As we are only concerned about virtual
fundamental classes, which are defined using the underlying morphism of stacks

\underline{\mathscr{M}(X,\beta)}_{W}\rightarrow\underline{\mathfrak{M}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}(\mathcal{X},\beta)}_{W}
, we may thus
replace
W
with
\operatorname{Spec}(R\rightarrow\Bbbk)\times B\mathbb{G}_{m}
, which also changes

\mathfrak{M}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}(\mathcal{X},\beta)_{W}
and
\mathscr{M}(X,\beta)_{W}
. Note that now the definition
of
W
is uniform across the two cases, and whether or not
r=0
, the ghost
sheaf of
W
is
R
.

Step 2.

The structure of
\mathfrak{M}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}(X,\beta)_{W}
.

We recall from
[
GS7
, Lem. 7.8]
and its proof that the projection

\mathfrak{M}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}(\mathcal{X},\beta)_{W}\rightarrow W
is integral, log smooth
and of fibre dimension and
log fibre dimension one. For the latter notion, see the review of

[
GS7
, Def. A.5]
.

Let
\bar{\eta}
be a generic point of an irreducible component

\mathfrak{M}_{\bar{\eta}}
of

\mathfrak{M}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}(\mathcal{X},\beta)_{W}
. We view the former as an integral closed
substack of the latter, so that

[\mathfrak{M}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}(\mathcal{X},\beta)_{W}]=\sum_{\bar{\eta}}\mu_{\bar{\eta}}[\mathfrak{M}_{\bar{\eta}}]

in the Chow group of
\mathfrak{M}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}(\mathcal{X},\beta)_{W}
. Here the sum is over
all generic points
\bar{\eta}
, and
\mu_{\bar{\eta}}
is the multiplicity
of
\mathfrak{M}_{\bar{\eta}}
in
\mathfrak{M}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}(\mathcal{X},\beta)_{W}
.

Let
Q_{\bar{\eta}}
be the stalk of the
ghost sheaf of
\mathfrak{M}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}(\mathcal{X},\beta)_{W}
at
\bar{\eta}
.
We then have

(6.3)

\operatorname{rk}Q^{{\operatorname{gp}}}_{\bar{\eta}}=\operatorname{rk}\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{W}^{{\operatorname{gp}}}=n;

the first equality comes from
[
GS7
, (A.1)]

and the above-mentioned equality of log fibre dimension and fibre dimension,
while the second equality is from the definition of
W
, as
R=\mathbb{N}^{n}
.
Let
\tau=(G,{\boldsymbol{\sigma}},{\bf u})
be the type of the punctured map corresponding
to
\bar{\eta}
.

Note that
\varepsilon_{W}^{!}[\mathfrak{M}_{\bar{\eta}}]
is a zero-dimensional cycle
in the Chow group of
\mathscr{M}(X,\beta)_{W}
. Assume that

\deg\varepsilon_{W}^{!}[\mathfrak{M}_{\bar{\eta}}]\not=0
.
We claim that in this case,
\tau
is a product type.
Definition
6.2
,(1) is immediate by the definition of

\beta
. For Condition (2) of the definition, note that

\tau
is necessarily realizable as it is the type of a punctured map
to
\mathcal{X}
. On the other hand, the assumption of non-zero virtual degree
implies that for some decoration
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}
of

\tau
with total curve class
A
,
\mathscr{M}(X,{\boldsymbol{\tau}})
is non-empty.
In particular, one may find a punctured map
C^{\circ}\rightarrow X

defined over a geometric point whose type is
\tau^{\prime}
with a contraction

\tau^{\prime}\rightarrow\tau
. Hence by Lemma
2.1
,
\tau^{\prime}

is a balanced type, and hence
\tau
is also balanced.

For Condition (3) of the definition of product type, let

Q_{\tau}
be the basic monoid associated to the type
\tau
, so that

Q^{\vee}_{\tau,\mathbb{R}}
coincides with the cone
\tau
. Note that in
general
Q_{\bar{\eta}}\not=Q_{\tau}
, as the fibre
product construction of
\mathfrak{M}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}(\mathcal{X},\beta)_{W}
changes the
log structure. We wish to show that
\dim\tau=n
.

From
[
ACGS1
, Prop. 5.2]
, we have

Q_{\bar{\eta},\mathbb{R}}^{\vee}=\tau\times_{\sigma_{\mathfrak{u}}}R^{\vee}_{\mathbb{R}}

where the map
R^{\vee}_{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow\sigma_{\mathfrak{u}}
is just the
inclusion

\psi^{t}
and the map
\tau\rightarrow\sigma_{\mathfrak{u}}
, induced
by the morphism
{\operatorname{ev}}_{\mathcal{X}}
, is given
by evaluation at the vertex
v_{\mathrm{out}}
adjacent to
L_{\mathrm{out}}
, i.e.,
is
h|_{\tau_{v_{\mathrm{out}}}}
. See
[
GS7
, Lem. 3.23]
for this latter
statement.

Note the images of the two projections

Q_{\bar{\eta},\mathbb{R}}^{\vee}\rightarrow\tau,R^{\vee}_{\mathbb{R}}
intersect
the interiors of
\tau
and
R^{\vee}_{\mathbb{R}}
respectively.
This is because these morphisms of cones are dual to local morphisms
of monoids, being induced by the two projections of log stacks

\mathfrak{M}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}(\mathcal{X},\beta)_{W}\rightarrow\mathfrak{M}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}(\mathcal{X},\beta),W
.
From this we may calculate the dimension of
Q^{\vee}_{\bar{\eta},\mathbb{R}}

by computing its tangent space at a point in the interior
of
Q^{\vee}_{\bar{\eta},\mathbb{R}}
mapping to the interiors of
\tau
and

R^{\vee}_{\mathbb{R}}
via the two projections.
This tangent space is
Q^{*}_{\bar{\eta},\mathbb{R}}=\tau^{{\operatorname{gp}}}\times_{\sigma_{\mathfrak{u}}^{{\operatorname{gp}}}}R_{\mathbb{R}}^{*}=\tau^{{\operatorname{gp}}}
as
R^{*}_{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow\sigma^{{\operatorname{gp}}}_{\mathfrak{u}}
is
an isomorphism of real vector spaces. Thus

\dim\tau=\dim Q^{\vee}_{\bar{\eta},\mathbb{R}}=n
, the latter equality
by (
6.3
). Hence
\dim\tau=\dim h(\tau_{v_{\mathrm{out}}})=n
, as
desired.

Step 3.

The multiplicity of
\mathfrak{M}_{\bar{\eta}}
in

\mathfrak{M}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}(\mathcal{X},\beta)_{W}
.
We use the log smoothness of the projection

\mathfrak{M}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}(\mathcal{X},\eta)_{W}\rightarrow W
already mentioned in Step 2. Note that

W
carries a natural idealized log structure given by the ideal

R\setminus\{0\}\subseteq R
. This ideal pulls back to give an idealized
structure on
\mathfrak{M}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}(\mathcal{X},\beta)_{W}
, and an idealized strict
morphism
10
10

10

We recall a morphism of idealized log schemes

f:(X,\mathcal{M}_{X},\mathcal{K}_{X})\rightarrow(Y,\mathcal{M}_{Y},\mathcal{K}_{Y})
is idealized strict
if
\mathcal{K}_{X}
agrees with the monoid ideal generated by
f^{\flat}(\mathcal{K}_{Y})
.

is idealized log smooth if and only if it is log smooth, see
[
Og
, IV,Variant
3.1.22]
. Thus
\mathfrak{M}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}(\mathcal{X},\beta)_{W}\rightarrow W
is idealized log
smooth, and hence, smooth locally at
\bar{\eta}
, is given as

\mathcal{A}_{Q_{\bar{\eta}},K}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}_{R,R\setminus\{0\}}
by
[
ACGS2
, Prop. B.4]
, where
K\subseteq Q_{\bar{\eta}}
is the monoid ideal generated by
the image of
R\setminus\{0\}
in
Q_{\bar{\eta}}
under the map

R\rightarrow Q_{\bar{\eta}}
.

The multiplicity
\mu_{\bar{\eta}}
of the component
\mathfrak{M}_{\bar{\eta}}
may now be
calculated as the multiplicity of
\mathcal{A}_{Q_{\bar{\eta}},K}
,
or equivalently,
\dim_{\Bbbk}\Bbbk[Q_{\bar{\eta}}]/K
. Note that

Q^{\vee}_{\bar{\eta},\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow R^{\vee}_{\mathbb{R}}

is surjective, as follows from the integrality of

\mathfrak{M}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}(\mathcal{X},\beta)_{W}\rightarrow W
and
[
GS7
, Prop. 2.4]
.
Further,

Q^{\vee}_{\bar{\eta},\mathbb{R}}
and
R^{\vee}_{\mathbb{R}}

are of the same dimension. It thus follows that

R_{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow Q_{\bar{\eta},\mathbb{R}}
is an isomorphism of real cones.
Identifying
Q^{{\operatorname{gp}}}_{\bar{\eta}}
with a super-lattice of

R^{{\operatorname{gp}}}=\mathbb{Z}^{n}
, the multiplicity is then the number of
points of
Q^{{\operatorname{gp}}}_{\bar{\eta}}
contained in
[0,1)^{n}\subseteq R_{\mathbb{R}}
.
This may be expressed as

(6.4)

\mu_{\tau}:=\mu_{\bar{\eta}}=|\operatorname{coker}(R^{{\operatorname{gp}}}\rightarrow Q^{{\operatorname{gp}}}_{\bar{\eta}})|,

noting this expression only depends on the type
\tau
.

Step 4.

Comparison with the
N_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}}
.

Consider the commutative diagram

\textstyle{\mathfrak{M}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}(\mathcal{X},\tau)_{W}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j^{\prime}_{\tau}}
\scriptstyle{k}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{M}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}_{\tau}(\mathcal{X},\beta)_{W}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{\tau}^{\prime}}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{M}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}(\mathcal{X},\beta)_{W}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{M}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}(\mathcal{X},\tau)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j_{\tau}}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{M}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}_{\tau}(\mathcal{X},\beta)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{\tau}}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{M}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}(\mathcal{X},\beta)}

where

\mathfrak{M}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}(\mathcal{X},\tau)_{W}=W\times^{\mathrm{fs}}_{\mathscr{P}(X,r)}\mathfrak{M}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}(\mathcal{X},\tau),\quad\mathfrak{M}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}_{\tau}(\mathcal{X},\beta)_{W}=W\times^{\mathrm{fs}}_{\mathscr{P}(X,r)}\mathfrak{M}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}_{\tau}(\mathcal{X},\beta).

In this diagram, all squares are Cartesian in both the log and fs log
categories, as all horizontal morphisms are strict. The morphism
\iota_{\tau}
,
hence also
\iota^{\prime}_{\tau}
, is a closed embedding,
while

j_{\tau}
, hence also
j^{\prime}_{\tau}
, is a finite morphism of degree

|\operatorname{Aut}(\tau)|
. Further, as
W\rightarrow\mathscr{P}(X,r)
is a finite morphism, so
is
k
.

For a stack
M
, denote by
M_{{\operatorname{red}}}
the reduction. Then

(6.5)

\iota^{\prime}_{\tau,*}[\mathfrak{M}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}_{\tau}(\mathcal{X},\beta)_{W,{\operatorname{red}}}]=\sum_{\bar{\eta}}[\mathfrak{M}_{\bar{\eta}}]

where the sum is over all generic points
\bar{\eta}
of

\mathfrak{M}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}(\mathcal{X},\beta)_{W}
whose type is
\tau
. Indeed, any
such generic point
\bar{\eta}
corresponds to a punctured map

C^{\circ}/\bar{\eta}\rightarrow\mathcal{X}
in
\mathfrak{M}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}(\mathcal{X},\beta)

of type
\tau
, hence lies in
\mathfrak{M}_{\tau}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}(\mathcal{X},\beta)
.
Combining (
6.5
) with
\deg j^{\prime}_{\tau}=|\operatorname{Aut}(\tau)|
gives

(6.6)

(\iota_{\tau}^{\prime}\circ j^{\prime}_{\tau})_{*}[\mathfrak{M}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}(\mathcal{X},\tau)_{W,{\operatorname{red}}}]=|\operatorname{Aut}(\tau)|\sum_{\bar{\eta}}[\mathfrak{M}_{\bar{\eta}}].

Let

k_{{\operatorname{red}}}:\mathfrak{M}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}(\mathcal{X},\tau)_{W,{\operatorname{red}}}\rightarrow\mathfrak{M}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}(\mathcal{X},\tau)

be the morphism induced by
k
. Bearing in mind that

\mathfrak{M}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}(\mathcal{X},\tau)
is reduced,
we may now calculate the
degree of the finite morphism
k_{{\operatorname{red}}}
as follows. We take a
(strict) geometric generic point

\operatorname{Spec}\kappa\rightarrow\mathfrak{M}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}(\mathcal{X},\tau)
.
The desired degree is the length of the reduction of the fibre

(6.7)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\operatorname{Spec}\kappa\times_{\mathfrak{M}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}(\mathcal{X},\tau)}\mathfrak{M}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}(\mathcal{X},\tau)_{W}={}&\operatorname{Spec}\kappa\times_{\mathfrak{M}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}(\mathcal{X},\tau)}(\mathfrak{M}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}(\mathcal{X},\tau)\times^{\mathrm{fs}}_{\mathscr{P}(X,r)}W)\\
={}&\operatorname{Spec}(Q_{\tau}\rightarrow\kappa)\times^{\mathrm{fs}}_{\mathscr{P}(X,r)}W.\end{split}

We may replace
\mathscr{P}(X,r)
with the strict closed substack
Z

with underlying closed substack
\underline{X}_{\sigma_{\mathfrak{u}}}\times B\mathbb{G}_{m}

as the morphism
\operatorname{Spec}\kappa\rightarrow\mathscr{P}(X,r)
factors through
this closed substack. Further, the morphism

\operatorname{Spec}\kappa\rightarrow Z
factors through
X_{\sigma_{\mathfrak{u}}}

as all line bundles on
\operatorname{Spec}\kappa
are trivial. Thus we may rewrite
(
6.7
) as

(6.8)

\operatorname{Spec}(Q_{\tau}\rightarrow\kappa)\times^{\mathrm{fs}}_{X_{\sigma_{\mathfrak{u}}}}\operatorname{Spec}(R\rightarrow\Bbbk)=(A_{Q_{\tau},Q_{\tau}\setminus\{0\}}\times^{\mathrm{fs}}_{A_{P_{\mathfrak{u}},P_{\mathfrak{u}}\setminus\{0\}}}A_{R,R\setminus\{0\}})\times_{\operatorname{Spec}\Bbbk}\operatorname{Spec}\kappa,

again following the notation of
[
ACGS2
, §B]
.
By
[
GS7
, Prop. A.4,(4)]
, (
6.8
) may then be expressed as

A_{Q_{\tau}\oplus^{\mathrm{fs}}_{P_{\mathfrak{u}}}R,J}\times_{\operatorname{Spec}\Bbbk}\operatorname{Spec}\kappa

where
J
is the ideal generated by the image of

Q_{\tau}\setminus\{0\}
and
R\setminus\{0\}
.
We pass to the reduction
by replacing
J
with its radical, which, by the explicit
description of the fs push-out of
[
GS7
, Prop. A.4,(3)]
,
is the complement of the group of
invertible elements of
Q_{\tau}\oplus_{P_{\mathfrak{u}}}^{\mathrm{fs}}R
.
Further, this group of invertible elements
is the torsion part of the cokernel of
the map
(\varphi_{1},-\varphi_{2}):P_{\mathfrak{u}}^{{\operatorname{gp}}}\rightarrow Q_{\tau}^{{\operatorname{gp}}}\oplus R^{{\operatorname{gp}}}
,
where
\varphi_{1}:P_{\mathfrak{u}}^{{\operatorname{gp}}}\rightarrow Q_{\tau}^{{\operatorname{gp}}}

and
\varphi_{2}:P_{\mathfrak{u}}^{{\operatorname{gp}}}\rightarrow R^{{\operatorname{gp}}}
are induced
by
\mathfrak{M}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}(\mathcal{X},\tau)\rightarrow\mathscr{P}(X,r)
and

W\rightarrow\mathscr{P}(X,r)
respectively.
Thus we see that the reduced fibre is

\operatorname{Spec}\kappa[\operatorname{coker}(P_{\mathfrak{u}}^{{\operatorname{gp}}}\rightarrow Q_{\tau}^{{\operatorname{gp}}}\oplus R^{{\operatorname{gp}}})_{\mathrm{tors}}],

whose length of course is given by the order of this group of invertible
elements.

In conclusion, we see that

(6.9)

k_{{\operatorname{red}},*}[\mathfrak{M}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}(\mathcal{X},\tau)_{W,{\operatorname{red}}}]=|\operatorname{coker}(P_{\mathfrak{u}}^{{\operatorname{gp}}}\rightarrow Q_{\tau}^{{\operatorname{gp}}}\oplus R^{{\operatorname{gp}}})_{\mathrm{tors}}|[\mathfrak{M}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}(\mathcal{X},\tau)].

Bearing in mind that

Q_{\bar{\eta}}=(Q_{\tau}\oplus^{\mathrm{fs}}_{P_{\mathfrak{u}}}R)/\mathrm{tors}
, it
again follows from the explict description of the fs pushout in

[
GS7
, Prop. A.4,(3)]
that

Q_{\bar{\eta}}^{{\operatorname{gp}}}=\operatorname{coker}(P_{\mathfrak{u}}^{{\operatorname{gp}}}\rightarrow Q_{\tau}^{{\operatorname{gp}}}\oplus R^{{\operatorname{gp}}})/\mathrm{tors},

and then a simple diagram chase gives

(6.10)

\displaystyle\begin{split}|\operatorname{coker}(P_{\mathfrak{u}}^{{\operatorname{gp}}}\rightarrow Q_{\tau}^{{\operatorname{gp}}}\oplus R^{{\operatorname{gp}}})_{\mathrm{tors}}||\operatorname{coker}(R^{{\operatorname{gp}}}\rightarrow Q_{\bar{\eta}}^{{\operatorname{gp}}})|={}&|\operatorname{coker}(P_{\mathfrak{u}}^{{\operatorname{gp}}}\rightarrow Q_{\tau}^{{\operatorname{gp}}})|\\
={}&|\operatorname{coker}(Q_{\tau}^{*}\rightarrow P_{\mathfrak{u}}^{*})|=k_{\tau}.\end{split}

We next consider the diagram

\textstyle{\coprod_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}=(\tau,{\bf A})}\mathscr{M}(X,{\boldsymbol{\tau}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon_{\tau}}
\textstyle{\coprod_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}=(\tau,{\bf A})}\mathscr{M}(X,{\boldsymbol{\tau}})_{W}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon_{\tau,W}}
\scriptstyle{k^{\prime}}
\textstyle{\mathscr{M}(X,\beta)_{W}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon_{W}}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{M}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}(\mathcal{X},\tau)}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{M}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}(\mathcal{X},\tau)_{W}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{k}
\scriptstyle{\iota^{\prime}_{\tau}\circ j^{\prime}_{\tau}}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{M}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}(\mathcal{X},\beta)_{W}}

where the disjoint unions are over all decorations of
\tau
with total
degree
A
.
Now observe that

\displaystyle N^{A,W}_{p_{1}p_{2}r}=\deg[\mathscr{M}(X,\beta)_{W}]^{\mathrm{virt}}={}

\displaystyle\deg\varepsilon_{W}^{!}[\mathfrak{M}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}(\mathcal{X},\beta)_{W}]

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\deg\sum_{\bar{\eta}}\mu_{\bar{\eta}}\varepsilon_{W}^{!}[\mathfrak{M}_{\bar{\eta}}].

Here we are summing over all generic points
\bar{\eta}
of

\mathfrak{M}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}(\mathcal{X},\beta)_{W}
, but of course we may restrict the
sum to those
\bar{\eta}
such that

\deg\varepsilon^{!}_{W}[\mathfrak{M}_{\bar{\eta}}]\not=0
.
Thus by (
6.4
), (
6.6
) and Step 2, we may rewrite this equation as

N^{A,W}_{p_{1}p_{2}r}=\deg\sum_{\tau}{\mu_{\tau}\over|\operatorname{Aut}(\tau)|}\varepsilon_{W}^{!}(\iota^{\prime}_{\tau}\circ j^{\prime}_{\tau})_{*}[\mathfrak{M}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}(\mathcal{X},\tau)_{W,{\operatorname{red}}}]

where, by Step 2, the sum is now over all product types

\tau
with inputs
p_{1},p_{2}
,
output
r
, and
\mathfrak{u}^{\prime}\subseteq h_{\tau}(\tau_{v_{\mathrm{out}}})
. Now
using the push-pull formula for virtual pull-back of
[
Mn
, Thm. 4.1]
for
the first and third equality, as well as (
6.4
),
(
6.9
) and (
6.10
) for the third, we obtain

\displaystyle N^{A,W}_{p_{1}p_{2}r}={}

\displaystyle\deg\sum_{\tau}{\mu_{\tau}\over|\operatorname{Aut}(\tau)|}\varepsilon_{\tau,W}^{!}[\mathfrak{M}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}(\mathcal{X},\tau)_{W,{\operatorname{red}}}]

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\deg\sum_{\tau}{\mu_{\tau}\over|\operatorname{Aut}(\tau)|}k^{\prime}_{*}\varepsilon_{\tau,W}^{!}[\mathfrak{M}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}(\mathcal{X},\tau)_{W,{\operatorname{red}}}]

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\deg\sum_{\tau}{k_{\tau}\over|\operatorname{Aut}(\tau)|}\varepsilon_{\tau}^{!}[\mathfrak{M}^{{\operatorname{ev}}}(\mathcal{X},\tau)]

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\sum_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}=(\tau,{\bf A})}k_{\tau}N_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}},

as desired.
∎

Lemma 6.5
.

Let
\tau=(G,{\boldsymbol{\sigma}},{\mathbf{u}})
be a product type with

N_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}}\not=0
for some decoration
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}
of
\tau
, and let
G^{\prime}

be the spine of
G
in the sense of Definition
2.4
.
Then
v_{\mathrm{out}}
is the unique trivalent vertex of
G^{\prime}
.
Further,
v_{\mathrm{out}}
is also a trivalent vertex of
G
, and if

v_{\mathrm{out}}
and
L_{\mathrm{out}}
are removed from
G
, then
\tau
splits into
two broken line types
\tau_{1},\tau_{2}
.

Proof.

Since
G^{\prime}
has three legs and no univalent vertices,
G^{\prime}
has a unique trivalent
vertex. Suppose that
v_{\mathrm{out}}
is not this trivalent vertex. Necessarily

{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(v_{\mathrm{out}})
is a maximal cell
\sigma\in\mathscr{P}^{{\operatorname{max}}}
since
\dim h(\tau_{\mathrm{out}})=n
, and thus for any punctured map
f:C^{\circ}\rightarrow X
in
the non-empty moduli space
\mathscr{M}(X,{\boldsymbol{\tau}})
, the union of irreducible components
of
C^{\circ}
corresponding to
v_{\mathrm{out}}
are mapped to the zero-dimensional
stratum
X_{\sigma}
. Hence by stability,
v_{\mathrm{out}}
must be at least trivalent
in
G
, and thus there is an edge
E
adjacent to
v_{\mathrm{out}}
which is not
contained in
G^{\prime}
. Let
\bar{\tau}=(\bar{G},\bar{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}},\bar{\bf u})
be the type
obtained by cutting
G
at the edge
E
and taking the connected component of
the resulting graph not containing
L_{1},L_{2}
and
L_{\mathrm{out}}
.
Thus
E
becomes the unique leg of

\bar{G}
. Let
h_{\bar{\tau}}
be the universal tropical map of type
\bar{\tau}
.
If
\bar{\tau}_{E}\in\Gamma(\bar{G},\bar{\ell})
is the cone corresponding to
E
,
then by Lemma
2.5
,(1),

\dim h_{\bar{\tau}}(\bar{\tau}_{E})\leq n-1
. But since
\dim h(\tau_{v_{\mathrm{out}}})=n
, we obtain a contradiction. Thus

v_{\mathrm{out}}
is a trivalent vertex of
G^{\prime}
.

A similar argument applies to show that
v_{\mathrm{out}}
is also trivalent in

G
: if the valency is higher than three, with an adjacent edge
E

not an edge of
G^{\prime}
, we may again cut at
E
to obtain a contradiction
as above.

Thus by removing
v_{\mathrm{out}},L_{\mathrm{out}}
from
G
, we obtain, for
i=1,2
, types

\tau_{i}
with legs
L_{i},L_{i,\mathrm{out}}
, where
L_{1,\mathrm{out}},L_{2,\mathrm{out}}
arise from
the edges (or legs)

E_{1},E_{2}
adjacent to
v_{\mathrm{out}}

other than
L_{\mathrm{out}}
. We only need to show that
\tau_{i}
is a broken line type.

First consider Condition (1) of Definition
3.19
.
The only thing to check here is that
{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(L_{i,\mathrm{out}})\in\mathscr{P}

and that
{\bf u}(L_{i,\mathrm{out}})\not=0
. For the first statement, note that
as
{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(v_{\mathrm{out}})=\sigma
and
{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(v_{\mathrm{out}})\subseteq{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(E_{i})={\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(L_{i,\mathrm{out}})
,
necessarily
{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(L_{i,\mathrm{out}})=\sigma

also. For the second statement, note that if
{\bf u}(L_{i,\mathrm{out}})=0
,
then we may vary
s\in\tau
by changing only
the affine length of the edge
E_{i}
of
G
, so that
h_{s}(v_{\mathrm{out}})

remains unchanged. Since
\dim h(\tau_{v_{\mathrm{out}}})=n
, this would
imply that
\dim\tau>n
, a contradiction.

Condition (2) of Definition
3.19
is immediate.
Finally, for Condition (3), it follows from

\dim h(\tau_{v_{\mathrm{out}}})=n
that
\dim h_{\tau_{i}}(\tau_{i,\mathrm{out}})=n
,
and in particular
\dim\tau_{i}\geq n-1
. However, if
\dim\tau_{i}>n-1

for
i=1
or
2
, it then immediately follows by gluing together
tropical maps in the families
\tau_{1},\tau_{2}
that
\dim\tau>n
.
Thus we get
\dim\tau_{i}=n-1
, as desired.
∎

Lemma 6.6
.

Let
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}
be a decorated product type with
N_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}}\not=0
, and let

{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{2}
be the decorated broken line types obtained from splitting

{\boldsymbol{\tau}}
at
v_{\mathrm{out}}
. Then

k_{\tau}N_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}}=k_{\tau_{1}}k_{\tau_{2}}N_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{1}}N_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{2}}.

Proof.

The proof is by a straightforward application of Yixian Wu’s gluing formula
Theorem
A.4
. We first assume that neither
\tau_{1}
nor

\tau_{2}
is a trivial broken line, that is, has no vertex. Then
v_{\mathrm{out}}
has
two adjacent edges
E_{1}
,
E_{2}
. Splitting
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}
at these two edges leads to,
by Lemma
6.5
,
the decorated broken line types
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{1}
,
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{2}
, and a third decorated type

{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{0}=(\tau_{0},\mathbf{A}_{0})
,
\tau_{0}=(G_{0},{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{0},\mathbf{u}_{0})
with only one
vertex
v_{\mathrm{out}}
and three adjacent legs
(E_{1},v_{\mathrm{out}})
,
(E_{2},v_{\mathrm{out}})
and

L_{\mathrm{out}}
, the outgoing leg of
\tau
. Moreover,
\tau_{0}
is a type entirely
contained in the maximal cell
\sigma
containing
h(\tau_{v_{\mathrm{out}}})
, that is,

{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{0}(v_{\mathrm{out}})=\sigma
. In particular, the curve
class
\mathbf{A}_{0}(v_{\mathrm{out}})
is trivial. Thus any punctured map of
type
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{0}
is constant on underlying schemes and
has domain a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
with three punctured points.
It then follows as in
[
GS7
, Claim 3.22]
that there is a
unique basic punctured map
f:C^{\circ}/W\rightarrow X
of type
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{0}

with
\underline{W}=\operatorname{Spec}\Bbbk
. Since further
\mathscr{M}(X,{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{0})

is unobstructed over
\mathfrak{M}(\mathcal{X},{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{0})
,
\mathscr{M}(X,{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{0})
is a reduced
point.

It is now clear by stability of the tropical situation under small
perturbations, and can be checked also explicitly, that the tropical gluing map

\varepsilon_{\omega}^{\operatorname{gp}}
in (
A.7
) is
surjective already for
\omega=\tau
. Thus the trivial displacement vector

\nu=0
in Definition
A.2
is general for
\tau
.
Moreover, the dimension formula (
A.8
) for

\omega=\tau
holds:

\textstyle\dim\widetilde{\tau}_{0}+\dim\widetilde{\tau}_{1}+\dim\widetilde{\tau}_{2}=(n+2)+n+n=(n+2)+2n=\dim\widetilde{\tau}+\sum_{E}\operatorname{rk}\Lambda_{E}.

Thus the set of transverse types
\Delta(\nu)
consist only of the one element

\tau
.

To compute the splitting multiplicity
m({\boldsymbol{\tau}})
(Definition
A.2
,3) consider the following commutative diagram with exact rows and
columns.

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\widetilde{\tau}^{\operatorname{gp}}_{\mathbb{Z}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(\widetilde{\tau}_{0}^{\operatorname{gp}})_{\mathbb{Z}}\times(\widetilde{\tau}_{1}^{\operatorname{gp}})_{\mathbb{Z}}\times(\widetilde{\tau}_{2}^{\operatorname{gp}})_{\mathbb{Z}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\varepsilon_{\tau}^{\operatorname{gp}})_{\mathbb{Z}}}
\textstyle{\operatorname{im}(\varepsilon_{\tau}^{\operatorname{gp}})_{\mathbb{Z}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Lambda\times\mathbb{Z}^{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(\Lambda\times\mathbb{Z}^{2})\times\Lambda\times\Lambda\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Lambda\times\Lambda\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{Z}/k_{\tau}\mathbb{Z}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{Z}/k_{\tau_{1}}\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}/k_{\tau_{2}}\mathbb{Z}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\operatorname{coker}(\varepsilon_{\tau}^{\operatorname{gp}})_{\mathbb{Z}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0}

Here
\Lambda=\sigma^{\operatorname{gp}}_{\mathbb{Z}}
, the first column is the exact sequence defining

k_{\tau}
, along with a trivial
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
factor in the first
two entries, the middle column the product of the exact sequences defining

k_{\tau_{1}}
and
k_{\tau_{2}}
, and the isomorphism

(\widetilde{\tau}_{0}^{\operatorname{gp}})_{\mathbb{Z}}\to\Lambda\times\mathbb{Z}^{2}
. The third row now shows

m({\boldsymbol{\tau}})=|\operatorname{coker}((\varepsilon_{\tau}^{\operatorname{gp}})_{\mathbb{Z}})|=\frac{k_{\tau_{1}}k_{\tau_{2}}}{k_{\tau}}.

Applying Theorem
A.4
we conclude

\deg\big[\mathscr{M}(X,{\boldsymbol{\tau}})\big]^{\mathrm{virt}}=\frac{k_{\tau_{1}}k_{\tau_{2}}}{k_{\tau}}\cdot\deg\big[\mathscr{M}(X,{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{1})\big]^{\mathrm{virt}}\cdot\deg\big[\mathscr{M}(X,{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{2})\big]^{\mathrm{virt}}.

The claimed equality
k_{\tau}N_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}}=k_{\tau_{1}}k_{\tau_{2}}N_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{1}}N_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{2}}
now follows by the definition of
N_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}}
,
N_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{1}}
,
N_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{2}}
and noting that
\operatorname{Aut}({\boldsymbol{\tau}})=\operatorname{Aut}({\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{1})\times\operatorname{Aut}({\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{2})
.

If one of
\tau_{1}
or
\tau_{2}
is a trivial broken line, the same analysis
omitting the trivial broken line factor applies to prove the result in this
case. If both
\tau_{1},\tau_{2}
are trivial broken lines the statement is
trivially true.
∎

Proof of Theorem
6.1
.

Take
x\in\operatorname{Int}(\mathfrak{u})
in the definition of
\alpha^{\mathrm{trop}}_{p_{1}p_{2}r}
;
by consistency of
\mathscr{S}_{{\mathrm{can}}}
, (
6.1
) is
independent of this choice of
x
. Further, in
(
6.1
), we may sum over decorated broken lines,
rather than broken lines, and get the same value. Thus by
Theorem
4.14
, we may instead
write

\alpha^{\mathrm{trop}}_{p_{1}p_{2}r}=\sum_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{1}=(\tau_{1},{\bf A}_{1}),{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{2}=(\tau_{2},{\bf A}_{2})}k_{\tau_{1}}k_{\tau_{2}}N_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{1}}N_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{2}}t^{A_{1}+A_{2}}

where now the sum is over admissible decorated broken line types

{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{2}
with

\mathfrak{u}\subseteq h_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{i}}(\tau_{i,\mathrm{out}})
,

{\bf u}_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{i}}(L_{i,\mathrm{in}})=p_{i}
, and
u_{\tau_{1}}+u_{\tau_{2}}=r
.
(Note that by definition of
\mathscr{S}_{\mathscr{B}}
, if
x\in h_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{i}}(\tau_{i,\mathrm{out}})

then
\mathfrak{u}\subseteq h_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{i}}(\tau_{i,\mathrm{out}})
.)

For such a pair
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{2}
, we obtain a decorated product type by taking
a vertex
v_{\mathrm{out}}
and gluing
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
to
v_{\mathrm{out}}
via
the legs
L_{1,\mathrm{out}}
,
L_{2,\mathrm{out}}
, and add an additional leg

L_{\mathrm{out}}
adjacent to
v_{\mathrm{out}}
with
{\bf u}(L_{\mathrm{out}})=-r
.
The data
{\boldsymbol{\sigma}},{\bf u},{\bf A}
are then determined by

{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{1}
,
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{2}
, with
{\bf A}(v_{\mathrm{out}})=0
.

We see
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}
is a decorated product type. Indeed, we only need
to check conditions (2) and (3) of Definition
6.2
.
Balancing just needs to be checked at
v_{\mathrm{out}}
, which follows
from
r={\bf u}_{1}(L_{1,\mathrm{out}})+{\bf u}_{2}(L_{2,\mathrm{out}})=u_{\tau_{1}}+u_{\tau_{2}}
.
For realizability and the dimension conditions (3), note that by
the corresponding dimension conditions for broken line types, for
each point
y\in\operatorname{Int}(\mathfrak{u})
, there exists a unique
s_{i}\in\tau_{i}
such that

y\in h_{\tau_{i},s_{i}}(L_{i,\mathrm{out}})
. Thus by gluing together

h_{\tau_{1},s_{1}}
and
h_{\tau_{2},s_{2}}
, we obtain a tropical map realizing

\tau
which takes
v_{\mathrm{out}}
to
y
. Furthermore, this map is necessarily
the unique such tropical map of type
\tau
, giving both realizability
and the dimension statements of (3).

Conversely, by Lemma
6.5
, all product
types
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}
arise in this way. Thus we obtain using Lemma

6.6
that

\alpha^{\mathrm{trop}}_{p_{1}p_{2}r}=\sum_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}=(\tau,{\bf A})}k_{\tau}N_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}}t^{A},

which coincides with
\alpha^{\log}_{p_{1}p_{2}r}
by
Theorem
6.4
.
∎

Appendix A
The gluing formula with toric gluing strata

Let
B=\operatorname{Spec}(Q_{B}\to\Bbbk)
be a logarithmic point,
X
a Zariski log scheme and

X\to B
a logarithmically smooth, integral,
projective morphism. We assume
\Sigma(X)

monodromy-free for simplicity. As before we write

\mathcal{X}
for the Artin fan of
X
. Let
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}=(\tau,\mathbf{A})
with

\tau=(G,{\boldsymbol{\sigma}},\bar{\mathbf{u}})
be a realizable decorated global type of a punctured
map of genus
0
.
11
11

11

The restriction to genus
0
is for convenience,
since this is the case relevant to us and the general statement is slightly
more complicated.
Recall from
[
ACGS2
, Def. 3.8]
the stacks

\mathscr{M}(X,{\boldsymbol{\tau}})
and
\mathfrak{M}(\mathcal{X},{\boldsymbol{\tau}})
of punctured stable maps to
X
and to

\mathcal{X}
marked by
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}
.

For each vertex
v\in V(G)
denote by
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{v}=(\tau_{v},\mathbf{A}_{v})
,

\tau_{v}=(G_{v},{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{v},\bar{\mathbf{u}}_{v})
the decorated global type with
V(G_{v})=\{v\}

and
E(G_{v})=\emptyset
obtained by splitting
G
at all
edges.
12
12

12

Splitting at only a subset of edges works the same way, with a
little more bookkeeping notation necessary. For simplicity of presentation we
only discuss splitting at all edges.
Note that each
\tau_{v}
is realizable as
well. Then
\mathfrak{M}(\mathcal{X},\tau)
is locally pure-dimensional, and hence defines a
virtual fundamental class for
\mathscr{M}(X,{\boldsymbol{\tau}})
by means of the obstruction theory
for
\mathscr{M}(X,{\boldsymbol{\tau}})
over
\mathfrak{M}(\mathcal{X},\tau)
.

According to
[
ACGS2
, Cor. 5.13, Prop. 5.15]
, the splitting
morphism

(A.1)

\delta:\underline{\mathscr{M}}(X,{\boldsymbol{\tau}})\ \longrightarrow\ \textstyle\prod_{v\in V(G)}\underline{\mathscr{M}}(X,{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{v})

is finite and representable.
13
13

13

We suppress here the log structure which
is irrelevant for the formulation of the statement, although it is central for
its proof.
The gluing formula expresses
\delta_{*}[\mathscr{M}(X,{\boldsymbol{\tau}})]^{\mathrm{virt}}
in
terms of the virtual fundamental classes of strata of
\mathscr{M}(X,{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{v})
. A
stratum is given by a
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{v}
-marked decorated global type
{\boldsymbol{\omega}}_{v}
as the
image of the finite map

j_{{\boldsymbol{\omega}}_{v}}:\mathscr{M}(X,{\boldsymbol{\omega}}_{v})\longrightarrow\mathscr{M}(X,{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{v})

defined by changing the marking
[
ACGS2
, Rem. 3.28]
.

We now explain some more notation needed to state the gluing formula. For each
edge
E\in E(G)
we obtain a gluing stratum
\sigma_{E}={\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(E)\in\Sigma(X)

defined by
\tau
. Note that if
v,v^{\prime}\in V(G)
are the vertices adjacent to
E

and
L\in L(G_{v})
,
L^{\prime}\in L(G_{v^{\prime}})
are the legs in
G_{v},G_{v^{\prime}}
obtained by
splitting
E
, then by the definition of
\tau_{v}
,

{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{v}(L)={\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{v^{\prime}}(L^{\prime})=\sigma_{E}.

We write
L=(E,v)
,
L^{\prime}=(E,v^{\prime})
in the following, where the notation indicates
that
v\in V(G_{v})\subseteq V(G)
and
v^{\prime}\in V(G_{v^{\prime}})\subseteq V(G)
are the
vertices adjacent to
E
.

Now the restriction of a
\tau_{v}
-marked basic tropical
punctured map, defined say over
\omega_{v}\in\mathbf{Cones}
, to the leg
(E,v)\in L(G_{v})
defines a map of cones

(A.2)

\omega_{E,v}=\big\{(h,\lambda)\in\omega_{v}\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\,\big|\,\lambda\leq\ell(E,v)(h)\big\}\longrightarrow\sigma,

for some
\sigma\in\Sigma(X)
containing
\sigma_{E}={\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{v}(E,v)
as a face.
Recall also that
\omega_{v}
is the basic cone, or real cone with integral points
the dual of the basic monoid
[
ACGS2
, Def. 2.36]
, associated to
\omega_{v}
.
We conflate notations for types and associated basic cones and hence write

\omega_{v}
also for this type unless there is a danger of confusion. Similarly,

\tau_{v}
denotes both a global type and the basic cone

(Q_{\tau_{v}})_{\mathbb{R}}^{\vee}
associated to the type. Note also that the marking by

\tau_{v}
induces a face embedding

(A.3)

\tau_{v}=(Q_{\tau_{v}})_{\mathbb{R}}^{\vee}\longrightarrow\omega_{v}.

of basic cones.

Letting
(E,v)
in (
A.2
) run over all the gluing
legs, we arrive at the following enlargement of the cone
\omega_{v}
that
records a point on each puncturing leg:

(A.4)

\widetilde{\omega}_{v}=\big\{(h,\lambda_{E,v})\in\omega_{v}\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{L(G_{v})}\,\big|\,\lambda_{E,v}\leq\ell(E,v)(h)\big\}.

For each leg
(E,v)\in L(G_{v})
, the projection forgetting all

\lambda
-components but
\lambda_{E,v}
defines a map of cones

(A.5)

\widetilde{\omega}_{v}\longrightarrow\omega_{E,v}.

Similarly, for a
\tau
-marked tropical punctured map defined over

\omega\in\mathbf{Cones}
, recording a point on each edge
E\in E(G)
leads to

(A.6)

\widetilde{\omega}=\big\{(h,\lambda_{E})\in\omega\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{E(G)}\,\big|\,\lambda_{E}\leq\ell(E)(h)\big\}.

We now add the following assumption:

Assumption A.1
.

Assume all gluing strata
\underline{Z}_{E}
,
E\in E(G)
, are complete toric varieties,
with
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{X}|_{\underline{Z}_{E}}
invariant under the torus action.

Under this assumption there is an embedding of the star of

\sigma_{E}\in\Sigma(X)
as a face-fitting complex of cones in the real vector
space associated to a lattice that we denote
\Lambda_{E}
. Moreover, this
embedding identifies the integral affine structures on each cone, and the
composition with the quotient by the image of the real subspace with lattice

(\sigma_{E})_{\mathbb{Z}}^{\operatorname{gp}}\cap\Lambda_{E}
maps the face-fitting complex of cones to a
complete fan. If
Z_{E}
is strictly embedded as a toric stratum of a toric
variety,
\Lambda_{E}
is the lattice underlying the describing fan. We now view
each of the cones
\sigma\in\Sigma(X)
containing
\sigma_{E}
as embedded in

(\Lambda_{E})_{\mathbb{R}}
.

Let
\omega=(G_{\omega},{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\omega},\mathbf{u}_{\omega})
be a not necessarily
realizable global type of punctured map marked by
\tau
. Splitting
\omega
at
the edges not contracted by the marking leads to a collection
(\omega_{v})_{v\in V(G)}
of types of tropical punctured maps, with
\omega_{v}
marked by
\tau_{v}

and with a leg
(E,v)\in L(G_{\omega_{v}})
for each edge
E\in E(G)
adjacent to

v
. For each
E\in E(G)
with
v,v^{\prime}
the adjacent vertices, the composition of
the embeddings
{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\omega}(E,v),{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\omega}(E,v^{\prime})\to(\Lambda_{E})_{\mathbb{R}}

with the quotient by the diagonal embedding
(\Lambda_{E})_{\mathbb{R}}\to(\Lambda_{E})_{\mathbb{R}}\times(\Lambda_{E})_{\mathbb{R}}
defines a map

\varepsilon_{E}:{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\omega}(E,v)\times{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\omega}(E,v^{\prime})\longrightarrow(\Lambda_{E})_{\mathbb{R}}\times(\Lambda_{E})_{\mathbb{R}}\longrightarrow(\Lambda_{E})_{\mathbb{R}}.

Explicitly, the arrow on the right can be taken as the difference map

(a,b)\mapsto a-b
.

Taking the product over all
E\in E(G)
of the composition of
\varepsilon_{E}

with the maps (
A.5
) and (
A.2
) yields the map

(A.7)

\textstyle\varepsilon_{\omega}:\prod_{v\in V(G)}\widetilde{\omega}_{v}\longrightarrow\prod_{E,v}\omega_{E,v}\longrightarrow\prod_{E}(\Lambda_{E})_{\mathbb{R}}\times(\Lambda_{E})_{\mathbb{R}}\longrightarrow\prod_{E}(\Lambda_{E})_{\mathbb{R}}.

Thus
\varepsilon_{\omega}
measures the failure of a collection of tropical
punctured maps of types
\omega_{v}
, together with points on the legs arising
from splitting, to patch to a tropical punctured map of type
\omega
together
with a choice of point on each of the splitted edges. We refer to an
element of
\prod_{E}(\Lambda_{E})_{\mathbb{R}}
as a
displacement vector
.

Definition A.2
.

1) We call a displacement vector
\nu=(\nu_{E})_{E\in E(G)}\in\prod_{E}(\Lambda_{E})_{\mathbb{R}}

general
for
\tau
if for each global
type
\omega
marked by
\tau
, either
\varepsilon_{\omega}^{\operatorname{gp}}
from (
A.7
) is surjective or
\nu\not\in\operatorname{im}(\varepsilon_{\omega}^{\operatorname{gp}})
.

2) Let
\nu=(\nu_{E})_{E}\in\prod_{E}\Lambda_{E}
be general for
\tau
. Define
the
set
\Delta(\nu)
of transverse types for
\nu
as the set of
isomorphism classes of global types
\omega
marked by
\tau
with

\nu\in\operatorname{im}(\varepsilon_{\omega})
, and such that

(A.8)

\textstyle\sum_{v}\dim\widetilde{\omega}_{v}=\dim\widetilde{\tau}+\sum_{E}\operatorname{rk}\Lambda_{E}.

The set of decorated global types
{\boldsymbol{\omega}}=(\omega,\mathbf{A})
marked by
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}
and
with
\omega\in\Delta(\nu)
is denoted by
\hat{\Delta}(\nu)
. We confuse a
transverse type
\omega
with its splitting
(\omega_{v})_{v\in V(G)}
, and
similarly in the decorated case.

3) For a general displacement vector
\nu
and
{\boldsymbol{\omega}}=(\omega,\mathbf{A})\in\hat{\Delta}(\nu)

define the
splitting multiplicity
by

m({\boldsymbol{\omega}})=m(\omega):=\big[\textstyle\prod_{E}\Lambda_{E}:\operatorname{im}(\varepsilon_{\omega}^{\operatorname{gp}})_{\mathbb{Z}}\big].

Remark A.3
.

The set
\Delta(\nu)
has an interpretation in terms of types of “broken”
tropical punctured maps, in the sense that the matching condition along an edge

E
is replaced by matching translated by
\nu_{E}
. Specifically, let
\omega
be
a global type marked by
\tau
and
(\omega_{v})
the collection of global types
marked by
\tau_{v}
obtained by splitting. Then
\omega\in\Delta(\nu)
iff

\omega
satisfies (
A.8
) and
there
exist
h_{v}\in\omega_{v}
and
\lambda_{v},\lambda_{v^{\prime}}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
with

(A.9)

V(h_{v})+\lambda_{v}\cdot u_{E}=V^{\prime}(h_{v^{\prime}})-\lambda_{v^{\prime}}\cdot u_{E}+\nu_{E},

as an equation in
(\Lambda_{E})_{\mathbb{R}}
. For the signs we assume

E
oriented from
v
to
v^{\prime}
. Here
V:\omega_{v}\to(\Lambda_{E})_{\mathbb{R}}
is the
restriction of the map in (
A.2
) to the face

\lambda=0
, that is, the image of the vertex
v
under the tropical punctured
map
h_{v}
, and analogously for
V^{\prime}
and
v^{\prime}
.

From this description it is also obvious that
\Delta(\nu)
does not change by
rescaling
\nu
by a positive constant. Moreover, if
\widetilde{\tau}
is
defined analogously to
\widetilde{\omega}
, with
\lambda_{E}
recording the
position of a point on the edge
E
, then
\Delta(\nu)
is also unchanged by
adding to
\nu
an element of the image of the map
\widetilde{\tau}\to\prod_{E}\Lambda_{E}
evaluating at all points on the splitting edges.

The dimension formula (
A.8
) is equivalent to
requiring
\omega=(\omega_{v})_{v}
to be a minimal type of punctured map with

\varepsilon_{\omega}^{\operatorname{gp}}
surjective.

We are now in position to state the gluing formula.

Theorem A.4
.

[
Wu
]

Let
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}=(\tau,\mathbf{A})
,
\tau=(G,{\boldsymbol{\sigma}},\bar{\mathbf{u}})
be a realizable decorated
global type of punctured maps of genus
0
to
X
, and
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{v}
the associated
decorated global type defined at
v\in V(G)
obtained from splitting all edges.
Assume that all gluing strata are toric in the sense of Assumption
A.1
. Let
\nu=(\nu_{E})_{E\in E(G)}
be a general displacement vector for

\tau
, and
\hat{\Delta}(\nu)
the set of decorated transverse types for
\nu

(Definition
A.2
). Denote by
\delta
the splitting morphism
from (
A.1
).

Then the following equality in the Chow group of
\prod_{v\in V(G)}\underline{\mathscr{M}}(X,{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{v})
holds:

\delta_{*}[\mathscr{M}(X,{\boldsymbol{\tau}})]^{\mathrm{virt}}=\sum_{{\boldsymbol{\omega}}=({\boldsymbol{\omega}}_{v})_{v}\in{\hat{\Delta}}(\nu)}\frac{m({\boldsymbol{\omega}})}{|\operatorname{Aut}({\boldsymbol{\omega}}/{\boldsymbol{\tau}})|}\cdot(j_{{\boldsymbol{\omega}}_{v}})_{*}\big[\textstyle\prod_{v}\mathscr{M}(X,{\boldsymbol{\omega}}_{v})\big]^{\mathrm{virt}}.
